--- Page 9 ---
<meta>This page serves as the formal dedication and introductory poem to the collection, establishing the tone of the work as a bridge between the medieval Persian poet Hafiz and the modern English reader.</meta>

### TO
## HAFIZ OF SHIRAZ

->Fate is a hand that possesses five fingers; when it desires to fulfill its purpose upon a man,<-
->It places two upon his eyes, another two upon his ears, and one upon his lips, saying: "Be silent!"<-
<note>original: "قضا دستيست پنج انگشت دارد..." This Persian couplet emphasizes the inescapable and silencing nature of destiny.</note>

*Thus said the Poet: "When Death comes to you,*
*All ye whose life-sand through the hour-glass slips,*
*He lays two fingers on your ears, and two*
*Upon your eyes he lays, one on your lips,*
*Whispering: Silence!" Although deaf thine ear,*
*Thine eye, my Hafiz <note>Hafiz (c. 1315–1390) was a Persian lyric poet whose collected works are regarded as a pinnacle of Persian literature.</note>, suffer Time's eclipse,*
*The songs thou sangest still all men may hear.*

*Songs of dead laughter, songs of love once hot,*
*Songs of a cup once flushed rose-red with wine,*
*Songs of a rose whose beauty is forgot,*
*A nightingale <note>In Persian poetry, the nightingale (bulbul) represents the lover longing for the rose (the beloved).</note> that piped hushed lays divine:*
*And still a graver music runs beneath*
*The tender love notes of those songs of thine,*
*Oh, Seeker of the keys of Life and Death!*

<summary>A poetic dedication to the 14th-century Persian poet Hafiz, reflecting on the themes of mortality, silence, and the enduring power of his verse despite the passage of time.</summary>
<keywords>Hafiz of Shiraz, Persian poetry, Fate, mortality, symbolism of the nightingale, Sufi philosophy, Victorian translation</keywords>

--- Page 10 ---
<meta>This page serves as the formal dedication of the work, following the title page. The translator maintains the poetic meter and rhyme scheme of the original English verse, which pays homage to the Persian poet Hafiz.</meta>

## DEDICATION

*While you were singing, the soft summer wind*
*That over Mosalla’s garden <note>A famous open-air place of prayer and recreation in Shiraz, often celebrated by the poet Hafiz.</note> blew, the stream*
*Of Ruknabad <note>A small stream near Shiraz, immortalized in Persian poetry for its clarity and beauty.</note> flowing where roses twined,*
*Carried your voice farther than you could dream.*
*To Isfahan and Baghdad’s Tartar horde,*
*Over waste and sea to Yezd and distant India;*
*Yes, even to the sunset they bore your word.*

*Behold we laugh, we warm ourselves at Love’s fire,*
*We thirst and scarce dare tell what wine we crave,*
*We lift our voices in Grief’s dark-robed choir;*
*Sing the wisdom that joy and sorrow gave!*
*If my poor rhymes held any of the heart’s lore,*
*Fresh wreaths were theirs to lay upon your grave—*
*Master and Poet, all was yours before!*

<summary>A poetic dedication addressed to the Persian poet Hafiz, acknowledging his enduring influence across the East and West and crediting him as the source of the author's inspiration.</summary>
<keywords>dedication, Hafiz, Shiraz, Persian poetry, Ruknabad, Mosalla, literary influence</keywords>

--- Page 11 ---
<meta>Following the poetic dedication, this page begins the formal historical introduction. It provides biographical context for the poet Hafiz, placing him in the turbulent social and political landscape of 14th-century Shiraz.</meta>

# INTRODUCTION

SHAMS AL-DIN MUHAMMAD <note>original: "Shemsuddin Mahommad"; the name translates to "Sun of the Faith" (Shams al-Din) and "The Praiseworthy" (Muhammad).</note>, better known by his poetical pen-name of **Hafiz** <note>A title given to one who has memorized the entire Qur'an.</note>, was born in **Shiraz** in the early part of the fourteenth century. <note>For the history of the times of Hafiz, see Defrémery in the *Journal Asiatique* (1844 and 1845), Malcolm's *History of Persia*, Price's *Mohammedan History*, and Markham's *History of Persia*. For the life of the poet, see von Hammer; Defrémery in the *Journal Asiatique* (1858); Sir Gore Ouseley and Daulat Shah, whose work is mainly a collection of anecdotes—I have been told that Lutfallah's is little better.</note> His names, when interpreted, signify the **Sun of the Faith**, the **Praiseworthy**, and **One who can recite the Qur'an**; he is further known to his countrymen under the titles of the <term>Tongue of the Hidden <note>original: "Lisan al-Ghaib"; a title suggesting that his poetry was divinely inspired or spoke from the unseen world.</note></term> and the <term>Interpreter of Secrets <note>original: "Tarjuman al-Asrar"; referring to his ability to voice mystical and spiritual truths.</note></term>. The better part of his life was spent in **Shiraz**, and he died in that city towards the close of the century. The exact date of either his birth or of his death is unknown. He lived during turbulent times. His delicate love-songs were chanted to the harsh accompaniment of the clash of arms, and his dreams must have been interrupted often enough by the pangs of famine in a besieged town, the violent arrival of conquerors, and the flight of the defeated.

<summary>This page introduces the 14th-century Persian poet Hafiz, explaining the spiritual significance of his various titles and describing the war-torn environment of Shiraz in which he wrote.</summary>
<keywords>Hafiz, Shams al-Din Muhammad, Shiraz, 14th century, Persian literature, biography, Lisan al-Ghaib, Tarjuman al-Asrar</keywords>

--- Page 12 ---
<meta>Following the biographical notes on Hafiz's early life and titles, this page provides a historical overview of the political instability in 14th-century Persia. It outlines the transition from the Mongol conquest of the Abbasid Caliphate to the rise of regional governorships in Fars.</meta>

# INTRODUCTION

->8<-

The history of Persia in the fourteenth century is exceedingly confused. Beyond a succession of wars and turmoils, there is little to be learnt concerning the political conditions under which **Hafiz** <note>The celebrated Persian lyric poet.</note> lived. Fifty years before the birth of the poet, **Hulagu** <note>A Mongol ruler who founded the Ilkhanate in Persia.</note>, a grandson of the great Tartar invader **Chinghis Khan** <note>original: "Chinghis Khan"; Genghis Khan.</note>, had conquered **Baghdad**, putting to death the last of the **Abbaside Khalifs** <note>original: "Abbaside Khalifs"; the Caliphs of the Abbasid dynasty who were the symbolic heads of the Islamic world.</note> and extinguishing the direct line of the race that had ruled over Persia since 750. For the next 200 years there is indeed a branch of the family of Abbas living in **Cairo**, members of which were set up as **Khalifs** <note>Caliphs.</note> by the **Mamluk Sultans of Egypt** <note>A powerful dynasty of professional soldier-slaves who ruled Egypt and Syria.</note>; but they were destitute of any real authority, and their position was that of dependants in the Mamluk court.

The sons and grandsons of Hulagu succeeded him as lords of **Persia** and **Mesopotamia** <note>The historical region in modern-day Iraq between the Tigris and Euphrates rivers.</note>, paying a nominal allegiance to the **Great Khan** of the Mongols in **Cambalec** <note>original: "Cambalec"; modern-day Beijing, the capital of the Mongol-led Yuan Dynasty.</note> or **Pekin**, but for all practical purposes independent, and the different provinces of their empire were administered by governors in their name. About the time of the birth of Hafiz, that is to say in the beginning of the fourteenth century, a certain **Mahmud Shah Inju** <note>The founder of the Injuid dynasty which ruled over the Fars province.</note> was governing the province of **Fars**, of which **Shiraz** is the capital, in the name of **Abu Said**, the last of the direct descendants of Hulagu. On the death of Mahmud Shah, Abu Said appointed **Sheikh Hussein ibn Juban** to the

It introduces the regional governors of Shiraz, setting the stage for the specific political environment of the poet Hafiz.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the historical narrative of 14th-century Persia, specifically focusing on the power struggle in the province of Fars following the decline of the Mongol Ilkhanate. # INTRODUCTION

->9<-

governorship of **Fars** <note>A province in southwestern Iran, legendary as the heart of Persian culture and the home of the city of Shiraz.</note>, a lucrative and much-coveted post. **Sheikh Hussein** <note>A governor representing the waning Mongol authority.</note> took the precaution of ordering the three sons of **Mahmud Shah** <note>Sharaf al-Din Mahmud Shah, the founder of the Inju dynasty which had gained local power.</note> to be seized and imprisoned; but while they were passing through the streets of **Shiraz** <note>The capital of Fars and the beloved city of the poet Hafiz.</note> in the hands of their captors, their mother, who accompanied them, lifted her veil and made a touching appeal to the people, calling upon them to remember the benefits they had received from their late ruler, the father of the three boys. Her words took instant effect; the inhabitants rose, released her and her sons, and drove Sheikh Hussein into exile. He, however, returned with an army supplied by **Abu Said** <note>The last effective ruler of the Mongol Ilkhanate.</note>, and induced Shiraz to submit again to his rule. In 1335, a year or two after these events, Abu Said died, and the power of the **house of Hulagu** <note>The Ilkhanid dynasty, founded by the grandson of Genghis Khan.</note> crumbled away. There followed a long period of anarchy, which was brought to an end when **Oweis** <note>Shaikh Uways I of the Jalayirid dynasty.</note>, another descendant of Hulagu, seized the throne. He and his son **Ahmed** <note>Sultan Ahmad Jalayir, who famously resisted the later Mongol invasions.</note> reigned in **Baghdad** until Ahmed was driven out by the invading army of **Timur** <note>Also known as Tamerlane, the Central Asian conqueror who sought to restore the empire of Genghis Khan.</note>. But during the years of anarchy the authority of the Sultan of Baghdad had been considerably curtailed. On Abu Said's death, **Abu Ishac** <note>Amir Abu Ishaq, one of the most beloved rulers of Shiraz and a patron of the arts.</note>, one of the three sons of Mahmud Shah Inju who had so narrowly escaped from the hands of Sheikh Hussein, took possession of Shiraz and **Isfahan** <note>A major city in central Iran, often a rival or partner to Shiraz in regional politics.</note>, finally ousting his old enemy, while **Mahommad ibn Muzaffar** <note>Mubariz al-Din Muhammad, the founder of the Muzaffarid dynasty, known for his religious austerity and military prowess.</note>,

<summary>This page details the political turmoil in 14th-century Fars, including a dramatic popular uprising in Shiraz led by the widow of Mahmud Shah and the eventual collapse of Mongol Ilkhanid authority.</summary>
<keywords>Fars, Shiraz, Abu Said, Inju Dynasty, Abu Ishac, Timur, Isfahan, Ilkhanate, Mahommad ibn Muzaffar</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the historical account of 14th-century Persia following the death of the Mongol ruler Abu Said. It details the rise of the Muzaffarid dynasty in Yazd and the decline of Abu Ishaq, the Injuid ruler of Shiraz, amidst shifting allegiances and civil unrest.</meta>

# INTRODUCTION

->10<-

who had earned a name for valour <note>Valour: great courage in the face of danger, especially in battle.</note> in the service of **Abu Said** <note>The last effective ruler of the Mongol Ilkhanate (d. 1335).</note>, made himself master of **Yezd** <note>Yazd, a major city in central Iran known for its desert architecture.</note>.

From this time onward the governors of the Persian provinces seem to have given a nominal <note>In name only; symbolic rather than actual.</note> allegiance now to the **Sultan of Baghdad**, now to the more distant **Khalif** <note>The Caliph, the supreme religious and political leader of the Muslim world.</note>. The position of **Shiraz** <note>The capital of the Fars province, famous for its gardens and poets.</note> between **Baghdad** and **Cairo** must have resembled that of **Venice** between **Rome** and **Constantinople**, and, like Venice, she was obedient to neither lord.

**Abu Ishac** <note>Jamal al-Din Abu Ishaq, the last Injuid ruler of Fars, known for his patronage of the poet Hafiz.</note> had not steered his bark <note>A poetic metaphor for a small ship or vessel, representing his leadership of the state.</note> into quiet waters. In 1340 **Shiraz** was besieged and taken by a rival **Atabeg** <note>term: Atabeg; a Turkish title for a military officer who often served as a governor or tutor to a prince.</note>, and the son of **Mahmud Shah** <note>Abu Ishac himself.</note> was obliged to content himself with **Isfahan**. But in the following year he returned, captured Shiraz by a stratagem <note>A clever trick or scheme to deceive an enemy.</note>, and again established himself as ruler over all **Fars**. The remaining years of his reign are chiefly occupied with military expeditions against **Yezd**, where **Mahommad ibn Muzaffar** and his sons were building up a formidable power. In 1352, determined to put an end to these attacks, **Mahommad** marched into **Fars** and laid siege to **Shiraz**.

**Abu Ishac**, whose life was one of perpetual dissipation <note>Dissipation: a life of over-indulgence in physical pleasures like drinking and partying, often at the expense of duty.</note>, redoubled his orgies in the face of danger. Uncertain of the fidelity <note>Loyalty or faithfulness.</note> of the people of **Shiraz**, he put to death all the inhabitants of two quarters of the town, and contemplated insuring himself of a third quarter in a similar manner. But these measures did not lead to the desired results.

<meta>This page continues the historical account of Abu Ishaq’s downfall, the Injuid ruler of Shiraz. It details his final defeat at the hands of the Muzaffarids and provides a contemporary character assessment from the famous traveler Ibn Battuta.</meta>

# INTRODUCTION

<page-num>II</page-num>

The chief of the threatened quarter got wind of the King’s design, and delivered up the keys of his gate to **Shah Shudja** <note>The son of the Muzaffarid ruler Muhammad, and later a famous patron of the poet Hafiz.</note>, son of **Mahommad ibn Muzaffar** <note>Mubariz al-Din Muhammad, the founder of the Muzaffarid dynasty.</note>, and **Abu Ishac** was obliged to seek refuge a second time in **Isfahan**. Four years later, in 1357, he was given up to **Mahommad**, who sent him to **Shiraz** and, with a fine sense of dramatic fitness, had him beheaded in an open space before the ruins of **Persepolis** <note>The ancient ceremonial capital of the Persian Empire; executing a modern ruler there served as a powerful symbol of the end of his dynasty.</note>.

The Arab traveller **Ibn Batuta** <note>One of the most famous world travelers of the Middle Ages, he journeyed across Africa, Asia, and the Middle East.</note>, who visited **Shiraz** between the years 1340 and 1350, has left a description of its ruler: “**Abu Ishac**,” says he, “is one of the best **Sultans** <note>A title for a Muslim sovereign or ruler.</note> that can be found” (it must be confessed that the average of **Sultans** was not very high in **Ibn Batuta’s** time); “he is fair of face, imposing of presence, and his conduct is no less to be admired. His mind is generous, his character remarkable, and he is modest although his power is great and his territories extensive. His army exceeds the number of 30,000 men, **Turks** and **Persians**. The most faithful of his subjects are the inhabitants of **Isfahan**; but he fears the **Shirazis**, who are a brave people, not to be controlled by kings, and he will not trust them with arms.”¹ This view of his relations with the two towns tallies with **Abu Ishac’s** subsequent history, and points to a considerable power of observation on the part of **Ibn Batuta**.

¹ *The Travels of Ibn Batuta*, edited by **Defrémery** and **Sanguinetti** <note>The French scholars who produced the standard 19th-century translation and edition of Ibn Battuta's works.</note>.

<summary>This page concludes the biography of the ruler Abu Ishaq with his execution at Persepolis and provides a glowing historical character sketch by the traveler Ibn Battuta.</summary>
<keywords>Abu Ishac, Shah Shudja, Ibn Batuta, Shiraz, Isfahan, Persepolis, Muzaffarid Dynasty, 14th century Persia</keywords>

--- Page 16 ---
<meta>This page continues the account of Abu Ishaq, the Injuid ruler of Shiraz, focusing on his reputation among his subjects, his failed ambitions as a patron of the arts, and his eventual execution as the Muzaffarid dynasty rose to power.</meta>

# INTRODUCTION

But **Ibn Batuta** <note>The traveler mentioned previously; his accounts provide a rare contemporary glimpse into the social life of 14th-century Persia.</note> relates a tale which would seem to show that **Abu Ishac** was not unpopular even in **Shiraz**: on a certain occasion he wished to build a great gate in that city, and hearing of his desire the inhabitants vied with each other in their eagerness to satisfy it; men of all ranks turned out to do the work, putting on their best clothes and digging the foundations with spades of silver. **Abu Ishac** shared the passion of the age for letters, and was anxious to be accounted a rival to the **King of Delhi** <note>Likely Muhammad bin Tughluq, the Sultan of Delhi (r. 1325–1351), renowned for his immense wealth and somewhat erratic but lavish patronage of scholars.</note> in his generosity to men of learning; "but," sighs **Ibn Batuta**, "how far is the earth removed from the **Pleiades**!" <note>An ancient metaphor used to describe an unreachable distance or a vast difference in quality, comparing the lowly earth to a prominent star cluster in the sky.</note> The Persian historian who describes **Abu Ishac's** execution, quotes a quatrain which the <term>Atabeg<note>Originally a Turkish title meaning "father-lord," referring to a high-ranking guardian or governor; here it refers to Abu Ishaq’s role as a regional ruler.</note></term> is supposed to have written while he was in prison:

> "Lay down thine arms when Fortune is thy foe,
> 'Gainst Heaven's wheel, Wrestler, try not a throw,
> Drink steadfastly the cup whose name is Death,
> Empty the dregs upon the earth, and go."

So perished the first patron of **Hafiz** <note>The legendary Persian poet whose work was shaped by the volatile politics of Shiraz.</note>. 

From 1353 to 1393, when **Timur** <note>Also known as Tamerlane, the Turco-Mongol conqueror who founded the Timurid Empire.</note> conquered **Shiraz** for the second and last time, the greater part of Persia was ruled by members of the house of **Muzaffar**. Scarcely a year passed undisturbed by civil war, scarcely a year in which one of the sons or grandsons of **Mahommad** did not suffer imprisonment or worse ills at the hands of his brothers.

<summary>This page describes the popular support for Abu Ishaq in Shiraz and his failed literary ambitions, concluding with his execution and the rise of the fractured Muzaffarid dynasty.</summary>
<keywords>Abu Ishaq, Shiraz, Ibn Battuta, Delhi Sultanate, Muzaffarid dynasty, Timur, Persian poetry, Atabeg</keywords>

--- Page 17 ---
<meta>This page continues the narrative from the downfall of Abu Ishaq, shifting focus to his conqueror, Mubariz al-Din Muhammad (the "Mahommad" of this text), and the violent transition of power within the Muzaffarid dynasty to his son, Shah Shuja.</meta>

# INTRODUCTION

**Mahommad** <note>Mubariz al-Din Muhammad (r. 1314–1358), the stern founder of the Muzaffarid dynasty who conquered Shiraz.</note> himself was the first to fall. **Shah Shudja** seized his father while he was reading the **Koran** <note>The Quran, the central religious text of Islam.</note> aloud with a poet of his court, and caused him to be blinded. A few years later the grim life beat itself out against the prison walls of **Ka'lah-i-Safid** <note>Literally "The White Castle," a historic and nearly impregnable mountain fortress in the Fars province used as a state prison.</note>. 

> "Without just cause," sings **Hafiz** <note>The celebrated Persian poet Hafez, who lived through these tumultuous regime changes in Shiraz.</note>, "the victor of victors suffered imprisonment; guiltless, the mightiest head was laid low. He had overcome **Shiraz** and **Tabriz** and **Irak** <note>Likely referring to Iraq-i 'Ajam, or "Persian Iraq," a region in central and western Iran.</note>; at the last his own hour came. He who, in the eyes of the world, was the light he had kindled <note>The poet uses a metaphor for the king’s son, Shah Shudja.</note>, through those eyes which had gazed victorious upon the world, thrust the hot iron."

A stern and pitiless man was this **Mahommad**, brave in battle, wise in council, ardent in religion, but hard and cruel beyond measure, a perfidious friend and a relentless enemy. The Persian historian, **Lutfallah**, relates that on several occasions he had seen criminals brought before **Mahommad** while the <term>Amir<note>A title for a high-ranking commander or ruler.</note></term> was engaged in reading the **Koran**. Laying the book aside, he would draw his sword and kill the offenders as they stood, and then return unmoved to his devotions. **Shah Shudja** once asked his father whether he had killed 1,000 men with his own hand. "No," replied **Mahommad**, "but I think that the number of them that I have slain must reach 800."

After his death, **Shah Shudja** reigned in **Shiraz**, and his brother **Shah Yahya** in **Yezd**. **Shah Shudja**

<summary>This page describes the violent end of the Muzaffarid ruler Mubariz al-Din Muhammad, who was blinded and imprisoned by his son Shah Shuja, and reflects on Muhammad's reputation for extreme piety mixed with brutal cruelty.</summary>
<keywords>Mubariz al-Din Muhammad, Shah Shuja, Hafiz, Muzaffarid dynasty, White Castle, Shiraz, Persian history, Quran</keywords>

--- Page 18 ---
<meta>This page continues the narrative of the Muzaffarid dynasty, shifting from the violent end of Mubariz al-Din Muhammad's reign to the character of his son, Shah Shuja, and the existential threat posed by the arrival of Tamerlane.</meta>

# INTRODUCTION

...was a man of similar energy to his father, but it was an energy channeled in very different directions. The stern religious devotion of the elder man became, in the younger, a spirit of frenzied excess. Whenever he was not leading military campaigns against his own brothers and nephews, he was indulging in the wildest celebrations in **Shiraz** <note>The capital of the Fars province and a historic center of Persian culture and poetry.</note>. He was nearly as cruel as **Mahommad** <note>Mubariz al-Din Muhammad (r. 1314–1358).</note>. In a drunken fit, he once ordered one of his own sons to be blinded; although he repented at the urging of his <term>vizir<note>A high-ranking political advisor or minister.</note></term> and sent a second messenger in great haste to stop the punishment, it was already too late to save the boy’s sight. Before **Shah Shudja’s** death, the death knell of the house of **Muzaffar** <note>The Muzaffarid dynasty (1314–1393), which ruled central and southern Iran.</note> had already begun to toll—**Tamberlain** <note>Timur the Lame (r. 1370–1405), the Turco-Mongol conqueror who founded the Timurid Empire.</note> and his **Tartar** hordes had advanced into Northern **Persia**. In 1382, **Shah Shudja** sent a peace-seeking embassy to him with gifts: jewels and silks, horses, a scarlet <term>daïs<note>A raised platform used for a throne or seat of honor.</note></term>, a royal standard, and a Chinese umbrella <note>In the 14th century, a Chinese-style umbrella was a prestigious symbol of sovereignty and protection.</note>. In return, **Timur** sent the King a robe of honor and a belt studded with jewels.

Exhausted before his time by a life of riotous living, **Shah Shudja** did his best to ensure the safety of his family before he died. He sent letters to both **Timur** and **Sultan Ahmed of Baghdad** <note>Ahmad Jalayir, the Jalayirid ruler of Iraq and parts of western Persia.</note>, asking them to protect his son **Zein-el-Abeddin** <note>Zayn al-Abidin, who briefly succeeded his father as ruler of Shiraz.</note>, his brothers, and his nephews. The curtain is pulled back for a moment from the King's deathbed, and an anecdote—the kind so favored by Eastern historians—reveals to us the...

...fearless and terrible face. <note>This likely refers to the imposing presence of the King even as he faced death.</note> Hearing that his brother **Ahmed** <note>Shah Ahmad (d. 1393), who held the governorship of Kerman.</note> was preparing to dispute the succession with **Zein-el-Abeddin** <note>Zayn al-Abidin (r. 1384–1387), the son and chosen successor of Shah Shuja.</note>, he sent for him in order to persuade him to withdraw his claims. But when **Ahmed** entered the room where **Shah Shudja** lay sick to death, both brothers burst into tears, and **Ahmed** was so much overcome by emotion that he was obliged to withdraw. Thereupon **Shah Shudja** sent him a letter by the hand of a faithful servant. "The world," he said, "is like unto the shadow of a cloud and a dream of the night; for the one has no resting-place, and when the dreamer awakens there remains to him but a vain memory of the other. I foresee much disturbance in **Shiraz**; **Kerman** <note>A major city in southeastern Iran, serving as a Muzaffarid power base.</note> is the home of our fathers. I have no complaint to lay at your door; but now that I am about to fare upon a long journey <note>A metaphorical reference to his impending death.</note>, if you were to become a sower of discord, not I alone would reproach you, but God also; and our enemies would rejoice. Go therefore to **Kerman** and renounce this unhappy city." And **Ahmed** went.

**Shah Shudja** died in the <term>odour of sanctity<note>A traditional expression describing the death of a person deemed holy or exceptionally pious.</note></term>. Ten holy men were with him continually, reading the **Koran** <note>The Quran; the central religious text of Islam.</note> aloud from end to end each day. He left behind him a name renowned for courage and for liberality. He was a poet, after the fashion of kings, and from boyhood he could repeat the **Koran** by heart <note>The text indicates he was a *Hafiz*, a title given to one who has memorized the entire Quran.</note>.

# INTRODUCTION

The son, whose future he had spent his last hours in assuring, was not to remain for long upon the throne bequeathed to him by his father. During his short reign, **Zein-el-Abeddin** <note>Zayn al-Abidin (r. 1384–1387), the Muzaffarid prince who succeeded his father, Shah Shuja.</note> was engaged in defending himself from the attacks of his cousin **Mansur** <note>Shah Mansur, often considered the most courageous of the Muzaffarid rulers.</note>, but in 1388 he was obliged to flee before an enemy more terrible than any he had yet known. **Timur** <note>Tamerlane (1336–1405), the Turco-Mongol conqueror who founded the Timurid Empire.</note>, who for several years had been hovering upon the borders of **Fars** <note>A province in southwestern Iran, the heartland of the dynasty.</note>, overran Southern Persia and took **Shiraz**. **Zein-el-Abeddin** sought refuge with **Mansur**, who repaid his confidence by imprisoning and blinding him. 

It must have been in the year 1388 that the celebrated interview between **Hafiz** <note>Khwaja Shams-ud-Din Muhammad Hafez-e Shirazi (c. 1315–1390), the world-renowned Persian lyric poet.</note> and **Timur** took place (see note to Poem V.), and not at the time of the second conquest of **Shiraz** in 1393. The confusion between the two dates has led several writers to doubt the truth of the story, since it is almost certain that the poet had died before 1393. <note>The legend claims Timur confronted Hafiz about a famous couplet where the poet offered Timur's grand cities, Samarkand and Bukhara, for the mole on a beloved's cheek.</note> **Timur** bestowed **Shiraz** upon **Shah Yahya** <note>A brother of Shah Shuja and rival to Shah Mansur.</note>, uncle to **Mansur**, and some time governor of **Yezd** <note>Yazd, a major desert city in central Iran.</note>; but no sooner was the **Tartar army** <note>Refers to Timur’s Mongol-Turkic forces.</note> called away by disturbances in the northern parts of the empire than **Mansur** overthrew his uncle and possessed himself of **Shiraz**. 

**Hafiz** did not live to see the end of the drama, but the end was not far off. In 1393 **Timur** advanced with 30,000 picked men against **Mansur**. The <term>Muzaffaride<note>The Muzaffarid dynasty, which ruled central and southern Iran during the 14th century following the collapse of the Ilkhanate.</note></term>, with only 3000 or 4000 men, twice charged into the heart of the

Tartar <note>A term often used historically to describe the Turco-Mongol forces led by Timur.</note> force, and at one moment **Timur’s** <note>Tamerlane (1336–1405), the founder of the Timurid Empire.</note> own life was in danger. **Mansur** <note>Shah Mansur, the last Muzaffarid ruler of Shiraz, known for his immense bravery.</note>, who was himself fighting in the thickest of the battle, sent a message back to the wings of his army, ordering them to support his desperate charge; but they did not obey his command. He fell fighting beneath the sword of **Shah Rukh Mirza** <note>The youngest son of Timur, who would eventually succeed him and become a great patron of Persian culture.</note>, **Timur’s** son, leaving the conqueror to "march in triumph through **Persepolis**." <note>A reference to Christopher Marlowe’s play *Tamburlaine the Great*. Persepolis was the ancient ceremonial capital of the Persian Empire, located near Shiraz.</note> Courage was a quality in which the descendants of **Mahommad ibn Muzaffar** <note>The founder of the Muzaffarid dynasty.</note> were not deficient, but among a race of soldiers **Mansur** seems to have been distinguished for his reckless bearing. He, too, like the other members of his family, was a patron of learning, and it is related that he used to distribute 200 <term>tomans</term> <note>A historical Persian currency. In this context, it represents a very generous daily sum for charitable support.</note> daily among the poor scholars of **Shiraz**. Both on account of their popularity and of their bravery, **Timur** saw that there would be no peace for him in **Shiraz** while one member of the house of **Muzaffar** remained alive; **Mansur’s** survivors were put to the sword.

Through all these changes of fortune, **Hafiz** <note>The celebrated Persian poet Shams-ud-Din Muhammad Hafiz (c. 1315–1390).</note> appears to have played the prudent, if rather unromantic part of the **Vicar of Bray**. <note>A reference to a famous English satirical song about a clergyman who changed his political and religious allegiances repeatedly to remain in his position; the author suggests Hafiz stayed in favor with whoever controlled the city.</note> The slender thread of his personal history is made up for the most part of more or less mythical anecdote. He was the son, according to one tradition, of a baker of **Shiraz**, in which city he was probably educated. # INTRODUCTION

->19<-

Perhaps he learned a wholesome philosophy which enabled him to see through the narrow-minded <term>asceticism</term> <note>A lifestyle characterized by abstinence from sensual pleasures, often for spiritual goals.</note> of other religious teachers, whether <term>Sufi</term> <note>A practitioner of the mystical dimension of Islam.</note> or orthodox, and he was not unmindful of the debt he owed him. “My Grey-Beard,” <note>Original: "Pir-e-ma," meaning "our Elder" or spiritual guide. In Persian poetry, the "Elder of the Tavern" is a symbol of spiritual wisdom that transcends formal religious rules.</note> he sings, “who scatters the dregs of the wine, has neither gold nor power, but God has made him both munificent and merciful.” And indeed if he succeeded in unchaining the spirit of his disciple from useless prejudice, it may be admitted that the <term>Sheikh</term> <note>An honorific title for a spiritual master or elder.</note> went far towards providing him with a good equipment for life. Although he never submitted to any strict monastic rule, **Hafiz** assumed the <term>dervish</term> <note>A member of a Sufi religious order; the term often implies a person who has chosen a life of poverty and austerity.</note> habit <note>The "kherqa," a traditional woolen cloak worn by Sufis.</note> of which he speaks so contemptuously. We must suppose that he took the precaution, which he himself recommends, of washing it clean in the wine that **Sheikh Mahmud** provided for him; in other words, that he tempered his orthodoxy with the freer doctrines he had derived from his teacher. He also became a <term>sheikh</term>.

How he first revealed his inimitable gift of song is not known. There is a tradition that upon a certain day one of his uncles was engaged in composing a poem upon <term>Sufiism</term>, and being but a mediocre <term>poetaster</term> <note>An inferior poet with little talent.</note>, could get no further than the first line. **Hafiz** took up the sheet in his uncle's absence and completed the verse. Men say that the curse still hangs over the <term>Divan</term> <note>A collected volume of a poet's work; in this context, the complete works of Hafiz.</note>, therefore let no one whose reason is not strongly seated venture to study the poet. There is a long poem addressed to **Abu Ishac**, in which he is called the King under whose feet the garden of his kingdom bursts into flower. "Oh great and holy!" cries the poet, "every man who is a servant of thine is uplifted so high that the stars of **Gemini** <note>The constellation of the Twins; in Persian poetic tradition, reaching the height of Gemini represents achieving the pinnacle of status or spiritual elevation.</note> are but as his girdle." **Hafiz** must have been in **Shiraz** when **Abu Ishac** was brought thither, a prisoner, from **Isfahan**; he may even have witnessed his execution outside **Persepolis** <note>The ancient ceremonial capital of the Persian Empire, located near Shiraz.</note>. "Fate overtook him," he sighs, "all too speedily—alas for the violence and oppression in this world of pitfalls! alas for the grace and the mercy that dwelt among us! Hast thou not heard, oh **Hafiz**, the laugh of the strutting partridge? Little considered be the clutching talons of the falcon of death." <note>This imagery contrasts the joyful, perhaps arrogant, life of the ruler (the partridge) with the sudden, inevitable arrival of mortality (the falcon).</note>

From the protection of **Abu Ishac**, **Hafiz** passed into that of **Shah Shudja** <note>The son of Mubariz al-Din, who overthrew the Inju dynasty. He had a long, though often difficult, relationship with Hafiz.</note>, but the relations between the two men seem to have been somewhat strained.

# INTRODUCTION

->21<-

**Shah Shudja** <note>Shah Shuja (reigned 1358–1384) was a ruler of the Muzaffarid dynasty. Unlike his austere father, he was a man of culture and a poet, though he often felt overshadowed by Hafiz’s genius.</note> may have distrusted the loyalty of one to whom **Abu Ishac** had been so good a patron; moreover, he nursed a professional jealousy of **Hafiz**, being himself a writer of occasional verse. The historian **Khondamir** <note>Ghiyas ad-Din Khwandamir (c. 1475–1534) was a famous Persian historian whose works provide much of the biographical detail known about the Timurid and Muzaffarid periods.</note> tells of an interview which cannot have increased the goodwill of either interlocutor towards the other. **Shah Shudja** reproached **Hafiz** with the discursiveness of his songs. “In one and the same,” he said, “you write of wine, of <term>Sufiism</term> <note>A mystical tradition within Islam. In Persian poetry, Sufi themes often use the metaphors of romantic love and wine-drinking to represent the soul's ecstatic search for the Divine.</note>, and of the object of your affections. Now this is contrary to the practice of the eloquent.” 

“That which your Majesty has deigned to speak,” replied **Hafiz** (laying his tongue in his cheek, though **Khondamir** does not mention the fact), “is the essence of the truth; yet the poems of **Hafiz** enjoy a wide celebrity, whereas those of some other writers have not passed beyond the gates of **Shiraz** <note>The capital of the Fars province in Iran, legendary for its beauty and as the lifelong home and inspiration of Hafiz.</note>.” But an occasional bandying of sharp speeches, in which the King usually came off second best, did little harm to a friendship which was based upon a marked correspondence in tastes. 

“Since the hour,” declares **Hafiz**, “that the wine-cup received honour from **Shah Shudja**, Fortune has put the goblet of joy into the hand of all wine-drinkers”; and in several poems he welcomes **Shah Shudja’s** accession to the throne and the consequent removal of an edict against the drinking of wine: “The daughter of the grape <note>original: "dukhtar-i tak"; a classic Persian poetic metaphor for wine.</note> has repented of her retirement; she went to the keeper of the peace (i.e. **Shah**

**Shah Shudja** was not the only member of the **House of Muzaffar** <note>The Muzaffarid dynasty (1314–1393), which ruled much of central and southern Iran.</note> who protected **Hafiz**; the warrior prince **Mansur** was his loyal friend. **Hafiz** seems to have been away from **Shiraz** when **Mansur** took the throne—perhaps he had followed the retreating army of **Timur** <note>Also known as Tamerlane, the Turco-Mongol conqueror who invaded Persia during Hafiz's lifetime.</note>. “The wind has brought me news,” the poet cries, “that the days of sorrow are over; I shall return to **Shiraz** through the favor of my friend. On the banners of the **Conqueror** <note>original: "Mansur." The name Mansur literally means "The Victorious" or "The Conqueror."</note>, **Hafiz** is lifted to the heavens; seeking a sanctuary, his fate has placed him upon the steps of a throne.”

**Mansur** held the poet in very high regard. There is a tradition that when he appointed one of his sons as a provincial governor, the young man asked his father to give him the **Vizir** <term>Vizier</term> <note>A high-ranking political advisor or government minister.</note> **Jelaleddin** as a counselor, and **Hafiz** as a teacher. “What!” **Mansur** replied, “Do you wish to be King while your father is still alive, that you demand the two wisest men in the kingdom?”

It is not often that a teacher and the favourite of princes enjoys unmixed popularity, especially when his criticisms of such as disagree with him are as harsh and as often repeated as are those of **Hafiz**; nor does he seem to have been an exception to the general rule. Moreover, his own conduct gave his enemies sufficient grounds for complaint. His biographers, as biographers will, take a rosy view of his life. **Daulat Shah** <note>Daulat Shah al-Samarqandi (died c. 1494), a famous biographer of Persian poets whose work, while influential, is often considered more hagiographical than strictly historical.</note>, for instance, states that “he turned always to the company of **dervishes** <term>Dervish</term> <note>A member of a Sufi religious order; while some were truly ascetic, others were wandering mendicants, and Hafiz often satirized those he deemed hypocritical.</note> and of wise men, and sometimes he attained also to the society of princes; a friend of persons of eminent virtue and perfection, and of noble youths.” But such accounts as these are not entirely borne out by other traditions, and his poems do not seem to the unbiased reader to be the works of a man of ascetic temperament. **Hafiz** was married and he had a son. He laments the death of both wife and child in two poems which are translated in this volume. In spite of all the favours which he received from the great men of his day, he is said to have died poor.

From India to Constantinople his songs are sung and repeated by all who speak the Persian tongue, and the number of his European translators shows that his uncle’s curse <note>A reference to a legend where Hafiz's uncle, Sa'id, supposedly cursed the poet's work to be a source of distraction, though the "curse" is often interpreted by fans as the reason for his irresistible charm.</note> has a special and peculiar influence in Western countries. It is related that Nadir Shah <note>Nadir Shah (r. 1736–1747), a powerful Iranian monarch known for his military conquests.</note> took counsel from Hafiz’s book when he was meditating an expedition against Tauris <note>The city of Tabriz.</note>, and opened it at the following verse: “Irak and Fars thou hast conquered with thy songs, oh Hafiz; now it is the turn of Baghdad and the appointed hour of Tabriz.” Nadir Shah took this as an encouragement to fresh conquest, and went on his way rejoicing.

It is not only as a maker of exquisite verse but also as a philosopher that Hafiz has gained so wide an esteem in the East. No European who reads his *Divan* but will be taken captive by the delicious music of his songs, the delicate rhythms, the beat of the refrain, and the charming imagery. Some of them are instinct with the very spirit of youth and love and joy, some have a nobler humanity and cry out across the ages with a voice pitifully like our own; and yet few of us will turn to Hafiz for wisdom and comfort, or choose him as a guide. Hafiz belonged to the great sect from which so many of the most famous among Persian writers have sprung. Like Sa’di <note>Abu-Muhammad Muslih al-Din bin Abdallah Shirazi (1210–1292), one of the greatest Persian poets, known for his works the *Bustan* and *Gulistan*.</note> and Jami <note>Nur ad-Din Abd ar-Rahman Jami (1414–1492), often called the last great classical Persian poet.</note> and Jelaleddin Rumi <note>Jalal al-Din Muhammad Rumi (1207–1273), the world-renowned mystic and founder of the Mevlevi Order of dervishes.</note> and a score of others, he was a Sufi <term>Sufi: A practitioner of Sufism, the mystical branch of Islam that focuses on the internal search for God and the abandonment of the ego.</term>. Some have supposed that Sufism was imported from India after the time of Muhammad <note>original: “Mahommad”</note>; some that it was a development of the doctrines of Zoroaster <note>The ancient Iranian prophet who founded Zoroastrianism, the state religion of pre-Islamic Persia.</note> which the Prophet’s successors silenced but did not destroy. In reply to the first theory it has been objected that there is no historic proof of relations between India and Islamic <note>original: “Mahommadan”</note> countries after the Islamic era and before the rise of Sufism, by which the doctrines of the Indian mystics could have been propagated; and as for the second, it seems improbable that Sufism, of which the essential doctrine is unity,

# INTRODUCTION

<page-num>35</page-num>

that the world has no external or tangible existence; all that is, is God, and beyond him there is nothing. The intelligences and the souls of men, the angels, the heavens, the stars, the elements, and the three kingdoms of nature exist only in the mind of God and have no existence beyond.” “If this **Indian** doctrine of **Maya** <note>A concept in Indian philosophy referring to the "illusion" or "magic" that masks the true nature of reality, which is the singular divine essence.</note>, or **Illusion**,” adds **M. de Sacy** <note>Antoine-Isaac Silvestre de Sacy (1758–1838), a foundational French scholar of Arabic and Persian studies.</note>, “had been transferred to **Persia**, there is every reason to believe that mysticism, grounded on the doctrine that all things are an emanation from God and that unto him they shall return, may be traced to the same source.”

The keynote of **Sufiism** <note>The mystical dimension of Islam, emphasizing the direct personal experience of God through love and meditation.</note> is the union, the identification of God and man. It is a doctrine which lies at the root of all spiritual religions, but pushed too far it leads to **pantheism** <note>The belief that God and the universe are the same thing.</note>, **quietism** <note>A form of mysticism that emphasizes reaching a state of perfect stillness and passive contemplation of the Divine.</note>, and eventually to **nihilism** <note>In this spiritual context, the belief that the individual self and the physical world have no real existence.</note>. The highest good to which the **Sufis** can attain, is the annihilation of the actual—to forget that they have a separate existence, and to lose themselves in the **Divinity** as a drop of water is lost in the ocean.¹ In order to obtain this end

---

¹ Numberless beautiful images are used to describe the union of God and man. **Jelaleddin Rumi** <note>Jalal ad-Din Muhammad Rumi (1207–1273), one of the most celebrated Persian poets and Sufi masters.</note> points the same moral in the following exquisite **apologue** <note>A short story or fable intended to teach a moral or spiritual lesson.</note>: “There came one and knocked at the door of the **Beloved** <note>In Sufi poetry, God is often referred to as "the Beloved" or "the Friend."</note>. And a voice answered and said, ‘Who is there?’ The lover replied, ‘It is I.’ ‘Go hence,’ returned the voice; ‘there is no room within for thee and me.’ Then came the lover a second time and knocked, and again the voice demanded, ‘Who is there?’ He answered, ‘It is thou.’ ‘Enter,’ said the voice, ‘for I am within.’”

# INTRODUCTION

<page-num>36</page-num>

they recommend ascetic living and solitude; but they do not carry asceticism to the absurd extremes enjoined by the **Indian** mystics, nor do they approve of artificial aids for the subduing of consciousness, such as opium, or hashish, or the wild physical exertions of the **dancing dervishes** <note>A reference to the Mevlevi Order of Sufis, known for their ritual whirling dance (Sama) as a form of meditation.</note>. The drunkenness of the **Sufi poets**, say their interpreters, is nothing but an ecstatic frame of mind, in which the spirit is intoxicated with the contemplation of **God** just as the body is intoxicated with wine. 

According to the **Dabistan** <note>original: "Dabistan-i-Mazaheb"; an influential 17th-century Persian text exploring various world religions and philosophical systems.</note> there are four stages in the manifestation of the **Divinity**: 

1. In the first, the mystic sees **God** in the form of a corporal being; 
2. In the second, he sees him in the form of one of his attributes of action, as the **Maker** or the **Preserver** of the world; 
3. In the third, he appears in the form of an attribute which exists in his very essence, as **knowledge** or **life**; 
4. In the fourth, the mystic is no longer conscious of his own existence. 

To the last stage he can hope to attain but seldom.

This losing of the soul in **God** is only a return (and here we come near to such **Platonic doctrines** as those embodied in the **Phaedrus** <note>original: "Phædrus"; a dialogue written by Plato in the 4th century BCE concerning the nature of the soul and the pursuit of divine truth.</note>) to the conditions which existed before birth into the world. Just as in the **Dialogue** <note>The *Phaedrus*.</note> the immortal steed which is harnessed to the chariot of the soul, longs to return to the plain of birth, and to see again the true **justice**, **beauty**, and **wisdom** of which it has retained

<summary>This page describes the moderate nature of Sufi asceticism, its rejection of drug-induced states, and the four stages of divine recognition as outlined in the Dabistan, ultimately comparing the soul's return to God with Plato's philosophical theories.</summary>

# INTRODUCTION

an imperfect recollection, so the soul of the **Sufi** <note>A practitioner of Sufism, the mystical branch of Islam that seeks a direct, personal experience of God.</note> longs to return to **God**, from whom it has been separated by the mortal veil of the body. But this reunion is pushed much further by the **Eastern philosophers** than by **Plato**; it implies, according to them, the complete annihilation of distinct personality, corresponding to the conditions, quite unlike those described by the **Platonic Socrates**, which they believe to have existed before birth. There is nothing which is not from **God** and a part of **God**. In himself he contains both being and not being; when he chooses he casts his reflection upon the void, and that reflection is the universe. There is a fine passage in **Jami's** <note>Nur ad-Din Abd ar-Rahman Jami (1414–1492), a master of Persian poetry and Sufi philosophy.</note> ***Yusuf and Zuleikha*** <note>original: "Yusuf u Zulaikha"; a famous Persian epic poem that uses the story of Joseph and Potiphar's wife as an allegory for the soul's search for Divine Beauty.</note> in which he sets forth this doctrine of the creation. "Thou art but the glass," the poet concludes, "his is the face reflected in the mirror; nay, if thou lookest steadfastly, thou shalt see that he is the mirror also." In a parable, **Jami** illustrates the universal presence of **God**, and the blind searching of man for that by which he is surrounded on every side. There was a frog which sat upon the shores of the ocean, and ceaselessly day and night he sang its praise. "As far as mine eyes can see," he said, "I behold nothing but thy boundless surface." Some fish swimming in the shallow water heard the frog's song, and were filled with a desire to find that wonderful ocean of which he spoke, but

# INTRODUCTION

...go where they would, they could not discover it. At last, in the course of their search, they fell into a fisherman’s net, and as soon as they were drawn out of the water they saw beneath them the ocean for which they had been seeking. With a leap they returned into it.

The story of the creation as told in the **Quran** <note>original: "Koran"; the central religious text of Islam.</note> it is impossible for the **Sufis** <note>Mystics within Islam who seek a direct personal experience of the Divine, often through internal meditation and allegory.</note> to accept literally; they are bound to give an outward adhesion to it, but in their hearts they treat it as an allegory. The world is posterior to **God** only in the nature of its existence and not in time: the **Sufis** were not far from the doctrine of the eternity of matter, from which they were only withheld by the necessity of conforming with the teaching of the **Quran**. They content themselves with saying that the world came into existence when it pleased **God** to manifest himself beyond himself, and will cease when it shall please him to return into himself again.

It is more difficult to dispose of the resurrection of the body, which is constantly insisted upon by **Muhammad** <note>original: "Mahommad"; the Prophet of Islam.</note>. That the soul, when it has at last attained to complete union with **God**, should be obliged to return to the prison from whence it has escaped at death, is entirely repugnant to all **Sufis**; nor can they explain satisfactorily the divergence of their opinions from those of the **Prophet**.

# INTRODUCTION

...asked him <note>Likely referring to Ali, the fourth Caliph and a central figure in Islamic mystical tradition, who is often cited in such anecdotes.</note>, ‘What is **predestination** <note>The doctrine that all events have been willed by God.</note>?’ he answered, ‘It is a deep sea.’ And a second time he replied, ‘It is a dark road.’ And a third time, ‘It is a secret which I will not declare since God has seen fit to conceal it.’”

The **Sufis** <note>Mystics within Islam who prioritize the internal, spiritual journey toward God.</note> were obliged to abandon the concept of **free will**: it was impossible to attach any responsibility to the "reflection in the mirror." <note>A signature Sufi metaphor: if the world is just a mirror reflecting God, the reflection (man) has no power to move on its own.</note> But here, again, they did not venture to give expression to their real opinions, and their statements are therefore both confused and contradictory. “A man may say,” remarks the author of the ***Dabistan*** <note>original: "Dabistan-i Mazahib" or "The School of Religions"; a famous 17th-century Persian work examining various religions and philosophies.</note>, “that his actions are his own, and with equal truth that they are God’s.”

In the ***Gulshen-i-Raz*** <note>original: "Gulshan-i Raz"; "The Rose Garden of Secrets," a foundational 14th-century Persian poem by Mahmoud Shabistari that explains Sufi metaphysics.</note>, a poem written in the year 1317, and therefore contemporary with the poet **Hafiz**, it is distinctly laid down that God will take men’s actions into account: “After that moment (*i.e.* the **Day of Judgment** <note>The final assessment of humanity's deeds in Islamic eschatology.</note>) he will question them concerning good and evil.”

But such expressions as these are in direct opposition to the rest of Sufi teaching. There is neither good nor evil, since both alike flow from God, from whom all flows. Some go so far as to prefer **Pharaoh** to **Moses**, or **Nimrod** to **Abraham**, because they say that though Pharaoh and Nimrod <note>Traditional "villains" of scripture who opposed the prophets Moses and Abraham.</note> were in apparent revolt against the Divinity, in reality they knew their own nothingness and accepted the part that the divine wisdom had imposed upon them. Or in the words of the ***Bhagavadgitah*** <note>original: "Bhagavad Gita"; a sacred Hindu text featuring a dialogue between Prince Arjuna and the god Krishna.</note>: He who knows himself in everything and everything in himself, will not injure himself by himself. This is the sum and tenor of all morality, and this is the standpoint of a man knowing himself a ***Brahman*** <note>In Indian philosophy, the ultimate, universal reality or absolute spirit.</note>.”

The **Sufis** were forced to pay an exaggerated deference to the **Prophet** <note>Muhammad, the founder of Islam.</note> and to **Ali** <note>Ali ibn Abi Talib, the Prophet's cousin and son-in-law, who is a central figure in Sufi spiritual lineages.</note> in order to keep on good terms with the orthodox, but since they believed God to be the source of all creeds they could not reasonably place one above another; nay more, since they taught that any man who practised a particular religion had failed to free himself from **duality** <note>The state of seeing oneself as separate from God, which Sufis believe is an illusion to be overcome.</note> and to reach perfect union with God, they

In the language of religious mysticism, God is not only the Creator and Ruler of the world, he is also the Essentially Beautiful and the True Beloved. Love, of which the divine being is at once the source and the object, plays a large part in Sufi <note>Sufism is the mystical dimension of Islam, focusing on the inward search for God and the shunning of materialism.</note> writings, a part which it is difficult, and sometimes unwise, to distinguish from an exaggerated expression of the human affections. Jami <note>Nur ad-Din Abd al-Rahman Jami (1414–1492), a celebrated Persian poet and scholar often called the last great classical poet of Persia.</note> describes Pure Being, before it had been manifested in Creation, “singing of love unto itself in a wordless melody,”<gloss>1</gloss> and in the same strain Hafiz <note>Shams al-Din Muhammad Hafiz-e Shirazi (1315–1390), a Persian poet whose work is considered the pinnacle of Persian literature; his ghazals often blend themes of earthly and divine love.</note> sings of “the Imperial Beauty which is for ever playing the game of love with itself.” Like the echo of a Greek voice falls Jami’s doctrine of human love: “Avert not thy face from an earthly beloved, since even this may serve to raise thee to the love of the True.” It is almost possible to read in the Persian poem the words of the wise Diotima to Socrates <note>A reference to Plato’s *Symposium*, where Diotima teaches Socrates the "Ladder of Love," moving from the beauty of individual bodies to the beauty of the soul, and finally to the Form of Beauty itself.</note>: “He who...

> “...has been instructed thus far in the things of love,

> far from the end.” <note>This is a quotation from Plato’s *Symposium*, describing the "Ladder of Love" where the soul ascends from physical attraction to the contemplation of Divine Beauty—a concept that closely parallels Sufi thought.</note>

The Sufis <note>Sufism is the mystical branch of Islam that emphasizes the internal, spiritual search for God over outward legalism.</note> had no difficulty in finding in the Koran <note>The Quran, the central religious text of Islam.</note> texts in support of their teaching. When Mahommad <note>The Prophet Muhammad (c. 570–632 CE).</note> exclaims, “There are times when neither cherubim <note>High-ranking celestial beings or angels.</note> nor prophet are equal unto me!” the Sufis declare that he alludes to moments of ecstatic union with God; and his account of the victory of Bedr <note>The Battle of Badr (624 CE), a pivotal early Islamic victory attributed by the Quran to divine intervention.</note>—“Thou didst not slay them, but God slew them, and thou didst not shoot when thou didst shoot, but God shot” <note>original: Quran 8:17. This verse is often cited by mystics to illustrate the concept of *fana*, the passing away of the individual self into the Divine action.</note>—they take as a proof of the Prophet’s belief in the essential oneness of God and man.<sup>1</sup> The whole book is twisted after this fashion <note>The author uses "twisted" to describe the esoteric interpretation (*ta'wil*) of the Quran, where mystics find hidden meanings that differ from literalist readings.</note> into agreement with their views.

Beautiful and spiritual as some of these doctrines are, they can hardly be said to form an adequate guide to conduct. The Sufis, however, are regarded in the East as men leading a virtuous and pure life. Even the etymology of their name points to the same conclusion: Sufi comes from an Arabic word signifying wool <note>original: *suf* (wool). Early Islamic ascetics wore simple, coarse woolen garments as a symbol of their rejection of worldly luxury and material wealth.</note>, and indicates that they were accustomed

to clothe themselves in simple woollen garments. <note>The word "Sufi" is widely believed to be derived from the Arabic word *suf*, meaning wool, referring to the coarse cloaks worn by early Muslim ascetics to signal their rejection of worldly luxury.</note> They occupy in the East much the same position that Madame Guyon and the Jansenists occupied in the West <note>Madame Guyon (1648–1717) was a French mystic, and the Jansenists were a Catholic movement; both emphasized an internal, personal relationship with God that often bypassed or challenged church hierarchy.</note>, and they teach the same doctrine of quietism <note>A form of religious mysticism that stresses a state of perfect passivity and the stilling of the individual will to allow the Divine to act within the soul.</note>, which, while it lends to its followers the virtues of exaggerated submission, saps the root of a faith that is manifested in works. So far as the Sufis are striving earnestly after union with God, they are saved from the logical consequences of their doctrines: “Their ear is strained to catch the sounds of the lute, their eyes are fixed upon the cup, their bosoms are filled with the desire of this world and of the world to come.”¹ And in the same spirit Hafiz <note>Hafiz of Shiraz (c. 1315–1390), the premier master of the Persian lyric poem (ghazal), whose work uses the imagery of wine and earthly beauty to symbolize spiritual intoxication.</note> sings: “Though the wind of discord shake the two worlds, mine eyes are fixed upon the road from whence cometh my Friend.” <note>In Sufi literature, "the Friend" is a standard term for God, emphasizing a relationship based on intimacy and love rather than fear.</note> The idealism of the Sufis led them to deny the morality of all actions <note>This refers to the belief that if God is the only true actor in the universe, human "good" and "evil" are secondary to the Divine Will.</note>, but they restricted the consequences of their principles to the adepts who had attained to perfect union with God, and even for them the moments of ecstasy are few. Most Sufis are good and religious men, holding it their duty to conform outwardly, and no discredit to use all artifices to conceal from the orthodox the beliefs which they cherish in their heart, but holding also that the practice of the Mahommadan religion <note>A historical term for Islam.</note>, to the rites of which they

<meta>This page continues the introduction’s exploration of Sufi philosophy, specifically moving into a critique of "quietism" as described by the French diplomat and author Count Gobineau, and how Persian poets popularized these mystical concepts through symbolism.</meta>

# INTRODUCTION

->47<-

have attached symbolic meanings, is the only way to the perfection to which they aspire. Nevertheless, Count Gobineau <note>Joseph Arthur de Gobineau (1816–1882) was a French diplomat and scholar who lived in Persia and wrote "Religions and Philosophies in Central Asia."</note> is of opinion that quietism <note>A state of spiritual passivity and the suppression of the individual will, which critics argued led to a lack of social or political action.</note> is the great curse of the East. “The dominant characteristic of Sufiism,” he says, “is to unite by a weak chain of doctrine, ideas the significance of which is very different, so different that there is in reality but one connecting link between them, and that link is a quietism adapted to them all, a passive disposition of spirit which surrounds with a nimbus of inert sentiment all conceptions of God, of man, and of the universe. It is this quietism, and not Islam, which is the running sore of all Oriental <note>A term historically used by Westerners to refer to the Middle East and Asia; here it specifically refers to the Islamic world of the 19th century.</note> countries.”

Unfortunately, as he points out, the conditions of Oriental life are such as to enforce rather than to control a disposition to mysticism. The poets found ready to their hand a mass of vague and beautiful thought eminently suited to imaginative treatment; whether they believed in it or not they used it, and thereby popularised it, delighting, as only an Oriental can, in the necessity of veiling it with exquisite symbolism, and throwing round it a cloud of charming phrases. These phrases caught and held the Oriental ear; and the Oriental mind is faithful to a formula once accepted. Moreover, when a man looked about him and saw the vicissitudes of mortal existence—nowhere more marked

I think it was not only to curry favour with a king that he welcomed the accession of Shah Shudja <note>Shah Shudja (reigned 1358–1384) was a ruler of the Mozaffarid dynasty in Shiraz and a known patron of Hafiz. Of the two conclusions that are commonly drawn from the statement that to-morrow we die, Hafiz accepted neither unmodified by the other. “Eat and drink,” seemed to him a poor solution of the mysterious purpose of human life, and an unsatisfactory sign-post to happiness; “the abode of pleasure,” he says, “was never reached except through pain.” On the other hand, he was equally unwilling to despise the good things of this world. “The Garden of Paradise <note>A reference to the celestial afterlife in Islamic tradition.</note> may be pleasant, but forget not the shade of the willow-tree and the fair margin of the fruitful field.” “Now, now while the rose is with us, sing her praise; now, while we are here to listen, Minstrel, strike the lute! for the burden of all thy songs has been that the present is all too short, and already the unknown future is upon us.” He, too, would have us cut down far reaching hope to the limit of our little day, though he

But, then, our appetites are not the same.

The tendency in dealing with a mystical poet is to read into him so-called deeper meanings, even when the simple meaning is clear enough and sufficient in itself. Hafiz is one of those who has suffered from this process; it has removed him, in great measure, from the touch of human sympathies which are, when all is said and done, a poet's true kingdom. Of a different age, a different race, and a different civilisation from ours, there are yet snatches in his songs of that melody of human life which is everywhere the same. As simple and as touching is his lament for his son: “Alas! he found it easy to depart, but unto me he left the harder pilgrimage.” <note>Hafiz here mourns the death of his son; the "harder pilgrimage" refers to the remaining years of life on earth after a loved one has died.</note> And for his wife: “Then said my heart, I will rest me in this city which is illumined by her presence; already her feet were bent upon a longer journey, but my poor heart knew it not.” Not Shakespeare himself has found a more passionate image for love than: “Open my grave when I am dead, and thou shalt see a cloud of smoke rising out from it; then shalt thou know that the fire still burns in my dead heart—yea, it has set my very winding-sheet alight.” <note>The "smoke" and "fire" represent the undying heat of passion that survives even the coldness of the grave.</note> Or: “If the scent of her hair were to blow across my dust when I had been dead a hundred years, my mouldering bones would rise and come dancing out of the tomb.” And he knows of what he writes when he says, “I have estimated the influence of Reason upon Love and found that it is like that of a raindrop upon the ocean, which makes one little mark upon the water’s face and disappears.” These are the utterances of a great poet, the imaginative interpreter of the heart of man; they are not of one age, or of another, but for all time. # INTRODUCTION

->57<-

"Destiny, and however grateful may be the shade of the holy trees, they could afford you no protection." <note>This continues the quote from the previous page regarding the cypress tree and the Garden of Paradise, suggesting that even spiritual rewards cannot shield one from divine decree.</note> Nor can I believe that it is an earthly love of whom he speaks when he says, "Since the **Beloved** <term>The Beloved: In Sufi poetry, this refers to the Divine (God), though expressed through the language of human romance.</term> has veiled his face, how comes it that his lovers are reciting his beauties? They can only tell what they imagine to be there." We are all engaged in telling each other—only what we imagine to be there.

It is a curious coincidence (if it be nothing more) that at the time when mystical poetry was taking a recognised place in the literature of **Persia** and of **India**, it was also springing into existence in the West. The songs of the **Troubadours** <term>Troubadours: Medieval lyric poets from Southern Europe who composed songs of courtly love, often containing layers of spiritual or hidden meaning.</term> were avowedly intended to convey a meaning deeper than that which lay upon the surface; the ***Romance of the Rose*** <note>original: "Le Roman de la Rose." A 13th-century French poem that uses an elaborate garden allegory to describe the pursuit of love and, arguably, spiritual enlightenment.</note> comes nearer than any other Western allegory to a full-fledged mysticism worthy of an Oriental poet. **St. Francis** <note>Saint Francis of Assisi (c. 1181–1226), an Italian mystic and friar whose poems to the Divine share the emotional intensity of Persian Sufi verse.</note> addresses his Redeemer in terms not very different from those used by **Hafiz** to express his longing after divine wisdom, and the **Beatrice**, perhaps of the ***Vita Nuova*** <note>Latin title meaning "The New Life." A work by Dante Alighieri chronicling his devotion to Beatrice.</note>, certainly of the ***Divine Comedy*** <note>Dante's masterpiece, "La Divina Commedia," which maps the soul's journey toward God.</note>, is no less intangible than the allegorical mistress (when she is allegorical) of the Persian.

**Hafiz** and **Dante**, it is interesting to note, were almost contemporaries. At the time when **Dante** was climbing **Can Grande’s** <note>Cangrande I della Scala, the Italian nobleman who hosted Dante during his exile in Verona. The "weary stair" refers to the bitterness of living as a dependent guest.</note> weary stair, **Hafiz** was opening his eyes upon a yet more tumultuous world.

It notes the historical coincidence that both Hafiz and Dante lived during the same era, exploring similar spiritual allegories.</summary>

# INTRODUCTION

->59<-

**circumstances** have no value; contemporary history is too small an episode to occupy his thoughts. During his lifetime the city that he loved <note>Shiraz, the capital of the Fars province in Iran, which was a major center of Persian culture and learning.</note>, perhaps as dearly as **Dante** loved Florence, was besieged and taken five or six times; it changed hands even more often. It was drenched with blood by one conqueror, filled with revelry by a second, and subjected to the hard rule of **asceticism** <term>Asceticism: A lifestyle of rigorous self-denial and the renunciation of worldly pleasures, often for spiritual discipline.</term> by a third. One after another **Hafiz** saw kings and princes rise into power and vanish "*like snow upon the desert's dusty face.*" <note>original: "like snow upon the desert's dusty face" — This is a famous line from Edward FitzGerald's 1859 translation of the Rubaiyat of Omar Khayyam, another Persian poet, used here to emphasize the fleeting nature of life.</note> Pitiful tragedies, great rejoicings, the fall of kingdoms, and the clash of battle—all these he must have seen and heard. But what echo of them is there in his poems? Almost none. An occasional allusion which learned commentators refer to some political event; an exaggerated effusion in praise first of one king, then of another; the celebration of such and such a victory and of the prowess of such and such a royal general—just what any self-respecting court poet would feel it incumbent upon himself to write; and no more.

But some of us will feel that the apparent indifference of Hafiz lends to his philosophy a quality which that of Dante does not possess. The Italian is bound down within the limits of his own **realism** <term>Realism: In this context, an artistic or philosophical focus on depicting things as they actually exist in the physical and social world.</term>, his theory of the universe is essentially of his own age, and what to him was so acutely real is to many of us merely a beautiful or a terrible image. The

# INTRODUCTION

->60<-

the picture that **Hafiz** drew represents a wider landscape, though the immediate foreground may not be so distinct. It is as if his mental eye, endowed with wonderful acuteness of vision, had penetrated into those provinces of thought which we of a later age were destined to inhabit. We can forgive him for leaving to us so indistinct a representation of his own time, and of the life of the individual in it, when we find him formulating ideas as profound as the warning that there is no musician to whose music both the drunk and the sober can dance. <note>This metaphor suggests that spiritual or poetic truths are perceived differently depending on one's state of mind or level of enlightenment.</note>

**Renan** <note>Ernest Renan (1823–1892) was an influential French expert on Middle Eastern languages and civilizations, known for his critical approach to religious history.</note> has put into a few luminous sentences his view of the mystical poets of India and Persia. 

> “It is well known that in these countries,” he says, “a vast literature has developed where divine love and earthly love intersect in a way that is often difficult to untangle. The origin of this singular genre of poetry is a question that has not yet been clarified. In many cases, the mystical meanings assigned to certain Persian and Indian erotic poems have no more reality than the allegories of the **Song of Songs** <note>original: "Cantique des Cantiques" — the Song of Solomon in the Bible, which is traditionally interpreted as an allegory of God's love for humanity.</note>. For **Hafiz**, for example, it seems quite likely that the allegorical explanation is most often a product of the commentators' fancy, or of the precautions that the poet's admirers were obliged to take to preserve the **orthodoxy** of their favorite author. Then, the imagination having been sparked by this theme, and minds having been skewed by an **exegesis** <term>Exegesis: The critical explanation or interpretation of a text, especially religious scripture.</term>...” <note>original: "On sait que dans ces pays... s’est développée une vaste littérature où l’amour divin et l’amour terrestre se croisen... l’explication allégorique est le plus souvent un fruit de la fantaisie des commentateurs..." — Renan argues that readers often "invent" mystical meanings for poems that were originally meant to be simple love songs, often to protect the poet from religious persecution.</note>

Indeed, it is as a reaction against the dryness of Islamism that **Sufism** has prospered among non-Arab Muslims. One must see in it a revolt of the Aryan spirit <note>Renan uses "Aryan" and "Semitic" here in a 19th-century linguistic and racial context to contrast Indo-European cultures (like Persia and India) with the Arab culture where Islam originated.</note> against the terrifying simplicity of the Semitic spirit, which, through the rigor of its theology, excludes all private devotion, all secret doctrine, and all living and varied religious combinations.” ¹

# INTRODUCTION

->62<-

...acquainted. “The nightingale, and none beside, knows the full worth of the rose,” sings **Hafiz** <note>Hafiz (c. 1315–1390) was a Persian poet whose collected works, the *Divan*, are considered a pinnacle of Persian literature. His poems often layer themes of love, wine, and spiritual devotion.</note>, “for many a one reads the leaf and understands not the meaning thereof.” But though we may not all be nightingales, we have some guide to the interpretation of the leaf. Many of the words in the **Sufi** <term>Sufi: A practitioner of Sufism, the mystical dimension of Islam that seeks a direct, personal experience of God through love and inward contemplation.</term> dictionary have been expounded to the outer world. The tavern, for instance, is the place of instruction or worship, of which the tavern-keeper is the teacher or priest, and the wine the spirit of divine knowledge which is poured out for his disciples; the idol is God; beauty is the divine perfection; shining locks the expansion of his glory; **down on the cheek** <note>Refers to the fine, new hair appearing on a youth's face; in mystical poetry, it symbolizes the "world of spirits" or the veil that both hides and reveals the Divine.</note> denotes the cloud of spirits that encircles his throne; and a **black mole** <note>original: "khal"</note> is the point of indivisible unity. The catalogue might be continued to any extent; almost every word has a vague and somewhat shifting significance in the language of mysticism, which he who has a mind for such exercises may decipher if he choose.

**Hafiz** is rather the forerunner than the founder of this school of poets. It is equally unsatisfactory to give a completely mystical or a completely material interpretation to his songs. He wrote of the world as he found it. In his experience pleasure and religion were the two most important incentives to human action; he ignored neither the one nor the other. # INTRODUCTION

From our point of view, then, the sum of his philosophy seems to be, that though there is little of which we can be certain, that little must always be the object of all men's desire ; each of us will set out upon the search for it along a different road, and if none will find his road easy to follow, each may, if he be wise, discover compensations for his toil by the wayside. And for the rest, “Who knows the **secret of the veil**?” <note>original: "asrar-i parda." In Persian mystical poetry, the "veil" symbolizes the boundary between the physical world and the Divine Truth; to know the "secret of the veil" is to understand the hidden mysteries of God.</note> Like many a good and brave man before his time and since, I think he was content to “faintly trust the **larger hope**.” <note>The author quotes Alfred Lord Tennyson’s poem *In Memoriam* (1850), signaling a Victorian-era perspective that finds a parallel between English poetry and Persian mysticism in their shared struggle with faith and doubt.</note>
