# FROM THE COLLECTED POEMS OF HAFIZ

<term>Hafiz</term> <note>Literally "the Memorizer," a title for one who has committed the entire Quran to memory; the pen name of the 14th-century Persian poet Shams al-Din Muhammad.</note>
<term>Divan</term> <note>A traditional collection of a poet's shorter works, usually organized alphabetically by the last letter of the rhyme.</note>

# INTRODUCTION

->63<-

...the poet is seen through the eyes of a Westerner. <note>This sentence continues from the previous page, likely contrasting a literal or materialistic view of the poet with a more Western, mystical interpretation.</note> It is difficult to define exactly why he is so deeply valued in the East, and perhaps impossible for us to truly grasp how his own people understand his teachings. From our perspective, the core of his philosophy seems to be that while there is very little we can be certain of in this life, that small amount of truth must be what everyone strives for. Each of us will begin our search for it on a different path. Although no one will find their journey easy, those who are wise may find small rewards for their hard work along the way. As for the rest, “Who knows the **secret of the veil**?” <note>original: "asrar-i parda." In the world of Persian mystical poetry, the "veil" represents the thin line between our physical world and the ultimate Divine Truth. To know the "secret" is to understand the mysteries of God that are hidden from regular sight.</note> Like many good and brave people who lived before him and since, I believe he was satisfied to “faintly trust the **larger hope**.” <note>The author is quoting from the poem *In Memoriam* (1850) by Alfred Lord Tennyson. This reflects a common Victorian-era perspective that found parallels between English literature and Persian mysticism, specifically in how both struggle with the balance of faith and doubt.</note>

## POEMS FROM THE
# DIVAN OF HAFIZ
### I

ARISE, oh <term>Cup-bearer</term><note>original: "Sāqī." In Sufi poetry, the Cup-bearer is often a symbol for the spiritual guide or the Divine presence who pours the "wine" of spiritual ecstasy into the heart of the seeker.</note>, rise ! and bring
To lips that are thirsting the bowl they praise,
For it seemed that love was an easy thing,
But my feet have fallen on difficult ways.
I have prayed the wind o'er my heart to fling
The fragrance of **musk** <note>A deep, earthy perfume used here to describe the intoxicating scent of the Beloved’s hair.</note> in her hair that sleeps—
In the night of her hair—yet no fragrance stays
The tears of my heart's blood my sad heart weeps.

Hear the <term>Tavern-keeper</term><note>original: "Pīr-i Mughān." Literally the "Elder of the Magi." This figure represents the wise spiritual master who understands the true nature of reality, often found in the "tavern" of the heart rather than the formal mosque.</note> who counsels you :
“ With **wine**, with red wine your **prayer carpet** dye ! ” <note>To soak a prayer rug in wine—forbidden in orthodox practice—is a powerful metaphor for prioritizing true spiritual experience and devotion over outward religious rituals.</note>
There was never a traveller like him but knew
The ways of the road and the hostelry.
Where shall I rest, when the still night through,
Beyond thy gateway, oh Heart of my heart,
The bells of the camels lament and cry :
“ Bind up thy burden again and depart ! ” <note>The sound of camel bells signaling a caravan's departure represents the transience of life and the soul's constant journey toward the Divine.</note>

The waves run high, night is clouded with fears,
And eddying whirlpools clash and roar ;
How shall my drowning voice strike their ears
Whose light-freighted vessels have reached the shore ? <note>The poet contrasts his own tumultuous spiritual struggle with those who remain safely on the "shore" of a mundane, uncomplicated life.</note>

I sought mine own; the unsparing years
Have brought me mine own, a dishonoured name.
What cloak shall cover my misery o’er
When each jesting mouth has rehearsed my shame!

Oh <term>Hafiz</term><note>The pen name of the poet, meaning "one who has memorized the Quran." It was traditional for Persian poets to include their name in the final stanza of a poem.</note>, seeking an end to strife,
Hold fast in thy mind what the wise have writ:
“If at last thou attain the desire of thy life,
Cast the world aside, yea, abandon it!”

### II

The bird of gardens sang unto the rose,
New blown in the clear dawn: “Bow down thy head!
As fair as thou within this garden close,
Many have bloomed and died.” She laughed and said:
“That I am born to fade grieves not my heart;
But never was it a true lover’s part
To vex with bitter words his love’s repose.” <note>This stanza introduces the "Gul-u-Bulbul" (Rose and Nightingale) motif, the most famous allegory in Persian poetry representing the pining lover and the beautiful, often indifferent, beloved.</note>

The tavern step shall be thy hostelry,
For Love’s diviner breath comes but to those
That suppliant on the dusty threshold lie.
And thou, if thou would’st drink the wine that flows
From Life’s bejewelled goblet, ruby red,
Upon thine eyelashes thine eyes shall thread
A thousand tears for this temerity.

Last night when <term>Irem’s</term><note>original: "Iram." A legendary, fabulous garden mentioned in the Quran, often used to symbolize a terrestrial paradise or the transience of worldly beauty.</note> magic garden slept,
Stirring the hyacinth’s purple tresses curled,
The wind of morning through the alleys stept.
“Where is thy cup, the **mirror of the world**? <note>original: "Jām-i Jam." The legendary Cup of Jamshid, a mythical Persian king. The cup allowed him to see all that was happening in the seven heavens and the world; in Sufism, it represents the heart of the mystic reflecting the Divine.</note>

“Ah, where is Love, thou <term>Throne of Djem</term><note>original: "Takht-i Jamshid." Referring to the mythical Persian King Jamshid, whose throne and legendary cup could reveal the entire world; here used to symbolize a place of ultimate power or wisdom.</note>?” I cried.
The breezes knew not; but “Alas,” they sighed,
“That happiness should sleep so long!” and wept.

Not on the lips of men Love’s secret lies,
Remote and unrevealed his dwelling-place.
Oh <term>Saki</term><note>The cup-bearer. In Persian mystical poetry, the Saki is often a symbol for the Divine or the spiritual guide who provides the "wine" of divine knowledge.</note>, come! the idle laughter dies
When thou the feast with heavenly wine dost grace.
Patience and wisdom, <term>Hafiz</term><note>The poet's pen name, meaning "one who has memorized the Quran."</note>, in a sea
Of thine own tears are drowned; thy misery
They could not still nor hide from curious eyes.

->III<-

Wind from the east, oh <term>Lapwing</term><note>original: "Hudhud." In Persian tradition, the Lapwing (or Hoopoe) was the messenger between King Solomon and the Queen of Sheba.</note> of the day,
I send thee to my Lady, though the way
Is far to <term>Saba</term><note>Sheba, the kingdom of the Queen who was beloved by Solomon; a metaphor for the distant home of the beloved.</note>, where I bid thee fly;
Lest in the dust thy tameless wings should lie,
Broken with grief, I send thee to thy nest,
->Fidelity.<-

Or far or near there is no halting-place
Upon Love’s road—absent, I see thy face,
And in thine ear my wind-blown greetings sound,
North winds and east waft them where they are bound,
Each morn and eve convoys of greeting fair
->I send to thee.<-

Unto mine eyes a stranger, thou that art
A comrade ever-present to my heart,
What whispered prayers and what full meed of praise
->I send to thee.<-

### IV

SLEEP on thine eyes, bright as <term>narcissus</term><note>original: "nargis." In Persian poetry, the narcissus is the standard metaphor for the eyes of the beloved, often described as languid, intoxicating, or heavy with sleep.</note> flowers,

->Ah, not in vain!<-

Before the milk upon thy lips was dry,
I said: “Lips where the <term>salt</term><note>original: "namak." To have "salt" in Persian aesthetics means to possess a piquant, sharp charm or attractiveness that goes beyond mere physical beauty.</note> of wit doth lie,
Sweets shall be mingled with thy mockery,
->And not in vain!”<-

Thy mouth the <term>fountain</term><note>original: "chashma-yi hayvān." The mythical Water of Life or Fountain of Youth, often associated in Persian poetry with the life-giving moisture of the beloved's lips.</note> where Life’s waters flow,
A dimpled well of tears is set below,
And death lies near to life thy lovers know,
->But know in vain !<-

God send to thee great length of happy days !
Lo, not for his own life thy servant prays ;
Love’s dart in thy bent brows the <term>Archer</term><note>The "Archer" is a common metaphor for the beloved, whose curved eyebrows are the bow and whose eyelashes are the arrows that pierce the lover's heart.</note> lays,
->Nor shoots in vain.<-

Art thou with grief afflicted, with the smart
Of absence, and is bitter toil thy part?
Thy lamentations and thy tears, oh Heart,
->Are not in vain !<-

Last night the <term>wind</term><note>original: "saba." The morning zephyr or breeze that acts as a messenger between lovers in Persian literary tradition.</note> from out her village blew,
And wandered all the garden alleys through,
Oh rose, tearing thy bosom’s robe in two ;
->’Twas not in vain !<-

And Hafiz, though thy heart within thee dies,
Hiding love’s agony from curious eyes,
Ah, not in vain thy tears, not vain thy sighs,
->Not all in vain !<-

### V

OH <term>Turkish maid of Shiraz</term><note>original: "Agar ān Turk-i Shīrāzī." In Persian poetry, "Turk" is a standard metaphor for a beautiful, often cruel or captivating beloved, while Shiraz is the poet's beloved home city.</note> ! in thy hand
If thou’lt take my heart, for the mole on thy cheek

Bring, <term>Cup-bearer</term><note>original: "Saqi." The one who serves wine, often symbolizing the spiritual guide who pours the "wine" of divine love and wisdom into the heart of the seeker.</note>, all that is left of thy wine !

## 72

In the Garden of Paradise vainly thou’lt seek
The lip of the fountain of <term>Ruknabad</term><note>A famous stream in Shiraz, the poet's hometown. Hafiz famously claimed its waters were superior to those of the celestial rivers of Heaven.</note>,
And the bowers of <term>Mosalla</term><note>original: "Musallā." An open-air place of prayer and recreation in Shiraz, celebrated for its rose gardens.</note> where roses twine.

They have filled the city with blood and broil,
Those soft-voiced <term>Lulis</term><note>original: "Lūliyān." Referring to gypsy musicians and dancers; in Persian poetry, they represent a wild, captivating beauty that "robs" the peace of the heart.</note> for whom we sigh ;
As **Turkish robbers** fall on the spoil,
They have robbed and plundered the peace of my heart.
Dowered is my mistress, a beggar am I ;
What shall I bring her ? a beautiful face
Needs nor jewel nor mole nor the tiring-maid’s art.

Brave tales of singers and wine relate,
The key to the **Hidden** ’twere vain to seek ;
No wisdom of ours has unlocked that gate,
And locked to our wisdom it still shall be.
But of <term>Joseph’s</term><note>original: "Yūsuf." Representing the ultimate archetype of male beauty in Islamic tradition.</note> beauty the lute shall speak ;
And the minstrel knows that <term>Zuleika</term><note>The name given in tradition to Potiphar's wife, whose overwhelming love for Joseph led her to tear his tunic.</note> came forth,
Love parting the curtains of modesty.

When thou spokest ill of thy servant ’twas well—
God pardon thee ! for thy words were sweet ;
Not unwelcomed the bitterest answer fell
From lips where the **ruby and sugar** lay.
But, fair Love, let good counsel direct thy feet ;
Far dearer to youth than dear life itself
Are the warnings of one grown wise—and grey !

The song is sung and the pearl is strung ;
Come hither, oh <term>Hafiz</term><note>The poet’s pen name, meaning "One who has memorized the Quran." It was common for Persian poets to include their name in the final stanza (the "takhallus").</note>, and sing again !
And the listening Heavens above thee hung
Shall loose o’er thy verse the <term>Pleiades’</term><note>original: "Iqd-i-Surayya." A cluster of stars. Hafiz compares the perfect arrangement of his poetic lines to the scattering of stars across the sky.</note> chain.

<summary>This page concludes one of the most celebrated poems in Persian literature, wherein Hafiz praises the landmarks of Shiraz and compares his poetic craft to the divine arrangement of the stars.</summary>

### VI

A flower-tinted cheek, the flowery close
Of the fair earth, these are enough for me—
Enough that in the meadow wanes and grows
The shadow of a graceful **cypress-tree** <term>Sarv</term><note>In Persian poetry, the cypress tree is a recurring symbol for the beloved’s tall, elegant, and perfectly balanced physical stature.</note>.
I am no lover of hypocrisy;
Of all the treasures that the earth can boast,
A brimming cup of wine I prize the most—
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;This is enough for me !

To them that here renowned for virtue live,
A heavenly palace is the meet reward;
To me, the drunkard and the beggar, give
The **temple of the grape** <term>Kharabat</term><note>original: "Kharābāt." Literally meaning "the ruins," this term refers to the tavern. In Sufi poetry, it represents a place of spiritual transformation where the seeker abandons social status and outward piety to find God through love.</note> with red wine stored!
Beside a river seat thee on the sward;
It floweth past—so flows thy life away,
So sweetly, swiftly, fleets our little day—
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Swift, but enough for me !

Look upon all the gold in the world’s mart,
On all the tears the world hath shed in vain;
Shall they not satisfy thy craving heart?
I have enough of loss, enough of gain;
I have my Love, what more can I obtain?
Mine is the joy of her companionship
Whose **healing lip** <note>Hafiz often describes the beloved’s lip or breath as having the "Messianic" power to heal or bring the dead back to life, a common metaphor for the restorative power of love.</note> is laid upon my lip—
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;This is enough for me !

<summary>Poem VI of the Divan explores the theme of spiritual contentment, where Hafiz rejects worldly ambition and religious hypocrisy in favor of the simple beauty of nature, wine, and the beloved.</summary>

<meta>This page continues from page 73, concluding Poem VI and beginning Poem VII. In Poem VI, the poet reinforces the theme of "Qana'at" (contentment), preferring the village of the beloved over the promise of Paradise. Poem VII introduces a classic carpe diem theme, urging the reader to enjoy the "cash" of the present moment—represented by wine and nature—rather than the "credit" of the afterlife, while acknowledging the inevitable cycle of mortality.</meta>

# POEMS FROM THE DIVAN OF HAFIZ
## 74

I pray thee send not forth my naked soul
From its poor house to seek for Paradise;
Though heaven and earth before me God unroll,
Back to thy village still my spirit flies. <note>In the Sufi tradition, the "village" or the threshold of the beloved is considered more sacred than the celestial Paradise, as it represents the immediate presence of the Divine.</note>
And, Hafiz, at the door of **Kismet** <term>Kismet</term><note>From the Arabic 'qismah,' meaning 'portion' or 'lot.' It refers to the destiny or fate ordained by God for every individual.</note> lies
No just complaint—a mind like water clear,
A song that swells and dies upon the ear,
->These are enough for thee !<-

### VII

From the garden of Heaven a western breeze <note>Original: "Bād-e Sabā." This is the Zephyr or the morning breeze that acts as a messenger between the lover and the beloved in Persian poetry.</note>
Blows through the leaves of my garden of earth;
With a love like a **huri** <term>Huri</term><note>original: "Hūr." In Islamic tradition, these are beautiful, pure beings who inhabit Paradise. Hafiz uses the term here to describe the transcendent beauty of his earthly companion.</note> I’d take mine ease,
And wine ! bring me wine, the giver of mirth !
To-day the beggar may boast him a king,
His banqueting-hall is the ripening field,
And his tent the shadow that soft clouds fling.

A tale of April the meadows unfold—
Ah, foolish for future credit to slave,
And to leave the cash of the present untold ! <note>Hafiz frequently employs the metaphor of "cash" versus "credit." "Cash" (naqd) represents the spiritual joy found in the present moment, while "credit" (nisyah) refers to the promise of rewards in the afterlife often emphasized by dogmatic preachers.</note>
Build a fort with wine where thy heart may brave
The assault of the world; when thy fortress falls,
The relentless victor shall knead from thy dust
The bricks that repair its crumbling walls. <note>This is a striking "memento mori" (remember you must die) image. Hafiz reminds the reader that after death, our physical remains—our dust—become the very earth and bricks used to rebuild the world for the next generation.</note>

Trust not the word of that foe in the fight !
Shall the lamp of the synagogue lend its flame
To set thy monastic torches alight ? <note>Hafiz often mentions various religious institutions (the mosque, the tavern, the monastery, the synagogue) to suggest that the true seeker of God finds the "divine light" in all places, transcending the boundaries of formal religious identity.</note>
Drunken am I, yet place not my name

<summary>This page concludes a poem on spiritual contentment and begins another celebrating the fleeting beauty of spring and the wisdom of living in the present. Hafiz uses metaphors of wine and gardens to argue that immediate spiritual experience is more valuable than promised rewards in the hereafter.</summary>

## 75

In the Book of Doom, <note>original: "Daftar-e 'Asiyan." This refers to the record of human transgressions or the Preserved Tablet upon which every person's destiny is inscribed.</note> nor pass judgment on it ;
Who knows what the secret finger of Fate
Upon his own white forehead has writ !

And when the spirit of Hafiz has fled,
Follow his bier with a tribute of sighs ;
Though the ocean of sin has closed o'er his head,
He may find a place in God's Paradise.

### VIII

The rose has flushed red, the bud has burst,
And drunk with joy is the nightingale—
Hail, **Sufis** ! <term>Sufis</term><note>Mystics who seek a direct, personal experience of the Divine, often using metaphors of love and intoxication to describe spiritual ecstasy.</note> lovers of wine, all hail !
For wine is proclaimed to a world athirst.
Like a rock your repentance <note>original: "Tawba." The formal act of repenting for sins and returning to religious law.</note> seemed to you ;
Behold the marvel ! of what avail
Was your rock, for a goblet has cleft it in two !

Bring wine for the king and the slave at the gate !
Alike for all is the banquet spread,
And drunk and sober are warmed and fed.
When the feast is done and the night grows late,
And the second door of the tavern gapes wide, <note>The "tavern" in this tradition often represents the world or the place of spiritual initiation. The "second door" is a common metaphor for death—the exit from the tavern of life.</note>
The low and the mighty must bow the head
'Neath the archway of Life, to meet what . . . outside ?

Except thy road through affliction pass,
None may reach the halting-station of mirth ;
God's treaty : Am I not Lord of the earth ? <note>This is a direct reference to the "Covenant of Alast" from the Quran (7:172). Before the world began, God asked all souls, "Am I not your Lord?" (Alastu bi-rabbikum). This primordial "Yes" is considered the foundation of the human-divine relationship in Sufi thought.</note>
Man sealed with a sigh : Ah yes, alas !

**Divan** <term>Divan</term><note>A collected volume of poems by a single author, usually arranged alphabetically by the last letter of the rhyme.</note>, **Hafiz**, **Book of Doom**, **Fate**, **Sufis**, **tavern**, **nightingale**

## 76

Nor with *Is* nor *Is Not* <note>original: "Wujud" (Existence) and "'Adam" (Non-existence). In Sufi philosophy, these terms describe the paradox of the material world versus the ultimate reality of God.</note> let thy mind contend;
Rest assured all perfection of mortal birth
In the great *Is Not* at the last shall end.

For Assaf's <note>Assaf ibn Barkhiya was the legendary vizier to King Solomon. He represents the height of human wisdom and administrative power.</note> pomp, and the steeds of the wind,
And the speech of birds, down the wind have fled,
And he that was lord of them all is dead; <note>A reference to King Solomon (Sulayman), who in Islamic and Persian tradition was granted power over the winds and the ability to speak with animals.</note>
Of his mastery nothing remains behind.
Shoot not thy feathered arrow astray!
A bow-shot's length through the air it has sped,
And then . . . dropped down in the dusty way.

But to thee, oh *Hafiz*, to thee, oh *Tongue* <note>original: "Lisan al-Ghayb." This was Hafiz’s honorary title, meaning "The Tongue of the Hidden" or "The Voice of the Unseen," suggesting his poems were divinely inspired.</note>
That speaks through the mouth of the slender reed, <note>The "reed" refers to the *ney* (the flute) and the *qalam* (the reed pen), both central symbols in Persian art and mysticism for the soul's longing and the act of creation.</note>
What thanks to thee when thy verses speed
From lip to lip, and the song thou hast sung?

### IX

OH Cup-bearer, <term>Saqi</term><note>The server of wine. In Sufi poetry, the Saqi is often a metaphor for the spiritual guide or God, who pours the "wine" of divine love and wisdom into the heart of the seeker.</note> set my glass afire
With the light of wine! oh minstrel, sing:
The world fulfilleth my heart's desire!
Reflected within the goblet's ring
I see the glow of my Love's red cheek,
And scant of wit, ye who fail to seek
The pleasures that wine alone can bring!

Let not the blandishments be checked
That slender beauties lavish on me,
Until in the grace of the cypress <note>In Persian literature, the cypress tree is the standard metaphor for a tall, graceful, and perfectly proportioned beloved.</note> decked,
My Love shall come like a ruddy pine-tree

### 77

He cannot perish whose heart doth hold
The life love breathes <note>original: "Hargiz namirad an-ki dilash zinda shud ba 'ishq." This is one of the most famous lines in Persian literature, asserting that spiritual love grants a form of eternal life that transcends physical death.</note>—though my days are told,
In the Book of the World lives my constancy.

But when the Day of Reckoning <note>The Day of Judgment (Qiyamah). Hafiz often uses this setting to challenge the moral superiority of the orthodox religious establishment.</note> is here,
I fancy little will be the gain
That accrues to the Sheikh <term>Sheikh</term><note>A title for a religious elder or legal authority. In this context, the Sheikh represents formal, outward piety.</note> for his lawful cheer,
Or to me for the draught forbidden I drain. <note>The "forbidden draught" is wine. While prohibited in Islamic law, in the "Divan" it symbolizes the intoxicating, direct experience of God's love which the poet prefers over the dry ritualism of the scholar.</note>
The drunken eyes of my comrades shine,
And I too, stretching my hand to the wine,
On the neck of drunkenness loosen the rein.

Oh wind <note>The "Saba," or the morning breeze, which in Persian poetry acts as a messenger (messenger-wind) between the lover and the beloved.</note>, if thou passest the garden close
Of my heart’s dear master, carry for me
The message I send to him, wind that blows !

->“ Why hast thou thrust from thy memory<-
->My hapless name ? ” breathe low in his ear ;<-
->“ Knowest thou not that the day is near<-
->When nor thou nor any shall think on me ? ”<-

If with tears, oh *Hafiz*, thine eyes are wet,
Scatter them round thee like grain, and snare
The Bird of Joy when it comes to thy net.
As the tulip shrinks from the cold night air,
So shrank my heart and quailed in the shade;
Oh Song-bird Fortune, the toils are laid,
When shall thy bright wings lie pinioned there?

The heavens’ green sea and the bark therein,
The slender bark of the crescent moon,
Are lost in thy bounty’s radiant noon,
Vizir and pilgrim, *Kawameddin*! <note>Qiwam al-Din Hasan (died 1353), a powerful vizier in Shiraz and a generous patron to whom Hafiz dedicated several poems. It was a standard poetic convention to end a lyric (ghazal) with praise for a protector.</note>

POEMS FROM THE
## X

Singer, sweet Singer, fresh notes strew,
->Fresh and afresh and new and new ! <note>original: "Taza ba taza, nau ba nau." This iconic Persian refrain emphasizes the constant renewal of the soul's joy and the ever-changing nature of the mystical experience.</note><-
Heart-gladdening wine thy lips imbrue,
->Fresh and afresh and new and new !<-

Saki <term>Saki</term><note>The cup-bearer. In the tavern of the soul, the Saki is the figure—often a metaphor for a spiritual teacher or the Divine—who pours the wine of spiritual intoxication into the seeker's heart.</note>, thy radiant feet I hail ;
Flush with red wine the goblets pale,
Flush our pale cheeks to drunken hue,
->Fresh and afresh and new and new !<-

Then with thy love to toy with thee,
Rest thee, ah, rest ! where none can see ;
Seek thy delight, for kisses sue,
->Fresh and afresh and new and new !<-

Here round thy life the vine is twined ;
Drink ! for elsewhere what wine wilt find ?
Drink to her name, to hours that flew,
->Hours ever fresh and new and new !<-

She that has stolen my heart from me,
How does she wield her empery ? <note>Supreme power or absolute sovereignty. In Persian poetry, the "Beloved" is often described as a cruel but beautiful monarch who rules the lover's heart with total authority.</note>
Paints and adorns and scents her too,
->Fresh and afresh and new and new !<-

Wind of the dawn <note>The "Saba," or the gentle morning breeze, which serves as a messenger between the lover and the Beloved.</note> that passest by,
Swift to the street of my fairy <note>original: "Peri." A beautiful, supernatural being in Persian folklore. The poet uses this to describe the ethereal, almost divine beauty of the one he loves.</note> hie,
Whisper the tale of Hafiz <note>The pen name of Khwaja Shams-ud-Din Muhammad, meaning "one who has memorized the Quran." Hafiz traditionally signs his name in the final verse of his poems.</note> true,
->Fresh and afresh and new and new !<-

<summary>This page features Poem X, a celebrated ghazal known for its rhythmic refrain "Fresh and afresh and new and new," celebrating the joy of music, wine, and the Beloved. It captures the essence of the "ecstatic" style of Hafiz, where earthly pleasures serve as metaphors for spiritual renewal.</summary>

->DIVAN OF HAFIZ<-

### XI

MIRTH, Spring, to linger in a garden fair,
What more has earth to give? All ye that wait,
Where is the Cup-bearer <term>Cup-bearer</term><note>Original: "Saki." In Persian poetry, the Saki is more than a servant; they are a spiritual guide who pours the wine of divine wisdom or mystical intoxication for the seeker.</note>, the flagon where?
When pleasant hours slip from the hand of Fate <note>Original: "Qaza." This refers to the divine decree or the inescapable flow of time. Hafiz frequently advises his readers to seize the moment because the future is governed by a destiny we cannot control.</note>,
Reckon each hour as a certain gain;
Who seeks to know the end of mortal care
Shall question his experience in vain.

Thy fettered life hangs on a single thread—
Some comfort for thy present ills devise,
But those that time may bring thou shalt not dread.
Waters of Life <note>The "Aab-e-Hayat," a legendary fountain of immortality associated with the prophet Khidr. Here, Hafiz contrasts eternal legends with the immediate pleasure of the present.</note> and Irem’s Paradise <note>The "Garden of Iram," a legendary lost city of gardens mentioned in the Quran, symbolizing the peak of human luxury and the vanity of earthly splendor.</note>—
What meaning do our dreams and pomp convey,
Save that beside a mighty stream, wide-fed,
We sit and sing of wine and go our way!

The modest and the merry shall be seen
To boast their kinship with a single voice;
There are no differences to choose between,
Thou art but flattering thy soul with choice!
Who knows the Curtain’s secret? <note>Original: "Asrar-e-Parda." The "Veil" or "Curtain" is a central Sufi metaphor for the boundary between the material world and the divine reality. Hafiz suggests that the ultimate truth is hidden from human reason.</note> . . . Heaven is
mute!
And yet with Him who holds the Curtain, e’en
With Him, oh Braggart, thou would’st raise dis-
pute!

<summary>In this poem, Hafiz reflects on the "carpe diem" theme, urging the reader to enjoy the fleeting beauty of spring and wine rather than worrying about fate or religious dogmas. He emphasizes that the secrets of the divine "Curtain" are beyond human understanding, mocking those who claim to know the mind of God.</summary>

<keywords>Saki, Fate, Irem, Waters of Life, The Veil, Carpe Diem, Sufi Mysticism, Transience</keywords>

<meta>This page concludes Poem XI from the previous page, which examined the mysteries of fate and the importance of living in the present. It transitions into Poem XII, one of Hafiz's most famous "rendi" (spiritual rogue) poems, which questions the compatibility of outward religious piety with the inward path of mystical love.</meta>

->DIVAN OF HAFIZ<-

Although His thrall <note>Original: "Banda," meaning a servant or slave of God.</note> shall miss the road and err,
'Tis but to teach him wisdom through distress,
Else Pardon and Compassionate Mercy were
But empty syllables and meaningless.
The Zealot <note>Original: "Zahid," a term for a dry, outward-facing ascetic or religious legalist whom Hafiz often satirizes.</note> thirsts for draughts of Kausar's wine <note>Kausar is a sacred river in Paradise mentioned in the Quran. The Zealot waits for the afterlife to taste joy, while the poet seeks it now.</note>,
And Hafiz doth an earthly cup prefer—
But what, between the two, is God's design?

### XII

WHERE is my ruined life <note>The "ruined" life refers to the Sufi concept of "Kharabat," where one's social reputation is destroyed to find spiritual truth.</note>, and where the fame
->Of noble deeds?<-
Look on my long-drawn road, and whence it came,
->And where it leads!<-

Can drunkenness be linked to piety
->And good repute?<-
Where is the preacher's holy monody <note>A monody is a poem or song of mourning; here it refers to the somber, restrictive sermons of the orthodox clergy.</note>,
->Where is the lute?<-

From monkish cell and lying garb released,
->Oh heart of mine,<-
Where is the Tavern fane <note>A "fane" is a temple. Hafiz uses the "Tavern" as a metaphor for the place of spiritual initiation, contrasting it with the "monkish cell."</note>, the Tavern priest,
->Where is the wine?<-

Past days of meeting, let the memory
->Of you be sweet!<-
Where are those glances fled, and where for me
->Reproaches meet?<-

<summary>This page concludes a poem on divine mercy and begins a new lyric exploring the vast gulf between social respectability and the ecstatic "ruin" of the mystical path. Hafiz asks rhetorical questions to highlight the incompatibility of dry religious law with the vibrant, musical experience of divine love.</summary>

<keywords>Hafiz, Kausar, Tavern, Piety, Zealot, Zahid, Kharabat, Divine Mercy, Spiritual Intoxication, Persian Poetry</keywords>

->F<-

His friend’s <note>In Sufi poetry, the "Friend" (Dost) usually refers to God or the Spiritual Guide.</note> bright face warms not the enemy
->When love is done—<-
Where is the extinguished lamp that made night day,
->Where is the sun ?<-

Balm to mine eyes the dust, my head I bow
->Upon thy stair.<-
Where shall I go, where from thy presence ? thou
->Art everywhere.<- <note>This reflects the Sufi concept of "Wahdat al-Wujud" or the Unity of Existence—the idea that the Divine is manifest in all parts of the universe.</note>

->Danger lurks there !<- <note>In Persian literary tradition, the dimple in the chin is often described as a "well" (chah-e zanakhdan) into which the lover's heart falls and becomes trapped.</note>

->Such mad haste—where ?<-

To steadfastness and patience, friend, ask not

### XIII

LADY <note>The "Lady" or "Beloved" in the Divan is often an ambiguous figure, representing either a human object of desire or the Divine Presence itself.</note> that hast my heart within thy hand,
Thou heed’st me not ; and if thou turn thine ear
Unto the wise, thou shalt not understand—
Behold the fault is thine, our words were clear.
For all the tumult in my drunken brain <note>In the "Rendi" (spiritual rogue) tradition, "drunkenness" (Sokr) symbolizes a state of spiritual intoxication where the worldly ego is dissolved to make room for divine love.</note>
Praise God ! who trieth not His slave in vain ;
Nor this world nor the next shall make me fear !

# POEMS FROM THE [DIVAN OF HAFIZ]

My weary heart eternal silence keeps—
I know not who has slipped into my heart;
Though I am silent, one within me weeps.
My soul shall rend the painted veil <note>original: "Hijab" — in Sufi philosophy, the "veil" represents the ego or the physical world that hides the Divine Reality from the seeker.</note> apart.
Where art thou, Minstrel! <term>Minstrel: original: "Motreb" — one who performs spiritual music to induce a state of ecstasy (Sama) in the listeners.</term> touch thy saddest strings
Till clothed in music such as sorrow sings,
My mournful story from thy zither <note>original: "Qanun" — a traditional Middle Eastern stringed instrument played in the lap.</note> sweeps.

Lo, not at any time I lent mine ear
To hearken to the glories of the earth;
Only thy beauty to mine eyes was dear.
Sleep has forsaken me, and from the birth
Of night till day I weave bright dreams of thee;
Drunk with a hundred nights of revelry,
Where is the tavern <term>Tavern: original: "Kharabat" — literally "the ruins," used by Hafiz to describe a place of spiritual transformation where the "sober" hypocrites of the mosque do not venture.</term> that sets forth such cheer!

My heart, sad hermit, stains the cloister <note>original: "Sawma'ah" — the cell or prayer hall of an ascetic. Hafiz often contrasts the "wine" of love with the dry, often hypocritical, piety of the cloister.</note> floor
With drops of blood, the sweat of anguish dire;
Ah, wash me clean, and o'er my body pour
Love's generous wine! the worshippers of fire <note>original: "Atash-parastan" — referring to Zoroastrians (Magians). In Persian poetry, the "Magian" often represents the holder of ancient, esoteric wisdom that predates or transcends formal religious law.</note>
Have bowed them down and magnified my name,
For in my heart there burns a living flame,
Transpiercing Death's impenetrable door.

What instrument through last night's silence rang?
My life into his lay the minstrel wove,
And filled my brain with the sweet song he sang.
It was the proclamation of thy love
That shook the strings of Life's most secret lyre, <note>original: "Chang" — a Persian harp.</note>
And still my breast heaves with last night's desire,
For countless echoes from that music sprang.

**Vocabulary from the original:**
<term>Minstrel</term>: The bringer of spiritual joy.
<term>Zither</term>: The instrument of the soul’s lament.
<term>Tavern</term>: The site of ego-dissolution.
<term>Cloister</term>: The traditional religious dwelling.
<term>Lyre</term>: The harmony of existence.
<term>Flame</term>: The burning passion of Divine Love.
<term>Silence</term>: The internal state of the mystic.

<summary>This page concludes Poem XIII, focusing on the poet's internal spiritual landscape where he hears a "mysterious minstrel" and finds more truth in the "tavern" and the "fire-worshippers" than in traditional religious cloisters. It emphasizes the Sufi concept of divine intoxication and the tearing of the "veil" of worldly perception.</summary>

### XIV

<note>This poem is widely regarded as an elegy written by Hafiz upon the death of his young son. The imagery shifts from the typical romantic longing of his other works to a profound, personal grief.</note>

The nightingale <term>Nightingale: original: "Bolbol" — the traditional symbol of the passionate lover in Persian poetry.</term> with drops of his heart’s blood
Had nourished the red rose <note>original: "Gol" — while the rose represents the Beloved, here it likely signifies the poet's child who died young.</note>, then came a wind,
And catching at the boughs in envious mood,
A hundred thorns about his heart entwined.
Like to the parrot <note>In Persian literature, the parrot represents an eloquent poet or a soul that tastes the "sugar" of divine knowledge.</note> crunching sugar, good
Seemed the world to me who could not stay
The wind of Death <note>original: "Bad-e-ajal" — the cold wind of destiny that cuts life short.</note> that swept my hopes away.

Light of mine eyes and harvest of my heart,
And mine at least in changeless memory!
Ah, when he found it easy to depart,
He left the harder pilgrimage <note>The "pilgrimage" here refers to the long, difficult journey of remaining alive while grieving.</note> to me!
Oh Camel-driver <term>Camel-driver: original: "Saban" — a metaphor for Fate or Time, which leads the caravan of life toward the grave.</term>, though the cordage start,
For God’s sake help me lift my fallen load,
And Pity be my comrade of the road!

My face is seamed with dust, mine eyes are wet.
Of dust and tears the turquoise firmament <note>original: "Gonbad-e-firoozeh" — the blue vault of the sky, often depicted in Persian poetry as a spinning wheel of indifferent fate.</note>
Kneadeth the bricks for joy’s abode; and yet . . .
Alas, and weeping yet I make lament!
Because the moon her jealous glances set
Upon the bow-bent eyebrows of my moon <gloss>My moon: a common Persian metaphor for a beautiful person.</gloss>,
He sought a lodging in the grave—too soon!

<vocab>Hafiz, Divan, nightingale, Camel-driver, turquoise firmament, red rose, pilgrimage</vocab>

# POEMS FROM THE [DIVAN OF HAFIZ]

<page-num>84</page-num>

I had not castled <term>Castled: A defensive move in chess. Hafiz uses this metaphor to express his regret at failing to protect his child from the "checkmate" of Death.</term>, and the time is gone.
What shall I play? Upon the chequered floor
Of Night and Day, Death won the game—forlorn
And careless now, Hafiz can lose no more.

### XV

<note>This poem is a "Ghazal," a short lyric poem. It is a plea for the return of the Beloved (God or a spiritual guide), whose absence has left the poet in a state of spiritual death and darkness.</note>

RETURN! that to a heart wounded full sore
Valiance and strength may enter in; return!
And Life shall pause at the deserted door,
The cold dead body breathe again and burn.
Oh come! and touch mine eyes, of thy sweet grace,
For I am blind to all but to thy face.
Open the gates and bid me see once more!

Like to a cruel Ethiopian band <term>Ethiopian band: original: "Lashkar-e-Zang" — in medieval Persian poetry, the "Zangi" (people of East Africa) were associated with the color black and used as a metaphor for the darkness of night or the "army" of sorrow.</term>,
Sorrow despoiled the kingdom of my heart—
Return! glad Lord of Rome <term>Lord of Rome: original: "Soltan-e-Rum" — referring to the Eastern Roman or Byzantine Empire. In poetic contrast to the "Ethiopian" night, "Rome" represents the light of the sun, beauty, and the fair-complexioned beloved.</term>, and free the land;
Before thine arms the foe shall break and part.
See now, I hold a mirror to mine eyes,
And nought but thy reflection therein lies;
The glass speaks truth to them that understand.

Night is with child <note>original: "Shab abestan ast" — a famous Persian proverb meaning "The night is pregnant with what the dawn will bring." It suggests that even in the darkest times, an unpredictable change is coming.</note>, hast thou not heard men say?
"Night is with child! what will she bring to birth?"
I sit and ask the stars when thou'rt away.
Oh come! and when the nightingale <term>Nightingale: original: "Bolbol" — the poet-lover who sings to the rose.</term> of mirth
Pipes in the Spring-awakened garden ground,
In Hafiz' heart shall ring a sweeter sound,
Diviner nightingales attune their lay.

<summary>This page concludes a tragic elegy on the death of Hafiz's son using chess metaphors and begins a new poem of spiritual longing that contrasts the darkness of sorrow with the light of the Beloved's return.</summary>
<keywords>chess metaphor, grief, Rumi and Zangi, light vs darkness, night is with child, spiritual rebirth, nightingale</keywords>

### XVI

<note>This poem is a meditation on the Sufi concept of "Fana" or the passing away of the material world. Hafiz repeatedly uses the refrain "all things are nought" (original: *hich*) to contrast the emptiness of worldly ambition with the fullness of Love.</note>

What is wrought in the forge of the living and life—
All things are nought! Ho! fill me the bowl,
For nought is the gear of the world and the strife!
One passion has quickened the heart and the soul,
The Beloved’s presence alone they have sought—
Love at least exists; yet if Love were not,
Heart and soul would sink to the common lot—
        All things are nought!

Like an empty cup is the fate of each,
That each must fill from Life’s mighty flood;
Nought thy toil, though to Paradise gate thou reach,
If Another has filled up thy cup with blood;
Neither shade from the sweet-fruited trees could be 
        bought
By thy praying—oh <term>Cypress of Truth: original: "Saru-ye-Rast" — the cypress tree is a common Persian metaphor for the Beloved because of its grace, height, and "freedom" (it bears no fruit to weigh it down).</term>, dost not see
That <term>Sidreh and Tuba: In Islamic tradition, these are the celestial trees of Paradise. Hafiz suggests that even these heavenly rewards are "nought" compared to the presence of the True Beloved.</term> were nought, and to thee
        All then were nought!

The span of thy life is as five little days,
<note>original: "Panj ruzi" — a common Persian idiom for the fleeting nature of human existence.</note>
Brief hours and swift in this halting-place;
Rest softly, ah rest! while the Shadow delays,
For Time’s self is nought and the dial’s face.
On the lip of Oblivion we linger, and short
Is the way from the Lip to the Mouth where we pass—
While the moment is thine, fill, oh <term>Saki: the wine-pourer or cup-bearer. In Sufi poetry, the Saki is often a metaphor for the spiritual master who provides the "wine" of divine wisdom.</term>, the glass
        Ere all is nought!

<summary>A contemplative poem emphasizing the transience of the material world and the vanity of religious rewards compared to the immediate experience of Love.</summary>
<keywords>Hafiz, Sufism, transience, Paradise, Saki, Cypress, vanity, Divine Love</keywords>

<meta>This page concludes Poem XVI and begins Poem XVII. It continues the themes of the "nothingness" of the material world and the hypocrisy of outward religious judgment, which were introduced on page 85.</meta>

<header>POEMS FROM THE DIVAN</header>

Consider the rose that breaks into flower,
Neither repines though she fade and die—
The powers of the world endure for an hour,
But nought shall remain of their majesty.
Be not too sure of your crown, you who thought
That virtue was easy and recompense yours;
From the monastery to the wine-tavern doors
<note>The "monastery" (khanaqah) represents formal, organized religion, while the "wine-tavern" (kharabat) represents the place of spiritual ruin where the ego is destroyed by Divine Love.</note>
->The way is nought !<-

What though I, too, have tasted the salt of my tears,
Though I, too, have burnt in the fires of grief,
Shall I cry aloud to unheeding ears?
Mourn and be silent! nought brings relief.
Thou, Hafiz, art praised for the songs thou hast wrought,
But bearing a stained or an honoured name,
The lovers of wine shall make light of thy fame—
->All things are nought !<-

### XVII

LAY not reproach at the drunkard’s door
Oh Fanatic, thou that art pure of soul;
<note>original: *Zahid*. In Hafiz’s poetry, the "Fanatic" or "Ascetic" is a recurring character who practices outward piety and judges others, but lacks the inner fire of true Love.</note>
Not thine on the page of life to enrol
The faults of others ! Or less or more
I have swerved from my path—keep thou to thine own !
For every man when he reaches the goal
Shall reap the harvest his hands have sown.

Leave me the hope of a former grace—
Till the curtain is lifted none can tell
<note>The "lifting of the curtain" refers to the moment of death or spiritual revelation (*kashf*), when the true nature of a person's soul is revealed behind the "veil" of the physical world.</note>
Whether in Heaven or deepest Hell,
Fair or vile, shall appear his face.

<vocab>Hafiz, monastery, wine-tavern, Fanatic, harvest, transience, virtue, recompense</vocab>

<summary>Hafiz concludes a meditation on the vanity of worldly status and begins a new poem defending the "drunkard" (the sincere mystic) against the judgmental "fanatic" (the hypocritical ascetic).</summary>
<keywords>Hafiz, Sufism, transience, Zahid, spiritual hypocrisy, Divine Love, Fana, afterlife</keywords>

--- Page 91 ---
<meta>This page completes Poem XVII, which began on page 86. It continues the poet’s defense of his spiritual path—often characterized by "drunkenness" and "love"—against the judgment of the dry, formalist ascetic (the Zahid).</meta>

<page-num>87</page-num>
<header>DIVAN OF HAFIZ</header>

Alike the drunk and the strict of fare
<note>Hafiz refers to the *rind* (the clever libertine or "drunkard") and the *parhizgar* (the abstinent or pious man). In Sufi thought, both are searching for the same Divine Truth, though their paths look different.</note>
For his mistress yearns—in the mosque Love doth
dwell
And the church, for his lodging is everywhere.
<note>original: *kenisht* (synagogue/church). Hafiz frequently uses the image of the church or temple to show that God’s presence transcends the boundaries of formal Islam.</note>

If without the house of devotion I stand,
I am not the first to throw wide the door;
My father opened it long before,
<note>This is a reference to the "Fall of Man." Hafiz identifies with his "father" (Adam), suggesting that if he is an exile from formal holiness, it is a legacy inherited from the first human who was cast out of Paradise.</note>
The eternal Paradise slipped from his hand.
All you that misconstrue my words' intent,
I lie on the bricks of the tavern floor,
<note>The "tavern" (*kharabat*) is the Sufi's place of spiritual ruin where the ego is destroyed to make room for Divine Love.</note>
And a brick shall serve me for argument.

Heaven's garden future treasures may yield—
Ah, make the most of earth's treasury!
The flickering shade of the willow-tree,
And the grass-grown lip of the fruitful field.
Trust not in deeds—the Eternal Day
Shall reveal the Creator's sentence on thee;
But till then, what His finger has writ, who can
say.
<note>Hafiz cautions against "deeds" or outward religious performance. He suggests that salvation depends on God's grace and the "Eternal Decree," not on the visible actions of the self-righteous.</note>

Bring the cup in thine hand to the Judgment-seat;
Thou shalt rise, oh Hafiz, to Heaven's gate
From the tavern where thou hast tarried late.
And if thou hast worshipped wine, thou shalt meet
The reward that the Faithful attain;
If such thy life, then fear not thy fate,
Thou shalt not have lived and worshipped in vain!
<note>In the final stanza (the *maqta*), the poet addresses himself by name. He concludes that his "worship of wine"—a metaphor for total devotion to Divine Love—is as valid as the formal prayers of the "Faithful" (*Mu'min*).</note>

<vocab>Hafiz, Divan, Sufism, Tavern, Paradise, Judgment-seat, Eternal Day</vocab>

<summary>This page concludes Poem XVII, asserting that Divine Love exists both in the mosque and the church, and that the poet’s life of "spiritual drunkenness" is a valid path to salvation. It emphasizes that human judgment is fallible compared to the ultimate sentence of the Creator.</summary>
<keywords>Adam, tavern (kharabat), Divine Love, religious hypocrisy, wine as metaphor, fate, mosque vs. church</keywords>

### XVIII

Slaves of thy shining eyes are even those
<note>In Persian poetry, the "shining eyes" represent the power and attraction of the Divine Beloved, to whom even worldly rulers are subject.</note>
That diadems of might and empire bear;
Drunk with the wine that from thy red lip flows,
Are they that e’en the grape’s delight forswear.
Drift, like the wind across a violet bed,
Before thy many lovers, weeping low,
And clad like violets in blue robes of woe,
<note>The violet is a symbol of humility and mourning in Persian literature because of its dark color and the way its head hangs low, as if in grief or prayer.</note>
Who feel thy wind-blown hair and bow the head.

Thy messenger the breath of dawn, and mine
<note>The "breath of dawn" (the morning breeze, or *Saba*) is the traditional courier of secrets between the lover and the Beloved in Sufi metaphor.</note>
A stream of tears, since lover and beloved
Keep not their secret; through my verses shine,
Though other lays my flower’s grace have proved
And countless nightingales have sung thy praise.
<note>The "nightingale" is the classic archetype of the lover singing to the "rose" (the Beloved). Hafiz acknowledges that while many have praised God, his own poetry seeks a unique connection.</note>
When veiled beneath thy curls thou passest, see,
To right and leftward those that welcome thee
Have bartered peace and rest on thee to gaze!

But thou that knowest God by heart, away!
<note>The poet likely addresses the *Zahid* (the dry ascetic). The phrase "knowest God by heart" may also refer to the title *Hafiz* itself—one who has memorized the Quran—suggesting that rote memorization is nothing without the experience of Love.</note>
Wine-drunk, love-drunk, we inherit Paradise,
His mercy is for sinners; hence and pray
Where wine thy cheek red as red <term>erghwan</term> dyes,
<note>original: *erghwan*. The Judas tree, known for its brilliant red-purple flowers; it is a standard metaphor for the color of wine or a blushing face.</note>
And leave the cell to faces sinister.
Oh <term>Khizr</term>, whose happy feet bathed in life’s fount,
<note>original: *Khizr*. An immortal prophet and guide in Islamic tradition who drank from the "Water of Life." He is often invoked to help travelers on a difficult spiritual or physical journey.</note>
Help one who toils afoot—the horsemen mount
And hasten on their way; I scarce can stir.

<vocab>erghwan, Khizr, nightingales, Paradise</vocab>

<summary>In this poem, Hafiz celebrates the intoxicating power of Divine Love, which humbles even kings and eclipses the joy of worldly wine. He rebukes the judgmental ascetic, suggesting that God's mercy is intended for the "sinful" lovers rather than the self-righteous.</summary>
<keywords>Sufism, Divine Love, Khizr, asceticism vs. ecstasy, spiritual wine, violet imagery</keywords>

<meta>This page concludes Poem XVIII and begins Poem XIX. It continues the previous page’s exploration of spiritual devotion, moving from the theme of humble submission to the joyful arrival of divine messages and the metaphorical "Spring" of the soul.</meta>

<page-type>text</page-type>
<page-num>89</page-num>
<header>DIVAN OF HAFIZ</header>
<note>A "Divan" is a collected volume of a poet's work, specifically their shorter lyrical poems known as ghazals.</note>

Ah, loose me not! ah, set not Hafiz free
From out the bondage of thy gleaming hair!
Safe only those, safe, and at liberty,
That fast enchainèd in thy linked ringlets are.
<note>In Sufi imagery, being "bound" by the hair of the Beloved symbolizes the soul's total preoccupation with God. This "bondage" is paradoxically seen as the only true freedom from the distractions of the material world.</note>
But from the image of his dusty cheek
Learn this from Hafiz: proudest heads shall bend,
And dwellers on the threshold of a friend
Be crownèd with the dust that crowns the meek.
<note>To be "covered in dust" at the doorstep of the Beloved is a sign of ultimate humility and spiritual attainment.</note>

### XIX

What drunkenness is this that brings me hope—
Who was the <term>Cup-bearer</term>, and whence the wine?
<note>The "Cup-bearer" (Saki) is the spiritual guide or the Divine itself who pours the "wine" of spiritual ecstasy and hidden knowledge into the heart of the seeker.</note>
That minstrel singing with full voice divine,
What lay was his? for ’mid the woven rope
Of song, he brought word from my Friend to me
<note>In the poetry of Hafiz, "The Friend" is a common term for God or the Divine Beloved.</note>
->Set to his melody.<-

The wind itself bore joy to Solomon;
The <term>Lapwing</term> flew from Sheba’s garden close,
<note>This refers to the Quranic and Biblical accounts of the Prophet Solomon. The Lapwing (or Hoopoe) served as a messenger between Solomon and Bilqis, the Queen of Sheba, symbolizing the transmission of divine secrets.</note>
Bringing good tidings of its queen and rose.
Take thou the cup and go where meadows span
The plain, whither the bird with tuneful throat
->Has brought Spring’s sweeter note.<-

Welcome, oh rose, and full-blown eglantine!
The violets their scented gladness fling,
Jasmin breathes purity—art sorrowing
Like an unopened bud, oh heart of mine?
The wind of dawn that sets closed blossoms free
<note>The "wind of dawn" (*Saba*) is a recurring character in Persian poetry, acting as a gentle messenger that awakens the soul just as it opens the flowers.</note>
->Brings its warm airs to thee.<-

<vocab>Hafiz: the pen name of the poet, meaning "one who has memorized the Quran." Solomon: the prophet-king known for his wisdom and ability to speak with birds. Sheba: the ancient kingdom of Queen Bilqis. Cup-bearer: the provider of spiritual wine. Lapwing: the Hoopoe, a mystical messenger. Eglantine: a species of wild rose. Jasmin: a fragrant flower representing purity. Drunkenness: spiritual ecstasy. Minstrel: a musician or poet.</vocab>

<summary>This page concludes a poem on the humility required to approach the Divine and begins a new poem celebrating the arrival of spiritual "Spring." Hafiz uses the stories of Solomon and the imagery of blooming gardens to describe the joy of receiving a message from the Creator.</summary>
<keywords>Hafiz, Sufism, Solomon and Sheba, divine love, spiritual intoxication, Hoopoe bird, Persian garden imagery, humility</keywords>

# POEMS FROM THE DIVAN OF HAFIZ
<note>A "Divan" is a collection of poems by a single author. These specific verses are ghazals, a form of lyrical poetry focusing on themes of love and divine mystical union.</note>

<term>Saki</term>, thy kiss shall still my bitter cry!
<note>original: "Sāqī". The Saki is the cup-bearer who pours wine, serving as a metaphor for the spiritual master or the Divine itself who provides the "wine" of spiritual ecstasy.</note>
Lift up your grief-bowed heads, all ye that weep,
The Healer brings joy's wine-cup—oh, drink deep!
Disciple of the <term>Tavern-priest</term> am I;
<note>original: "Pir-e Moghan" or "Elder of the Magians." This figure represents the enlightened spiritual master who operates outside the rigid structures of formal religion, often found in the metaphorical "tavern" of spiritual ruin.</note>
The pious <term>Sheikh</term> may promise future bliss,
<note>A Sheikh is a formal religious leader or elder. In Hafiz’s work, the Sheikh often represents outward piety and legalism, contrasted with the inward, experiential love of the poet.</note>
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;He brings me where joy is.

The greedy glances of a <term>Tartar</term> horde
<note>This refers to the Mongol and Timurid invasions that Hafiz witnessed during the 14th century. Here, the "Tartar horde" is used as a metaphor for the relentless and cruel nature of fate or worldly suffering.</note>
To me seemed kind—my foeman spared me not
Though one poor robe was all that I had got.
But Heaven served Hafiz, as a slave his lord,
And when he fled through regions desolate,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Heaven brought him to thy gate.

->XX<-

### XX

From out the street of <insert>the Friend</insert>,
<note>The OCR reads "So-and-So," likely a placeholder for "Kuy-i Dust," the Street of the Friend/Beloved, a common destination for the seeker's soul.</note>
Oh wind, bring perfumes sweet to me!
For I am sick and pale with woe;
Oh bring me rest from misery!
The dust that lies before her door,
Love's long desired <term>elixir</term>, pour
<note>original: "al-iksir." In alchemy, the elixir turns base metals into gold. Here, the dust from the Beloved's threshold is the alchemical substance that transforms the lover's suffering heart into spiritual gold.</note>
Upon this wasted heart of mine—
Bring me a promise and a sign!

Between the ambush of mine eyes
And my heart's fort there's enmity—
Her eye-brow's bow, the dart that flies,
Beneath her lashes, bring to me!
<note>Hafiz frequently employs military and archery metaphors: the beloved's curved eyebrows are the "bow," and the long eyelashes are the "arrows" or "darts" that pierce the lover's heart-fortress.</note>

<header>DIVAN OF HAFIZ</header>

Sorrow and absence, and glances grown cold,
Have made me, before my time, weary and old;
A wine-cup from the hand of Youth, I pray,
Bring me for pity and for mercy's sake today!

Then shall all those who lack faith come to taste
A draught or two of that same sacred wine;
But if they find it bitter, oh make haste!
And let the cup of flowing joy be mine.
<term>Saki</term>, seize this day! Do not wait
<note>original: "Sāqī". The cup-bearer in Sufi poetry represents the spiritual guide or the Divine Presence who serves the "wine" of mystical knowledge.</note>
Until tomorrow—or from the hand of Fate
Some passport to true happiness,
Some written guarantee of peace, bring to me!

My heart cast off its heavy veil of woe,
Finding its comfort in Hafiz's melody:
From out the street of the <term>Beloved</term>,
<note>The original English text uses "So-and-So" to translate a phrase usually referring to the specific neighborhood or "kuy" of the beloved, representing the goal of the spiritual journey.</note>
Oh wandering wind, bring those sweet perfumes to me!

### XXI

Not all the sum of earthly happiness
Is worth the price of one moment's grief or pain,
And if I trade for wine my <term>dervish dress</term>,
<note>original: "Khirqa." This is the patched cloak worn by Sufi mystics. To "sell it for wine" is a radical metaphor for abandoning outward religious identity in favor of inward spiritual intoxication.</note>
Worth more than what I sell is the joy I gain!
The land where my Lady dwells holds me
In golden chains; otherwise <term>Fars</term> were but a barren soil,
<note>Fars is the province in southwestern Iran where Hafiz's beloved city, Shiraz, is located. The poet often claims that the region's beauty is derived entirely from the presence of the Beloved.</note>
Not worth a journey over land and sea,
->Not worth the toil!<-

Down in the quarter where they sell red wine,
My <term>holy carpet</term> scarce would fetch a cup—
<note>original: "Sajjāda." The prayer mat. In Sufi poetry, offering one's prayer mat as collateral for wine is a provocative metaphor for discarding outward, performative religious piety in exchange for a single drop of genuine spiritual experience.</note>
How brave a pledge of piety is mine,
Which is not worth a goblet foaming up!
Mine enemy heaped scorn on me and said:
“Forth from the <term>tavern gate</term>!” Why am I thrust
<note>original: "Kharaabāt." While literally meaning "ruins," in Hafiz’s lexicon, the tavern is a sacred space of spiritual annihilation where the ego is dissolved by divine love.</note>
From off the threshold? is my fallen head
->Not worth the dust?<-

Wash white that travel-stained sad robe of thine!
Where word and deed alike one colour bear,
The grape’s fair purple garment shall outshine
Thy many-coloured rags and tattered gear.
Full easy seemed the sorrow of the sea
Lightened by hope of gain—hope flew too fast!
A hundred pearls were poor indemnity,
->Not worth the blast.<-

The <term>Sultan’s crown</term>, with priceless jewels set,
<note>Hafiz frequently contrasts the visible, material power of the "Sultan" with the invisible, spiritual sovereignty of the "dervish."</note>
Encircles fear of death and constant dread;
It is a head-dress much desired—and yet
Art sure ’tis worth the danger to the head?
’Twere best for thee to hide thy face from those
That long for thee; the <term>Conqueror’s</term> reward
Is never worth the army’s long-drawn woes,
->Worth fire and sword.<-

Ah, seek the treasure of a mind at rest
And store it in the <term>treasury of Ease</term>;
<note>original: "Ganj-i-qanā'at." Literally the "treasure of contentment." This reflects the Sufi ideal of finding wealth in poverty and peace in detachment from the world.</note>
Not worth a loyal heart, a tranquil breast,
Were all the riches of thy lands and seas!

Ah, scorn, like <term>Hafiz</term>, the delights of earth,
<note>Hafiz is the pen name of the 14th-century Persian poet Shams-ud-Din Muhammad. The name literally means "one who has memorized the Quran."</note>
Ask not one grain of favour from the base,
Two hundred sacks of jewels were not worth
->Thy soul's disgrace !<-

### XXII

The rose is not fair without the <term>beloved’s face</term>,
<note>In Persian mystical poetry, the "Beloved" is often an ambiguous figure, representing both an earthly lover and the Divine. Without this central focus, the beauties of nature are considered hollow.</note>
Nor merry the Spring without the sweet laughter of wine;
<note>Wine is a recurring metaphor in Sufi literature for spiritual intoxication and the joy of divine revelation.</note>
The path through the fields, and winds from a flower-strewn place,
Without her bright cheek, which glows like a <term>tulip</term> fine,
<note>The tulip, with its red petals and dark "branded" center, frequently symbolizes the lover's heart, wounded by passion.</note>
Nor winds softly blowing, fields deep in corn, are fair.

And lips like to sugar, grace like a flower that sways,
Are nought without kisses many and dalliance sweet;
If thousands of voices sang not the rose’s praise,
The joy of the <term>cypress</term> her opening bud to greet,
<note>The cypress tree is a classic Persian metaphor for the beloved’s tall, graceful, and upright stature.</note>
Nor dancing of boughs nor blossoming rose were fair.

Though limned by most skilful fingers, no pictures please
Unless the beloved’s image is drawn therein;
The garden and flowers, and hair flowing loose on the breeze,
Unless to my Lady’s side I may strive and win,
Nor garden, nor flowers, nor loose flying curls are fair.

<summary>This page concludes a poem on the superiority of spiritual peace over worldly wealth and begins a new lyric celebrating the Beloved as the essential source of all beauty in nature and art.</summary>

This page concludes that thought with a metaphor of a wedding feast before transitioning into a somber elegy, likely mourning the death of the poet's wife.</meta>

<header>POEMS FROM THE</header>

Have you seen at a <term>marriage-feast</term>, when the joy runs high,
<note>In Persian culture, it was traditional to scatter small gold coins (nissar) over the bride and groom or guests during celebrations.</note>
The revellers scatter gold with a careless hand?
The gold of your heart, oh <term>Hafiz</term>, lies ignored,
<note>Hafiz is the pen name of the poet; he often includes his name in the final stanza of a poem as a "signature" or "takhallus."</note>
Not worthy of your love to be tossed by a drunken crowd
At the feet of her who is more beautiful than all beauty.

### XXIII

My lady, who changed this house of mine
Into a heaven while she lived within it,
An <term>angel’s grace</term> divine wrapped her 
From head to foot; she was pure and spotless of sin;
Her face was fair as the moon, and she was wise;
Her eyes, those masters of the kind and tender look,
Shone with an overflowing loveliness.

Then my heart said: "Here I will take my rest!"
This city breathes her love in every corner.
But she was destined for a distant destination,
Alas! My poor heart did not know it!
The influence of some <term>cold malignant star</term>
<note>An astrological reference to an unlucky alignment, often associated with the planet Saturn, believed to bring about death or misfortune.</note>
Has forced my hand to let go of hers; far and alone
She travels—she who once lay against my breast.

Not only did she lift the veil from my heart
And reveal its deepest secrets, but her grace
Drew back the curtain from the pale <term>mansions of Heaven</term>,
<note>This refers to the "eternal dwelling place" or the afterlife, suggesting the lady has passed away.</note>
And gave her an eternal home there.
The flower-strewn riverbank and the lovely meadows,
Even the rose herself, were only fleeting treasures;
Regret and Winter now follow in her wake.

<summary>This page concludes a poem on the worthlessness of worldly beauty without love and begins a poignant elegy, generally believed to be Hafiz’s lament for the death of his wife.</summary>

<header>DIVAN OF HAFIZ</header>

Dear were the days that passed away with my friend—
<note>Scholars often identify the "friend" in this poem as Hafiz’s wife, whose death left him in profound grief.</note>
Ah, what is left of life, now that she is dead?
I spend my time without wisdom or purpose!
The nightingale sheds his own life’s blood,
<note>The "nightingale and the rose" is a central metaphor in Persian literature; the bird represents the lover who sings until his heart breaks for the beautiful but indifferent rose.</note>
When the morning wind’s kisses 
Unveil the rose’s splendor—with his torn 
And jealous breast, he dyes her petals red.

Yet pardon her, oh Heart, for you were poor—
A humble <term>dervish</term> on the dusty path;
<note>original: "darvish" — a seeker of truth who often chooses a life of poverty and spiritual discipline.</note>
But the crown of an empire sat upon her brow,
And she ruled over the realms of beauty.
Yet all the joy that Hafiz’s hand could hold
Lay in the beads that he counted morning and evening,
<note>This refers to the use of prayer beads (tasbih) to recite the names of God, a practice known as dhikr.</note>
Worn down by praising God; and look! He holds them still.

### XXIV

No one is filled with a madness like mine
In all the <term>taverns</term>! My soiled robe lies here,
<note>In Sufi poetry, the "tavern" is a metaphor for a place of spiritual ecstasy, and "wine" represents the love of God which intoxicates the soul.</note>
And there is my neglected book; I have traded both for wine.
My heart is thick with dust, though it should be clear—
A mirror to reflect the <term>Great King’s face</term>;
<note>The "Great King" refers to God. In Persian mysticism, the heart is a mirror that must be polished of worldly desires to see the Divine.</note>
Oh God, send one ray of light from Your dwelling-place
To pierce my darkness and draw me near to You.

From my eyes down to the hem of my garment,
A river of tears flows; perhaps my <term>cypress-tree</term>
<note>The cypress is a symbol of the Beloved’s graceful stature and also represents the "Perfect Man" or spiritual freedom.</note>
May grow tall beside that stream,
Watering her roots with my tears. Ah, bring to me...

## 96

The wine vessel! since my Love's cheek is hid,
A flood of grief comes from my heart unbid,
And turns mine eyes into a bitter sea!
<note>Hafiz frequently uses the image of "the sea" to describe the overwhelming nature of divine love or profound sorrow.</note>

Nay, by the hand that sells me wine, I vow
No more the brimming cup shall touch my lips,
Until my mistress with her radiant brow
Adorns my feast—until Love's secret slips
From her, as from the candle's tongue of flame,
Though I, the <term>singèd moth</term>, for very shame,
<note>original: "parvaneh" — the moth. In Sufi literature, the moth is the ultimate symbol of the lover who is so drawn to the light (the Divine) that he willingly consumes himself in the flame.</note>
Dare not extol Love's light without eclipse.

Red wine I worship, and I worship her!—
Speak not to me of anything beside,
For nought but these on earth or heaven I care.
What though the proud <term>narcissus</term> flowers defied
<note>In Persian poetry, the narcissus (nargis) is a standard metaphor for the eyes of the beloved, often described as "languid" or "intoxicated."</note>
Thy shining eyes to prove themselves more
->bright,<-
Yet heed them not! those that are clear of sight
Follow not them to whom all light's denied.

Before the tavern door a <term>Christian</term> sang
<note>In 14th-century Shiraz, wine was officially forbidden to Muslims. Consequently, taverns were often located in the Christian or Jewish quarters, and the "wine-seller" is frequently depicted as a non-Muslim figure who provides the "spiritual wine" of truth.</note>
To sound of pipe and drum, what time the earth
Awaited the white dawn, and gaily rang
Upon mine ear those harbingers of mirth:
> " If the True Faith be such as thou dost say,
> Alas! my <term>Hafiz</term>, that this sweet To-day
<note>The poet’s pen-name, meaning "one who has memorized the Quran." He often uses his own name in the final stanzas of his poems to address himself.</note>
> Should bring unknown To-morrow to the birth! "

### XXV

The days of absence and the bitter nights
Of separation, all are at an end!
<note>original: "hijran" — the state of being separated from the Beloved (God or a lover), a central theme in Persian mystical poetry.</note>
Where is the influence of the star that blights
My hope? The omen answers: At an end!
<note>Hafiz refers here to *fal*, the practice of seeking an omen or divination, often by opening the Quran or a book of poetry at random.</note>
Autumn’s abundance, creeping Autumn’s mirth,
Are ended and forgot when o’er the earth
The wind of Spring with soft warm feet doth wend.

The Day of Hope, hid beneath Sorrow’s veil,
Has shown its face—ah, cry that all may hear:
Come forth! the powers of night no more prevail!
Praise be to God, now that the rose is near
With long-desired and flaming coronet,
The cruel stinging thorns all men forget,
The wind of Winter ends its proud career.

The long confusion of the nights that were,
Anguish that dwelt within my heart, is o’er;
’Neath the protection of my lady’s hair
Grief nor disquiet come to me no more.
<note>In Sufi symbolism, the "tresses" or "hair" of the Beloved represent the multiplicity of the material world which, though distracting, ultimately provides a "shade" or protection for the lover.</note>
What though her curls wrought all my misery,
My lady’s gracious face can comfort me,
And at the end give what I sorrow for.

Light-hearted to the tavern let me go,
<note>The tavern (<term>kharabat</term>) is the place where the ego is destroyed and spiritual wine is served to the "drunkards" of God.</note>
Where laughs the pipe, the merry cymbals kiss;
Under the history of all my woe,
My mistress sets her hand and writes: Finis.
<note>original: "tamam" — ended or completed. The "mistress" here acts as the ultimate authority who validates the lover's suffering and brings it to a close.</note>

<summary>Poem XXV celebrates the transition from the "winter" of spiritual separation to the "spring" of union, concluding with the poet finding peace in the symbolic tavern.</summary>
<keywords>separation (hijran), spring, tavern (kharabat), omen (fal), Sufi symbolism, hair/tresses</keywords>

--- Page 102 ---
<meta>This page concludes Poem XXV, which celebrates the end of a period of spiritual longing and separation, and begins Poem XXVI. This new poem transitions into a classic Persian "memento mori" theme—meditating on the transience of life, the futility of trying to outsmart fate with science (astronomy), and the recurring image of the dust of ancient kings becoming the clay of a wine cup.</meta>

<page-num>98</page-num>
<header>POEMS FROM THE DIVAN OF HAFIZ</header>

Oh, linger not, nor trust the inconstant days
That promised: Where thou art thy lady stays—
The tale of separation ends with this!

Joy’s certain path, oh <term>Saki</term> <note>The Saki is the cupbearer, but in Sufi poetry, they represent the spiritual guide or the Divine who pours the "wine" of spiritual realization into the heart of the seeker.</note>, thou hast shown—
Long may thy cup be full, thy days be fair!
Trouble and sickness from my breast have flown,
Order and health thy wisdom marshals there.
Not one that numbered Hafiz’ name among
The great—unnumbered were his tears, unsung;
Praise him that sets an end to endless care!

### XXVI

The secret draught of wine and love repressed
Are joys foundationless—then come whate’er
May come, slave to the grape I stand confessed!
Unloose, oh friend, the knot of thy heart’s care,
Despite the warning that the Heavens reveal!
For all his thought, never astronomer
That loosed the knot of Fate <note>original: "qada" — the divine decree or destiny that cannot be altered by human effort or scientific calculation.</note> those Heavens conceal!

Not all the changes that thy days unfold
Shall rouse thy wonder; Time’s revolving sphere
Over a thousand lives like thine has rolled.
That cup within thy fingers, dost not hear
The voices of dead kings speak through the clay?
Kobad, Bahman, Djemshid, <note>These are legendary kings of ancient Persia. Djemshid (Jamshid) is particularly famous for his "world-revealing cup" which allowed him to see everything in the universe.</note> their dust is here,
“Gently upon me set thy lips!” they say.

<vocab>Hafiz, Saki, Kobad, Bahman, Djemshid, wine, love, Fate, Heavens, astronomer</vocab>

<summary>This page concludes a poem of spiritual restoration and begins a meditation on the vanity of worldly power, reminding the reader that even the greatest Persian kings have returned to the dust from which wine cups are made.</summary>

## DIVAN OF HAFIZ

What man can tell where Kaus and Kai <note>original: "Kay Kaus" and "Kay Khosrow"—legendary kings of the Kayanian dynasty from the Persian national epic, the *Shahnameh*.</note> have
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;gone?
Who knows where even now the restless wind
Scatters the dust of Djem's <note>original: "Jamshid," a mythical king of ancient Iran. His "imperial throne" refers to Persepolis, traditionally called the "Throne of Jamshid."</note> imperial throne?
And where the tulip, following close behind
The feet of Spring, her scarlet chalice rears,
There Ferhad <note>Farhad, the master stone-cutter who fell in love with Queen Shirin. His tragic labor and devotion are staples of Persian romantic literature.</note> for the love of Shirin pined,
Dyeing the desert red with his heart's tears.

Bring, bring the cup! drink we while yet we may
To our soul's ruin the forbidden draught <note>In Sufi poetry, wine is a metaphor for divine love or spiritual intoxication which "ruins" the rational, worldly ego to reveal the "treasure" of the soul.</note>;
Perhaps a treasure-trove is hid away
Among those ruins where the wine has laughed!—
Perhaps the tulip knows the fickleness
Of Fortune's smile, for on her stalk's green shaft
She bears a wine-cup through the wilderness.

The murmuring stream of <term>Ruknabad</term> <note>A famous brook in Shiraz, praised by Hafiz for its pure water and its ability to inspire the heart.</note>, the breeze
That blows from out <term>Mosalla</term>'s <note>The "Mosalla of Shiraz" was a public prayer ground and garden. It is where Hafiz's tomb is located today.</note> fair pleasaunce,
Summon me back when I would seek heart's ease,
Travelling afar; what though Love's countenance
Be turned full harsh and sorrowful on me,
I care not so that Time's unfriendly glance
Still from my Lady's beauty turned be.

Like Hafiz, drain the goblet cheerfully
While minstrels touch the lute and sweetly sing,
For all that makes thy heart rejoice in thee
Hangs of Life's single, slender, silken string.

<summary>In this concluding section of Poem XXVI, Hafiz muses on the transience of legendary Persian kings, suggesting that the only true escape from the "unfriendly glance" of Time is through the joy of the present moment and the spiritual beauty of his home in Shiraz.</summary>
<keywords>Hafiz, Shiraz, Ruknabad, Mosalla, Jamshid, Farhad and Shirin, memento mori, Sufi metaphor, Transience</keywords>
