### XXVII

My friend has fled! alas, my friend has fled,
And left me nought but tears and pain behind!
Like smoke above a flame caught by the wind,
So rose she from my breast and forth she sped.
Drunk with desire, I seized Love’s cup divine,
But she that held it poured the bitter wine
Of <term>Separation</term> <note>original: "firaq"—a central theme in Persian poetry denoting the painful distance between the lover (the seeker) and the Beloved (the Divine).</note> into it and fled.

The hunter she, and I the helpless prey;
Wounded and sick, round me her toils she drew,
My heart into a sea of sorrow threw,
Bound up her camel loads <note>The image of "binding the camel loads" evokes the departure of a desert caravan, a standard metaphor in Persian literature for the sudden and often heart-breaking end of a period of union.</note> and fled away.
Fain had I laid an ambush for her soul,
She saw and vanished, and the timid foal,
Good Fortune, slipped the rein and would not stay.

My heart was all too narrow for my woe,
And tears of blood <note>original: "ashk-e khun"—a common poetic exaggeration in Persian lyrics to express the highest degree of grief or suffering.</note> my weeping eyes have shed,
A crimson stream across the desert sped,
Rising from out my sad heart’s overflow.
She knew not what Love’s meanest slave can tell:
“’Tis sweet to serve!” but threw me a Farewell,
Kissing my threshold, turned, and cried “I go!”

In the clear dawn, before the east was red,
Before the rose had torn her veil in two <note>The "tearing of the veil" refers to the blooming of the rose, which in Persian poetry serves as a metaphor for the unveiling of spiritual truth or the physical manifestation of beauty.</note>,
A nightingale <note>The nightingale (*bolbol*) is the traditional symbol of the lover or the poet, whose song is a perpetual lament for the fleeting beauty of the rose.</note> through <term>Hafiz’</term> <note>Shams al-Din Muhammad Hafiz-e Shirazi (c. 1315–1390), the most celebrated poet of the Persian language, whose works are often used for divination and spiritual guidance.</note> garden flew,
Stayed but to fill its song with tears, and fled.

<summary>This page features a lyrical poem by Hafiz mourning the departure of a beloved, utilizing classic Persian motifs such as the caravan's departure, tears of blood, and the nightingale in the garden. It reflects the Sufi concept of "firaq" or the agonizing separation from the divine presence.</summary>
<keywords>Hafiz, Persian poetry, Separation, Sufism, Nightingale and Rose, Shiraz, Divine Love</keywords>

--- Page 105 ---
<meta>This page continues the sequence of lyrical poems from the Divan of Hafiz. While the previous poem (XXVII) lamented the sudden departure and "separation" of the beloved, Poem XXVIII shifts tone to a nostalgic and questioning remembrance. The poet asks if the beloved remembers their shared moments of spiritual and physical intimacy, moving from the personal to the mystical environment of the "tavern" of divine truth.</meta>

<page-type>text</page-type>
<header>POEMS FROM THE DIVAN OF HAFIZ</header>
<page-num>101</page-num>

### XXVIII

Hast thou forgotten when thy stolen glance
Was turned to me, when on my happy face
Clearly thy love was writ, which doth enhance
All happiness? or when my sore disgrace
(Hast thou forgot?) drew from thine eyes reproof,
And made thee hold thy sweet red lips aloof,
Dowered, like **Jesus’ breath**, with healing grace? <note>original: "dam-e Masiha"—in Persian literary tradition, Jesus is revered for his "healing breath" which could raise the dead. Hafiz uses this to describe the life-giving power of the beloved's speech or kiss.</note>

Hast thou forgotten how the glorious
Swift nights flew past, the cup of dawn brimmed high?
My love and I alone, God favouring us!
And when she like a waning moon did lie,
And Sleep had drawn his coif about her brow,
Hast thou forgot? Heaven’s crescent moon would bow
The head, and in her service pace the sky! <note>The poet suggests that the beauty of the beloved is so great that even the celestial bodies—like the moon—humble themselves to serve her.</note>

Hast thou forgotten, when a sojourner
Within the **tavern** <term>Kharabat</term> <note>Literally "the ruins" or "the tavern." In Sufi mysticism, this represents a place of spiritual intoxication where the seeker loses their ego to find Divine Truth, far from the hypocrisy of formal institutions.</note> gates and drunk with wine,
I found Love’s passionate wisdom hidden there,
Which in the **mosque** none even now divine?
The goblet’s **carbuncle** <note>A deep red gemstone (garnet or ruby). Here, it refers to the glowing, jewel-like color of the wine in the cup.</note> (hast thou forgot?)
Laughed out aloud, and speech flew hot
And fast between thy ruby lips and mine!

Hast thou forgotten when thy cheek’s dear torch
Lighted the beacon of desire in me,
And when my heart, like foolish **moths** that scorch
Their wings and yet return, turned all to thee? <note>The moth and the flame is the classic Persian metaphor for the lover's soul. Just as a moth is irresistibly drawn to a candle until it is consumed, the mystic is drawn to the light of the Divine, seeking annihilation in the beloved.</note>

<summary>This page features Poem XXVIII, a lyrical "ghazal" where the speaker reminds the beloved of their past intimacy and the spiritual wisdom found in the "tavern" of mystical love. It highlights the contrast between institutional religion and the direct, intoxicating experience of the Divine.</summary>
<keywords>Hafiz, Divan, Sufi mysticism, Jesus' breath, Tavern vs Mosque, Moth and Flame, Divine Love, Persian Poetry</keywords>

--- Page 106 ---
<meta>This page concludes Poem XXVIII and introduces Poem XXIX, transitioning from a nostalgic reflection on shared intimacy and the craft of poetry to one of the most celebrated ghazals of Hafiz—a message of hope and resilience in the face of suffering. Poem XXIX famously draws upon the Quranic story of Joseph and Jacob to offer comfort during times of exile and sorrow.</meta>

<page-type>text</page-type>
<page-num>102</page-num>
<header>POEMS FROM THE DIVAN OF HAFIZ</header>

Within the banquet-hall of Good Repute <note>The "banquet-hall of Good Repute" refers to the spiritual tavern where the seeker's ego is dismantled. Hafiz often contrasts social "reputation" with the "disgrace" of divine love.</note>
(Hast thou forgot?) the wine’s self-pressed my suit,
And filled the morn with drunken jollity!

Hast thou forgotten when thou laid’st aright
The **uncut gems** of Hafiz’ inmost thought,
And side by side thy sweet care strung the bright
Array of verse on verse—hast thou forgot? <note>Hafiz compares the composition of poetry to the stringing of pearls or the cutting of gems, a common metaphor for the craft of the ghazal.</note>

->XXIX<-

FROM **Canaan** <note>Canaan is the biblical and Quranic land of the prophets. In Persian poetry, it represents the home from which the beloved has been exiled.</note> **Joseph** <term>Yousof</term> <note>Joseph is the archetype of supreme beauty and the "lost beloved." His return to his father, Jacob, is used by Hafiz as a metaphor for the eventual return of joy and the presence of God after a period of spiritual "darkness."</note> shall return, whose face
A little time was hidden: weep no more—
Oh, weep no more! in sorrow’s dwelling-place
The roses yet shall spring from the bare floor!
And heart bowed down beneath a secret pain—
Oh stricken heart! joy shall return again,
Peace to the love-tossed brain—oh, weep no more!

Oh, weep no more! for once again **Life’s Spring**
Shall throne her in the meadows green, and o’er
Her head the **minstrel of the night** <note>The "minstrel of the night" is the nightingale (*bulbul*), which in Persian literature is the devoted lover of the rose.</note> shall fling
A canopy of rose leaves, score on score.
The secret of the world thou shalt not learn,
And yet behind the **veil** Love’s fire may burn—
Weep’st thou? let hope return and weep no more!

To-day may pass, to-morrow pass, before
The **turning wheel** <note>The "turning wheel" (*charkh-e falak*) refers to the celestial spheres or Fate, which governs the ups and downs of human life.</note> give me my heart’s desire;
Heaven’s self shall change, and turn not evermore
The universal wheel of Fate in ire.

<summary>This page contains the conclusion of a poem on poetic inspiration and the beginning of Hafiz's famous poem of consolation, which uses the story of Joseph's return to Canaan as a metaphor for hope.</summary>
<keywords>Hafiz, Joseph of Canaan, Sufi metaphors, Persian poetry, resilience, nightingale and rose, Fate</keywords>

<meta>This page concludes the famous "Ghazal of Hope" (Poem XXIX), which uses the story of Joseph and Jacob as a metaphor for the soul's journey through suffering. It then transitions to Poem XXX, one of Hafiz's most famous tributes to his beloved hometown, Shiraz.</meta>

<header>DIVAN OF HAFIZ</header>

Oh Pilgrim nearing Mecca’s holy fane, <note>Mecca is the holiest city in Islam; the "fane" refers to the Kaaba. Hafiz compares the spiritual seeker's journey to the arduous Hajj pilgrimage.</note>
The thorny <term>maghilan</term> <note>The maghilan is a sharp desert thorn or camel-thorn bush. In Sufi poetry, it represents the painful trials one must endure on the path to the Beloved.</note> wounds thee in vain,
The desert blooms again—oh, weep no more!

What though the river of mortality
Round the unstable house of Life doth roar,
Weep not, oh heart, Noah shall pilot thee, <note>A reference to Prophet Noah and his Ark; Hafiz suggests that even in the "flood" of worldly chaos, divine guidance will ensure safety.</note>
And guide thine ark to the desirèd shore!
The goal lies far, and perilous is thy road,
Yet every path leads to that same abode
Where thou shalt drop thy load—oh, weep no more!

Mine enemies have persecuted me,
My Love has turned and fled from out my door—
God counts our tears and knows our misery;
Ah, weep not! He has heard thy weeping sore.
And chained in poverty and plunged in night,
Oh Hafiz, take thy <term>Koran</term> <note>The central religious text of Islam. The poet's pen name, "Hafiz," literally means "one who has memorized the entire Quran."</note> and recite
Litanies infinite, and weep no more!

---

->XXX<-

All hail, <term>Shiraz</term> <note>The city in south-central Iran where Hafiz lived. It was a major center of Persian culture, known for its gardens and poets.</note>, hail! oh site without peer!
May God be the Watchman before thy gate,
That the feet of Misfortune enter not here!
Lest my <term>Ruknabad</term> <note>A stream or canal in Shiraz that Hafiz famously preferred over the rivers of Paradise. It was known for its exceptionally clear and sweet water.</note> be left desolate,
A hundred times, “God forbid!” I pray;
Its limpid stream where the shadows wait
Like the fount of <term>Khizr</term> <note>Khizr (or Al-Khidr) is a legendary figure who discovered the "Water of Life" and gained immortality. Hafiz compares the water of Shiraz to this miraculous, life-giving source.</note> giveth life for aye.

<summary>This page concludes a poem of spiritual consolation and begins a famous ode to the city of Shiraz, celebrating its beauty and its refreshing stream, Ruknabad.</summary>
<keywords>Hajj, pilgrimage, maghilan, Noah, Quran, Shiraz, Ruknabad, Khizr, Water of Life</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the translation of Poem XXX, a celebratory ode to the city of Shiraz, and begins Poem XXXI, which focuses on the spiritual renewal associated with the arrival of Spring.</meta>

<page-type>text</page-type>
<page-num>104</page-num>
<header>POEMS FROM THE DIVAN OF HAFIZ</header>

Between <term>Jafrabad</term> <note>A suburb of Shiraz known in Hafiz’s time for its lush gardens and refreshing air.</note> and the garden of <term>Mosalla</term> <note>One of the most famous recreational and spiritual sites in Shiraz; Hafiz requested to be buried there, and his tomb remains there today.</note>
The north wind blows, heavy with the scent of <term>ambergris</term> <note>A rare and costly substance used in perfumery; in Persian poetry, it frequently describes the fragrance of a beloved's hair or the sweetness of the air.</note>—
Oh, come to Shiraz when the north wind blows!
There the peace of the Archangel Gabriel dwells
With the one who is the master of its treasures; the fame
Of the sugar of Egypt shall wither and cease,
For the sweet breath of our beauties has put it to shame. <note>Persian poets often compared the "sweetness" of their own land and language against Egypt, which was the historical center of the sugar trade.</note>

Oh wind that blows from the sunrise,
What news do you bring of the maiden with the <term>drunken eyes</term>? <note>In the mystical tradition, "drunken eyes" (*chashm-e mast*) do not refer to literal intoxication, but to the alluring, sleepy, or heavy-lidded look of beauty that "intoxicates" the soul of the seeker.</note>
What news of that lovely girl do you carry?
Do not ask me to wake from my dreaming and rise,
For in my dreams I have rested my head at her feet—
When a perfect stillness surrounds me,
The vision of her makes my solitude sweet.

If the <term>Cup-bearer</term> <note>The *Saki*, a central figure in Sufi poetry who represents the spiritual guide or the Divine who pours the "wine" of spiritual ecstasy.</note> should pour out my very blood as wine,
Flowing as freely as milk from a mother’s breast,
At his word, let my heart yield its crimson flood.
But, Hafiz, Hafiz! You are one of those
Who always fears that the time of absence is near;
For the days when you held the Beloved so close,
Why do you not offer your thanks so that all may hear?

### XXXI

The breath of the musk-strewing wind of Dawn shall blow,
The ancient world shall turn to youth again,
And other wines from out the chalice of Spring shall flow;

<vocab>Jafrabad, Mosalla, Shiraz, Gabriel, Hafiz, ambergris, Cup-bearer, Dawn, Spring</vocab>

<meta>This page continues the collection of lyrical poems (ghazals) by the Persian poet Hafiz. While the previous page celebrated the physical beauty of Shiraz, this page transitions into a classic Sufi meditation on the fleeting nature of spring, the necessity of joy before the arrival of the fasting month, and the spiritual "tavern" as a place of enlightenment.</meta>

<page-type>text</page-type>
<page-num>105</page-num>

## DIVAN OF HAFIZ

Wine-red, the <term>judas-tree</term> <note>In Persian poetry, the *Arghavan* (Judas-tree) is prized for its vibrant, deep-red blossoms that appear in early spring, often compared to the color of wine.</note> shall set before
The pure white jessamine a brimming cup,
And wind flowers lift their scarlet chalice up
For the star-pale narcissus to adore.

The long-drawn tyranny of grief shall pass,
Parting shall end in meeting, the lament
Of the <term>sad bird</term> <note>The nightingale (*bulbul*), which in Persian literature is the eternal lover of the rose, singing of its longing and the pain of separation.</note> that sang "Alas, alas!"
Shall reach the rose in her red-curtained tent.
Forth from the mosque! The tavern calls to me!
Would'st hinder us? The preacher's homily
Is long, but life will soon be spent!

Ah, foolish Heart! The pleasures of To-day,
If thou abandon, will To-morrow stand
Thy surety for the gold thou'st thrown away?
In <term>Sha'aban</term> <note>The eighth month of the Islamic lunar calendar, immediately preceding Ramadan. It was traditionally viewed as a time of joy and preparation.</note> the troops of Grief disband,
And crown the hours with wine's red coronet——
The sun of merriment ere long will set,
And meagre <term>Ramazan</term> <note>The holy month of fasting (Ramadan), during which the poet laments the temporary "closure" of the taverns and the end of wine-drinking festivals.</note> is close at hand!

Dear is the rose—now, now her sweets proclaim,
While yet the purple petals blush and blow;
Hither adown the path of Spring she came,
And by the path of Autumn she will go.
Now, while we listen, <term>Minstrel</term> <note>The *Mutrib*, or musician, who accompanies the recitation of poetry. In Sufi thought, music is a vehicle for reaching a state of spiritual ecstasy.</note>, tune thy lay!
Thyself hast said: "The Present steals away;
The Future comes, and bringing—what? Dost know?"

Summoned by thy melody did <term>Hafiz</term> <note>The poet's pen name, which means "one who has memorized the Quran." It is a hallmark of the *ghazal* form for the poet to include his name in the final stanza.</note> rise
Out of the darkness near thy lips to dwell;
Back to the dark again his pathway lies—
Sing out, sing clear, and singing cry: Farewell!

<summary>This page features a celebratory poem urging the reader to embrace the joys of spring and spiritual companionship before the arrival of the ascetic month of Ramadan. It uses nature imagery, such as the rose and the judas-tree, to illustrate the transience of life and the beauty of the present moment.</summary>

<keywords>Hafiz, Sha'aban, Ramazan, Sufi symbolism, Shiraz, spring poetry, Persian ghazal, transience</keywords>

### XXXII

Upon a branch of the straight <term>cypress-tree</term> <note>The *sarv* (cypress) is a symbol of the beloved's graceful stature and, being evergreen, represents a free soul that remains unchanged by the turning of the seasons.</note>
Once more the patient <term>nightingale</term> <note>The *bulbul* (nightingale) represents the devoted lover who sings of his longing for the rose.</note> doth rest:
“Oh Rose!” he cries, “evil be turned from thee!
I sing thee all men’s thanks; thou blossomest
And hope springs up in every joyless heart—
Let not the nightingale lament apart,
Nor with thy proud thorns wound his faithful breast.’

I will not mourn my woeful banishment,
He that has hungered for his lady’s face
Shall, when she cometh, know a great content.
The <term>Zealot</term> <note>The *Zahid* (ascetic/zealot) is a recurring character in Hafiz's work, representing those who follow the outward, legalistic rules of religion without experiencing the inner, spiritual fire of love.</note> seeks a heavenly dwelling-place,
<term>Huris</term> <note>Celestial maidens of Paradise. Hafiz often contrasts the "deferred" rewards of the religious orthodox with the "immediate" spiritual ecstasy found in love.</note> to welcome him in Paradise;
Here at the <term>tavern gate</term> <note>The *kharabat* (tavern or ruins) is a Sufi metaphor for a place of spiritual transformation where the ego is annihilated.</note> my heaven lies,
I need no welcome but my lady’s grace.

Better to drink red wine than tears, say I,
While the <term>lute</term> <note>The *barbat* or *ud*, instruments often associated with the "Sama" (spiritual music) that moves the soul toward the divine.</note> sings; and if one bid thee cease,
“God is the merciful!” thou shalt reply.
To some, life brings but joy and endless ease;
Ah, let them laugh although the jest be vain!
For me the source of pleasure lay in pain,
And weeping for my lady I found peace.

Hafiz, why art thou ever telling o’er
The tale of absence and of sorrow’s night?
Knowest thou not that parting goes before
All meeting, and from darkness comes the light!

<summary>This poem explores the Sufi concept of finding divine truth through the "tavern" of spiritual ruin rather than the "heaven" of the religious zealot. It concludes with the poet reminding himself that the pain of separation is a necessary precursor to the joy of union.</summary>

<keywords>Hafiz, nightingale and rose, Sufi metaphors, Tavern of Ruin, Zahid (Zealot), spiritual love, Persian ghazal</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the translation of the Divan of Hafiz. Following the previous page’s focus on the tavern as a site of spiritual refuge (Ghazal XXXII), this poem (Ghazal XXXIII) explores the themes of eternal devotion and the "hidden treasury" of the heart, utilizing classical Persian metaphors of the sun ripening gems and the beloved's hair as a snare for the soul.</meta>

<page-type>text</page-type>
<header>DIVAN OF HAFIZ</header>

### XXXIII

The jewel of the secret treasury <note>In Persian mysticism, the "secret treasury" refers to the divine essence or the hidden mysteries of Love that reside within the human heart.</note>
Is still the same as once it was; the seal
Upon Love’s treasure casket, and the key,
Are still what thieves <note>The "thieves" are the uninitiated or the worldly who try to grasp the secrets of spiritual ecstasy without the requisite devotion.</note> can neither break nor steal;
Still among lovers loyalty <term>wafa</term> <note>The concept of "wafa" (faithfulness/loyalty) is the primary virtue of the lover in Persian poetry, contrasting with the "jafa" (cruelty or indifference) of the beloved.</note> is found,
And therefore faithful eyes still strew the ground
With the same pearls <note>A poetic metaphor for tears.</note> that mine once strewed for thee.

Question the wandering winds <term>Saba</term> <note>The "Saba" is the morning breeze, the traditional messenger between the lover and the beloved in the Persian tradition.</note> and thou shalt know
That from the dusk until the dawn doth break,
My consolation is that still they blow
The perfume of thy curls across my cheek.
A dart from thy bent brows <note>The beloved's arched eyebrows are frequently compared to a hunter's bow, while the eyelashes are the arrows or "darts" that wound the lover.</note> has wounded me—
Ah, come! my heart still waiteth helplessly,
Has waited ever, till thou heal its pain.

If seekers after rubies there were none,
Still to the dark mines where the gems had lain
Would pierce, as he was wont, the radiant sun, <note>Hafiz references an ancient scientific belief that the heat of the sun, over centuries, transformed common stones into precious rubies within the earth.</note>
Setting the stones ablaze. Would’st hide the stain
Of my heart’s blood? Blood-red the ruby glows
(And whence it came my wounded bosom knows)
Upon thy lips <note>The beloved's lips are commonly compared to "rubies of Badakhshan" for their color and preciousness.</note> to show what thou hast done.

Let not thy curls waylay my pilgrim soul,
As robbers use, and plunder me no more!
Years join dead year, but thine extortionate rule
Is still the same, merciless as before.
Sing, Hafiz, <note>It is customary for the poet to include his pen name (takhallus) in the final stanza (maqta) of a ghazal.</note> sing again of eyes that weep!
For still the fountain of our tears is deep
As once it was, and still with tears is full.

<page-num>107</page-num>

<summary>Ghazal XXXIII reflects on the unchanging nature of spiritual love and the poet's enduring suffering, using the imagery of the morning breeze and the alchemical ripening of rubies. It emphasizes that despite the passage of time, the intensity of the lover's devotion and the "theft" of the soul by the beloved's beauty remain constant.</summary>

<keywords>Hafiz, Persian poetry, Sufism, beloved, rubies, morning breeze, devotion, mystical love</keywords>

This ghazal explores the mythic creation of humanity and the "Divine Trust" that angels could not bear, but which was placed upon the shoulders of man.</meta>

<header>DIVAN OF HAFIZ</header>

## XXXIV

Last night I dreamed that angels <note>In the original Persian, these are the *Malā'ik* (angels), often depicted in Sufi poetry as beings of pure intellect who lack the capacity for the passionate, "drunken" love that characterizes humanity.</note> stood without
The tavern door <note>The "tavern" (*maykhana*) is a central metaphor in Sufi poetry, representing the place of spiritual instruction where the "wine" of divine love is served, away from the dry legalism of the mosque.</note>, and knocked in vain, and wept;
They took the clay of Adam <term>Khāk-i Ādam</term> <note>Adam's clay. According to Sufi interpretation of the creation story, God kneaded the clay of the first human with the wine of love for forty days.</note>, and, methought,
Moulded a cup therewith while all men slept.
Oh dwellers in the halls of Chastity! <note>Refers to the angels in the celestial realm who remain pure but lack the "fire" of earthly passion.</note>
You brought Love's passionate red wine to me,
Down to the dust I am, your bright feet stept.

For Heaven's self was all too weak to bear
The burden <term>Amānat</term> <note>This refers to the "Trust" mentioned in the Quran (33:72), which was offered to the Heavens, the Earth, and the Mountains, but they refused to bear it out of fear. Only Man was "foolish" or "bold" enough to accept the burden of divine love and free will.</note> of His love God laid on it,
He turned to seek a messenger elsewhere,
And in the Book of Fate my name was writ.
Between my Lord and me such concord lies
As makes the Huris <term>Hūr</term> <note>The beautiful, celestial maidens of Paradise. Hafiz suggests that the joy of the union between the lover and God is so great that even the inhabitants of heaven are envious or celebratory.</note> glad in Paradise,
With songs of praise through the green glades they flit.

A hundred dreams of Fancy's garnered store
Assail me—Father Adam went astray
Tempted by one poor grain of corn! <note>In the Islamic and Persian tradition, the forbidden fruit of Paradise is often identified as wheat or a grain of corn, rather than an apple.</note> Wherefore
Absolve and pardon him that turns away
Though the soft breath of Truth reaches his ears,
For two-and-seventy jangling creeds <note>A reference to the "Hadith of the 73 Sects," suggesting that while there are many conflicting religious dogmas (the 72), there is only one path to the "Truth" of divine love.</note> he hears,
And loud-voiced Fable calls him ceaselessly.

That, that is not the flame of Love's true fire
Which makes the torchlight shadows dance in rings,
But where the radiance draws the moth's <term>parvāna</term> desire
And sends him forth with scorched and drooping wings. <note>The moth and the candle is the quintessential Persian metaphor for the lover's ultimate goal: *fanā*, or the annihilation of the self in the presence of the Divine Beloved.</note>

<vocab>Adam, Chastity, Love, Book of Fate, Huris, Paradise, Truth, Fable, Hafiz</vocab>

<summary>This page contains one of Hafiz's most famous poems, depicting the creation of humanity from "love-kneaded clay" and man's unique role as the bearer of a divine burden that even the angels could not carry. It utilizes the classic Sufi metaphors of the tavern, the moth and the candle, and the wine of ecstasy.</summary>
<keywords>Adam, creation myth, divine trust, tavern, angels, Sufism, moth and candle, 72 sects, intoxication</keywords>

This page shifts from the cosmic scale of creation to the intimate, melancholic longing for past companionship and the enduring power of memory.</meta>

<header>DIVAN OF HAFIZ</header>

The heart of one who dwells retired <note>Original: *Khalwat-nishīn*. This refers to a mystic or hermit who sits in seclusion to focus entirely on the Divine.</note> shall break,
Rememb’ring a black mole <note>In Persian poetics, the 'black mole' on the beloved's face represents the point of Divine Unity—the singular source of all creation that draws the seeker in.</note> and a red cheek,
And his life ebb, sapped at its secret springs.

Yet since the earliest time that man has sought
To comb the locks of Speech <note>A traditional metaphor where the poet acts as a beautician, "combing" and "arranging" the messy tresses of language into the beautiful order of poetry.</note>, his goodly bride,
Not one, like Hafiz, from the face of Thought
Has torn the veil of Ignorance aside.

### XXXV

FORGET not when dear friend to friend returned,
Forget not days gone by, forget them not! <note>This poem is a *Ghazal* built around the haunting refrain *Yād bād*, which means "May it be remembered" or "Long live the memory."</note>
My mouth has tasted bitterness, and learned
To drink the envenomed cup of mortal lot <term>Dahr</term> <note>Fate or the material world, often depicted in Persian poetry as a fickle and sometimes cruel force.</note>;
Forget not when a sweeter draught was mine,
Loud rose the songs of them that drank that wine—
->Forget them not!<-

Forget not loyal lovers long since dead,
Though faith and loyalty should be forgot,
Though the earth cover the enamoured head,
And in the dust wisdom and passion rot.
My friends have thrust me from their memory;
Vainly a thousand thousand times I cry:
->Forget me not!<-

Weary I turn me to my bonds again.
Once there were hands strong to deliver me,
Forget not when they broke a poor slave’s chain! <note>The "chain" in Sufi imagery often refers to the "tresses" or "curls" of the Beloved's hair, which bind the lover in a state of spiritual intoxication.</note>
Though from mine eyes tears flow unceasingly,

<summary>This page concludes a poem on the poet's unique ability to uncover truth through verse and begins a poignant lamentation on the passage of time and the sanctity of memory.</summary>

I think on them whose rose gardens are set
Beside the Zindeh Rud <note>Original: *Zindah-rūd*. Literally the "Life-giving River," the main river passing through the city of Isfahan. It is a symbol of vitality and beauty in Persian literature.</note>, and I forget
->Life's misery.<-

Sorrow has made her lair in my breast,
And undisturbed she lies—forget them not
That drove her forth like to a hunted beast!
Hafiz <note>The pen name of the poet, meaning "one who has memorized the Quran." He often addresses himself in the final lines of his poems.</note>, thou and thy tears shall be forgot,
Lock fast the gates of thy sad heart! But those
That held the key to thine unspoken woes—
->Forget them not!<-

### XXXVI

BELOVED, who has bid thee ask no more
How fares my life? to play the enemy
And ask not where he dwells that was thy friend?
Thou art the breath of mercy passing o'er
The whole wide world, and the offender I;
Ah, let the rift my tears have channelled end,
->Question the past no more!<-

If thou would'st know the secret of Love's fire,
It shall be manifest unto thine eyes:
Question the torch flame burning steadfastly,
But ask no more the sweet wind's wayward choir.
Ask me of faith and love that never dies;
Darius, Alexander's sovereignty <note>Darius and Alexander the Great represent the height of ancient worldly power. In Sufi poetry, their fallen empires are frequently contrasted with the eternal, "sovereign" power of Divine Love.</note>,
->I sing of these no more.<-

Ask not the monk <note>Original: *Zāhid*. Usually referring to a dry, outward-facing ascetic or preacher whom Hafiz often critiqued for hypocrisy.</note> to give thee Truth’s pure gold,
He hides no riches ’neath his lying guise;
And ask not him to teach thee alchemy <note>In Sufi poetry, alchemy is the spiritual process of transforming the "base metal" of the human ego into the "gold" of divine love.</note>
Whose treasure-house is bare, his hearth-stone cold.
Ask to what goal the wandering dervish <note>A practitioner of Sufism who has taken vows of poverty; a mendicant seeking spiritual truth.</note> hies,
They knew not his desire who counselled thee:
                ->Question his rags no more!<-

And in their learned books thou’lt seek in vain
The key to Love’s locked gateway; Heart grown wise
In pain and sorrow, ask no remedy!
But when the time of roses comes again,
Take what it gives, oh Hafiz, ere it flies,
And ask not why the hour has brought it thee,
                ->And wherefore ask no more!<-

### XXXVII

Arise! and fill a golden goblet up
Until the wine of pleasure overflow,
Before into thy skull’s pale empty cup
A grimmer Cup-bearer <note>Original: *Sāqī*. While the Saki usually brings the wine of spiritual ecstasy, here the poet refers to Death as the final cup-bearer who fills the skull with dust.</note> the dust shall throw.
Yea, to the Vale of Silence <note>A poetic metaphor for the grave or the transition into the afterlife.</note> we must come;
Yet shall the flagon laugh and Heaven’s dome
Thrill with an answering echo ere we go!

Thou knowest that the riches of this field
Make no abiding, let the goblet’s fire
Consume the fleeting harvest Earth may yield!
Oh Cypress-tree! <note>The cypress (*sarv*) is a recurring symbol in Persian poetry representing the graceful stature of the beloved and the concept of spiritual freedom and eternity.</note> green home of Love’s sweet choir,

<summary>This page concludes a poem dismissing religious hypocrisy in favor of lived experience and begins a new ghazal urging the reader to embrace life's joys before death arrives. It uses the traditional imagery of the Saki (cup-bearer) and the cypress tree to illustrate the beauty and fragility of existence.</summary>

When I unto the dust I am have passed,
Forget thy former wantonness, and cast
Thy shadow o’er the dust of my desire. <note>The poet asks the Beloved to visit his grave, suggesting that even in death, the lover's soul remains longing for a sign of the Divine.</note>

Flow, bitter tears, and wash me clean! for they
Whose feet are set upon the road that lies
’Twixt Earth and Heaven: “Thou shalt be pure,” they
say,
“Before unto the pure thou lift thine eyes.”
Seeing but himself, the Zealot <note>Original: *Zāhid*. A recurring figure in Hafiz’s poetry representing outward, judgmental piety as opposed to inward, spiritual love.</note> sees but sin;
Grief to the mirror of his soul let in,
Oh Lord, and cloud it with the breath of sighs!

No tainted eye shall gaze upon her face,
No glass but that of an unsullied heart
Shall dare reflect my Lady’s perfect grace.
Though like to snakes that from the herbage start,
Thy curling locks have wounded me full sore,
Thy red lips hold the power of the <term>bezoar</term> <note>Original: *Pāzahar*. A "bezoar" is a stone-like mass found in the stomachs of animals, historically prized as a universal antidote to poison. In Persian poetry, the Beloved's lips are the antidote to the "poisonous" bite of their snake-like curls.</note>—
Ah, touch and heal me where I lie apart!

And when from her the wind blows perfume sweet,
Tear, Hafiz, like the rose, thy robe in two, <note>A reference to the *Chak-i gariban* (rending the collar), a gesture of spiritual ecstasy or overwhelming grief. The rose "tears its robe" as its petals open from the bud.</note>
And cast thy rags beneath her flying feet,
To deck the place thy mistress passes through.

### XXXVIII

I CEASE not from desire till my desire
Is satisfied; or let my mouth attain
My love’s red mouth, or let my soul expire,
Sighed from those lips that sought her lips in vain.

# DIVAN OF HAFIZ

<column-break/>

->113<-

**Others may find another love as fair;**
Upon her threshold I have laid my head,
The dust shall cover me, still lying there,
When from my body life and love have fled.

My soul is on my lips <note>Original: *jān bar lab*. A classical Persian idiom for the moment of death, where the soul is imagined as a bird perched on the lips, ready to depart. In poetry, the lover hopes the soul will depart only upon meeting the Beloved.</note> ready to fly,
But grief beats in my heart and will not cease,
Because not once, not once before I die,
Will her sweet lips give all my longing peace.
My breath is narrowed down to one long sigh
For a **red mouth** <note>In Persian mystical poetry, the "mouth" or "lips" of the Beloved often symbolize the life-giving word or the breath of the Divine that can resurrect the spirit.</note> that burns my thoughts like fire;
When will that mouth draw near and make reply
To one whose life is straitened with desire?

When I am dead, open my grave and see
The cloud of smoke that rises round thy feet:
In my dead heart the fire still burns for thee;
Yea, the smoke rises from my **winding-sheet**! <note>Original: *kafan*. This striking image suggests that the fire of the poet's love is so intense that it continues to smolder even after burial, turning the shroud to smoke.</note>
Ah, come, Beloved! for the meadows wait
Thy coming, and the thorn bears flowers instead
Of thorns, the **cypress** <note>The cypress (*sarv*) is a staple of Persian gardens, usually celebrated for its "freedom" because it bears no fruit. Here, the poet suggests that the Beloved’s presence is so miraculous it causes even the fruitless cypress to bear fruit.</note> fruit, and desolate
Bare winter from before thy steps has fled.

Hoping within some garden ground to find
A **red rose** soft and sweet as thy soft cheek,
Through every meadow blows the **western wind**, <note>Original: *Saba*. The morning breeze or zephyr, traditionally the messenger that carries the scent of the Beloved to the lover and the lover's sighs back to the Beloved.</note>
Through every garden he is fain to seek.
Reveal thy face! that the whole world may be
Bewildered by thy radiant loveliness;
The cry of man and woman comes to thee,
Open thy lips and comfort their distress!

<summary>This page concludes a poem of intense devotion, where the poet declares that his love will persist even in the grave, manifesting as smoke from his shroud. It ends with a plea for the Beloved to reveal themselves and bring a miraculous spring to the world.</summary>
<keywords>Hafiz, Sufism, Divine Beloved, immortality of love, Saba (wind), martyrdom of love, Persian garden imagery</keywords>

# DIVAN OF HAFIZ

<column-break/>

->114<-

Each curling lock of thy luxuriant hair
Breaks into barbèd hooks to catch my heart,
My broken heart is wounded everywhere
With countless wounds from which the red drops start.
Yet when sad lovers meet and tell their sighs,
Not without praise shall **Hafiz’** <note>The poet's pen name (takhallus), meaning "one who has memorized the Quran." In the tradition of the ghazal, the poet almost always includes his name in the final stanza.</note> name be said,
Not without tears, in those pale companies
Where joy has been forgot and hope has fled.

### XXXIX

Cypress and Tulip and sweet Eglantine,
Of these the tale from lip to lip is sent;
Washed by three cups, oh **Saki** <note>The wine-pourer. In mystical poetry, the Saki represents the spiritual guide (Murshid) who pours the "wine" of divine knowledge into the heart of the seeker.</note>, of thy wine,
My song shall turn upon this argument.
Spring, bride of all the meadows, rises up,
Clothed in her ripest beauty: fill the cup!
Of Spring’s handmaidens runs this song of mine.

The **sugar-loving birds** <note>Original: *tūtiyān-i Hind* (parrots of India). In Persian literature, parrots are often symbols of eloquent poets. Legend says Hafiz sent this specific poem to Sultan Ghiyasuddin of Bengal after the ruler invited him to his court.</note> of distant **Ind**,
Except a **Persian sweetmeat** <note>A metaphor for the poem itself. Hafiz is boasting that his Persian poetry is so "sweet" that even the parrots (poets) of India find no other "food" to their liking.</note> that was brought
To fair **Bengal**, have found nought to their mind.
See how my song, that in one night was wrought,
Defies the limits set by space and time!
  O’er plains and mountain-tops my fearless rhyme,
Child of a night, its year-long road shall find.

And thou whose sense is dimmed with **piety** <note>A reference to the *Zahid* (the ascetic or outward zealot), a recurring character in Hafiz’s work who focuses on the letter of the law rather than the spirit of love.</note>,
Thou too shalt learn the magic of her eyes;
Forth comes the caravan of sorcery
When from those gates the blue-veined curtains rise.

<vocab>Hafiz: the poet's pen name; Saki: wine-bearer; Ind: India; Bengal: a region in eastern India/modern Bangladesh; Persian: language/culture of Iran; Eglantine: a type of wild rose; Cypress: a tree symbolizing the Beloved's stature; Tulip: a flower symbolizing the martyr's heart or a wine cup; sorcery: supernatural charm or magic.</vocab>

And when she walks the flowery meadows through,
Upon the jasmine's shamèd cheek the dew
Gathers like sweat, she is so fair to see !

Ah, swerve not from the path of righteousness
Though the world lure thee ! like a wrinkled crone,
Hiding beneath her robe lasciviousness,
She plunders them that pause and heed her moan.
From **Sinai Moses** <note>A reference to the prophet Moses receiving the Divine Law on Mount Sinai; in Sufi thought, this represents the soul attaining true spiritual wealth through communion with God.</note> brings thee wealth untold ;
Bow not thine head before the **calf of gold**
Like **Samir** <note>Original: *Samiri*. According to Quranic tradition, Samiri was the man who tempted the Israelites to worship a golden calf while Moses was away on the mountain.</note>, following after wickedness.

From the **Shah's** <note>The Persian title for a King or Monarch.</note> garden blows the wind of Spring,
The tulip in her lifted chalice bears
A dewy wine of Heaven's minist'ring ;
Until **Ghiyasuddin** <note>Sultan Ghiyasuddin Azam Shah, the ruler of Bengal (reigned 1389–1410). Hafiz famously exchanged correspondence with him, though the poet never actually traveled to India.</note>, the Sultan, hears,
Sing, **Hafiz**, of thy longing for his face.
The breezes whispering round thy dwelling-place
Shall carry thy lament unto the King.

### XL

THE margin of a stream, the willow's shade,
A mind inclined to song, a mistress sweet,
A **Cup-bearer** <note>Original: *Saki*. While often referring to a literal wine-server in a tavern, in this mystical context, the Saki represents the spiritual guide who provides the "wine" of divine love.</note> whose cheek outshines the rose,
A friend upon whose heart thy heart is laid :
Oh Happy-starred ! let not thine hours fleet
Unvalued ; may each minute as it goes
Lay tribute of enjoyment at thy feet,
That thou may'st live and know thy life is sweet.

# POEMS FROM THE DIVAN OF HAFIZ

<column-break/>

->116<-

Let every one upon whose heart desire
For a fair face lies like a burden sore,
That all his hopes may reach their goal unchecked,
Throw branches of **wild rue** <note>Original: *sipand*. In Persian culture, seeds or branches of wild rue are thrown into the fire to create a crackling smoke believed to ward off the "evil eye" and protect against envy or misfortune.</note> upon his fire.
My soul is like a bride, with a rich store
Of maiden thoughts and jewelled fancies decked,
And in Time’s gallery I yet may meet
Some picture meant for me, some image sweet.

Give thanks for nights spent in good company,
And take the gifts a tranquil mind may bring;
No heart is dark when the kind moon doth shine,
And grass-grown river-banks are fair to see.
The **Saki’s** <note>The "Cup-bearer." While this can mean a literal servant in a tavern, in Hafiz's poetry, the Saki is often a metaphor for the Spiritual Guide or the Divine source of inspiration.</note> radiant eyes, God favouring,
Are like a wine-cup brimming o’er with wine,
And him my drunken sense goes out to greet,
For e’en the pain he leaves behind is sweet.

**Hafiz** <note>The pen name of the poet, meaning "one who has the Quran by heart." He frequently addresses himself in the final lines of his poems.</note>, thy life has sped untouched by care,
With me towards the **tavern** <note>Original: *kharabāt*. In the Sufi tradition, the tavern is a place of spiritual transformation where the "wine" of divine love is consumed and the ego is destroyed.</note> turn thy feet!
The fairest robbers thou’lt encounter there,
And they will teach thee what to learn is sweet.

->XLI<-

The days of Spring are here! the **eglantine** <note>A type of wild rose prized for its sweet fragrance, often appearing in Persian garden imagery.</note>,
The rose, the tulip from the dust have risen—
And thou, why liest thou beneath the dust?
Like the full clouds of Spring, these eyes of mine
Shall scatter tears upon the grave thy prison,
Till thou too from the earth thine head shalt thrust.

<vocab>Hafiz (the poet), Saki (cup-bearer), eglantine (wild rose), tavern (place of spiritual ruin/rebirth), wine-cup, Spring (season of resurrection)</vocab>

# THE COLLECTED POEMS OF HAFIZ

<column-break/>

->117<-

### XLII

True love has vanished from every heart;
What has befallen all lovers fair?
When did the bonds of friendship part?—
What has befallen the friends that were?
Ah, why are the feet of **Khizr** <note>Original: *Khiżr*. A legendary prophet and "Green Man" of Islamic tradition who discovered the Fountain of Youth. He represents spiritual immortality and serves as a guide to those lost in the wilderness of the soul.</note> lingering?—
The **waters of life** <note>Original: *Āb-i Hayāt*. The mythical spring of immortality. Hafiz suggests that the spiritual source that once nourished the world has become clouded or inaccessible.</note> are no longer clear,
The purple rose has turned pale with fear,
And what has befallen the wind of Spring?

None now sayeth: "A love was mine,
Loyal and wise, to dispel my care."
None remembers love’s right divine;
What has befallen all lovers fair?
In the midst of the field, to the players’ feet,
The **ball of God’s favour** <note>This is a metaphor drawn from the game of **Polo** (*Chaugan*), which originated in Persia. The "ball" represents divine opportunity or grace, and the "horsemen" are the seekers or spiritual warriors who should be striving to "strike" it.</note> and mercy came,
But none has leapt forth to renew the game—
What has befallen the horsemen fleet?

Roses have bloomed, yet no bird rejoiced,
No vibrating throat has rung with the tale;
What can have silenced the hundred-voiced?
What has befallen the nightingale?
Heaven’s music is hushed, and the planets roll
In silence; has **Zohra** <note>The Persian name for the planet Venus. In classical poetry, Venus is the celestial musician or harpist of the heavens; her silence indicates a loss of cosmic harmony and joy.</note> broken her lute?
There is none to press out the vine’s ripe fruit,
And what has befallen the foaming bowl?

<summary>In this poem, Hafiz laments a perceived decline in the world, mourning the loss of true friendship, spiritual vitality, and the joyous tradition of divine love. He uses cosmic and courtly metaphors—such as the silence of Venus and the abandoned game of polo—to express a deep sense of cultural and spiritual stagnation.</summary>

<keywords>Khizr, Water of Life, Polo (Chaugan), Zohra (Venus), Lamentation, Persian Sufism, Spiritual Decay</keywords>

--- Page 122 ---
<meta>This page concludes Ghazal XLII, where Hafiz laments the decline of his beloved city, Shiraz, and begins one of his most celebrated spiritual anthems, Ghazal XLIII. This transition moves from a world-weary observation of social and spiritual decay toward a personal, ecstatic declaration of faith and the desire for reunion with the Divine.</meta>

<page-num>118</page-num>

A city where kings are but lovers crowned,
A land from the dust of which friendship springs—
Who has laid waste that enchanted ground? <note>Hafiz is likely referring to his home city of **Shiraz**, which he often praised as a paradise on earth. This stanza laments a period of political turmoil or social coldness that gripped the city.</note>
What has befallen the city of kings?
Years have passed since a **ruby** <note>Original: *la'l*. In Persian poetry, the ruby often symbolizes the heart's blood or the precious spiritual essence of a person. To find one in the "mine of manhood" means to find a person of true character and chivalry (*muruwwat*).</note> was won
From the mine of manhood; they labour in vain,
The fleet-footed wind and the quickening rain,
And what has befallen the light of the sun?

Hafiz, the secret of God's dread task
No man knoweth, in youth or prime
Or in wisest age; of whom would'st thou ask:
What has befallen the wheels of Time?

### XLIII

Where are the tidings of **union**? <note>Original: *vaṣl*. This is a central concept in Sufi mysticism, representing the ultimate goal of the seeker: the reunification of the human soul with the Divine Creator.</note> that I may arise—
Forth from the dust I will rise up to welcome thee!
My soul, like a **homing bird** <note>A classic metaphor in Persian literature. The soul is viewed as a celestial bird from the gardens of Heaven, temporarily imprisoned in the cage of the physical body and the material world.</note>, yearning for Paradise,
Shall arise and soar, from the snares of the world set
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;free.
When the voice of thy love shall call me to be thy
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;slave,
I shall rise to a greater far than the mastery
Of life and the living, time and the mortal span:
Pour down, oh Lord! from the clouds of thy **guiding**
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;**grace**, <note>Original: *hidāyat*. This refers to the divine assistance that leads a traveler on the spiritual path toward the Truth.</note>
The rain of a mercy that quickeneth on my grave,
Before, like dust that the wind bears from place to place,
I arise and flee beyond the knowledge of man.

<vocab>Hafiz, union, Paradise, Time, mercy</vocab>

<summary>This page concludes a lament for the lost virtues of Shiraz and begins a famous ghazal expressing the poet's yearning for spiritual union with the Divine, framing death as a joyful liberation of the soul.</summary>
<keywords>Ghazal 42, Ghazal 43, Shiraz, Sufi Union, Soul as a Bird, Divine Grace, Resurrection, Persian Chivalry</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the second half of Ghazal XLIII. Following the previous page's imagery of the soul as a homing bird seeking paradise, Hafiz now addresses the Beloved directly, envisioning a resurrection sparked not by a trumpet blast, but by the presence of Divine love.</meta>

<page-num>119</page-num>

->DIVAN OF HAFIZ<-

When to my grave thou turnest thy blessed feet,
Wine and the lute thou shalt bring in thine hand to me, <note>In the tradition of Persian mystical poetry, **wine** (*bada*) symbolizes spiritual intoxication and divine knowledge, while the **lute** (*rud*) represents the harmony of the soul. Bringing these to a grave suggests that spiritual joy survives physical death.</note>
Thy voice shall ring through the folds of my winding-
    sheet, <note>Original: *kafan*. The traditional plain cloth used to wrap the deceased for burial. Hafiz suggests that the spiritual ear remains open to the Beloved's call even after the body is shrouded.</note>
And I will arise and dance to thy minstrelsy.

Though I be old, clasp me one night to thy breast,
And I, when the dawn shall come to awaken me,
With the flush of youth on my cheek from thy bosom
    will rise. <note>This "resurrection of youth" represents the spiritual rejuvenation of the seeker. In the presence of the Divine, the "old" (meaning the world-weary or spiritually exhausted) soul is made new again.</note>

Rise up ! let mine eyes delight in thy stately grace ! <note>The "stately grace" refers to the **cypress tree** (*sarv*), a ubiquitous metaphor in Persian literature for the Beloved’s perfect, upright, and elegant stature.</note>
Thou art the goal to which all men’s endeavour has
    pressed,
And thou the **idol** <note>Original: *sanam* or *but*. While literal idols are forbidden, Sufi poets used the term to describe the irresistible beauty of the Divine Beloved which demands the seeker's total devotion.</note> of Hafiz’ worship ; thy face
From the world and life shall bid him come forth and arise !

<summary>In the concluding verses of Ghazal XLIII, Hafiz describes a spiritual resurrection where the presence of the Beloved restores youth to the soul and calls the seeker out of the physical world. The imagery blends the somber reality of the grave with the joyous celebration of music and wine to illustrate the triumph of Divine Love over death.</summary>

# NOTES

## I

Stanza 1.—The first line of this song, the opening poem in the <term>Divan</term> <note>A collection of poems by a single author, in this case, the complete works of Hafiz.</note>, is borrowed from an Arabic poem by Yezid ibn Moawiyah <note>Yazid I, the second ruler of the Umayyad Caliphate.</note>, the second <term>Khalif</term> <note>Caliph; the title for the civil and religious leader of the Muslim community.</note> of the Ommiad <note>Umayyad</note> line. This prince was held in abomination by the Persian Shi'ites, both as the head of the Sunnis and because he was the cause of the death of Hussein, the son of Ali, whom the Shi'ites regarded as the rightful successor to the <term>Khalifate</term>. Hafiz was frequently reproached for setting a quotation from the works of the abhorred Yezid at the head of his book, a reproach which he is said to have met with the reply, that it was good policy to steal from the heretics whatsoever they possessed of worth. <note>The line borrowed is "Ala ya ayyuha’s-saqi adir ka’san wa nawilha," which translates to "O Cup-bearer, pass around the cup and proffer it."</note>

> “ In this country (*i.e.* North-Eastern China) is found the best <term>musk</term> <note>A strong-smelling substance used in perfume, often used in Persian poetry to describe the scent of the Beloved's hair. original: "misk"</note> in the world, and I will tell you how it is produced. There exists in that region a kind of wild animal like a gazelle. It has feet and tail like the gazelle’s, a stag’s hair of a very coarse kind, but no horns. It has four tusks, two below and two above, about three inches long, and slender in form, one pair growing downwards and the other upwards. It is a very pretty creature. The musk is found in this way : when the creature has been taken, they find at the navel, between the flesh and the skin, something like an imposthume <note>An archaic term for a swelling or cyst; here referring to the musk gland.</note> filled with blood, which they cut out and remove, with all the skin attached to it; and the blood inside this imposthume is the musk that produces that powerful perfume. There is an immense number of these beasts in the country we are speaking of. The flesh is very good to eat. Messer Marco brought the dried head and feet of one of these animals to Venice with him.”—*Travels of Marco Polo.*

# NOTES

There is a play of meaning upon the **musk** <note>A fragrant secretion from the musk deer, frequently used in Persian poetry to symbolize the scent of the Beloved’s hair or the spread of one’s reputation.</note> which is obtained at the cost of the deer's life-blood and the tears of blood which the lover weeps for his mistress.

*Stanza* 2.—The title which Hafiz gives to the **Tavern-keeper** is <term>Pir-i-Maghan</term> <note>original: "Pir-i-Maghan"; literally, the "Old Man of the Magians."</note>. The history of this title is an epitome of the history of Persian faiths. It indicated primarily the priest of the first of Persian religions, that of **Zoroaster** <note>The ancient Iranian prophet who founded Zoroastrianism, which predated the arrival of Islam.</note>. When the Muslims <note>original: "Mahommadans"</note> invaded Persia, and the preachers of the Prophet supplanted the priests of Zoroaster, their title fell into disrepute, and was degraded so far that it came to mean only the keeper of a tavern or <term>caravanserai</term> <note>A roadside inn where travelers could rest and recover from the day's journey.</note>. But in this sense it gradually regained the honorable place from which it had fallen; for the keepers of such places of resort were, for the most part, men well acquainted with the “ways of the road and the hostelry.” In their time they may themselves have served travelers upon their journey; they had heard and learnt much from the wayfarers who stopped at their gates, and they were able to guide others upon their journey, sending them forth refreshed and comforted in body. And here the **Sufis** <note>Mystics who sought a direct, personal experience of God, often using the imagery of wine and love to describe spiritual ecstasy.</note> took up the ancient name and used it to mean that wise old man who supplied weary travelers upon life’s road with the spiritual draught of Sufi doctrine which refreshes and comforts the soul.

### II

*Stanza* 1.—This poem has been expounded to me as a description of the poet’s quest for love. In an allegory he shows how he looked for it in vain from that image of earthly devotion, the nightingale; he warns men that it comes not but by humiliation and sorrow; he questions the magic garden, but its breezes cannot answer him; finally, he concludes that love is not that which lies upon the lips of men, and calls upon the **Cup-bearer** <note>original: "Saqi"; in Sufi poetry, the one who serves the wine of spiritual knowledge.</note> to silence their idle talk with the wine of divine knowledge.

*Stanza* 2.—The **Garden of Irem** <note>original: "Bagh-i-Iram"; a legendary paradise mentioned in the Quran, said to have been built by King Shaddad to mimic the celestial Heaven.</note> was planted by the mythical **King Shedad**, the son of Ad, the grandson of Irem, who was himself the son of **Shem** <note>In religious tradition, the son of Noah.</note>. The tribe of Ad settled in the sandy...

Immediately, the <term>Fari Yazdan</term> <note>original: "Fari Yazdan"; the Divine or Royal Glory, also known as the "Farr," which legitimizes the rule of Iranian kings.</note>—that is to say, the royal glory that comes from God—abandoned him; a three-headed serpent, named **Zohab** <note>Usually rendered as Zahhak or Azhi Dahaka, the demonic tyrant of Persian mythology.</note>, came from Arabia and took his throne; he fled to India and remained hidden there for a thousand years; then one fine day, having ventured out of his retreat, he was delivered to the serpent, who sawed him in two with a fish-bone. Among other splendors, King Jamshid, in the time of his glory, possessed a **magic cup** <note>Known as the Jām-e Jam, a cup in which the king could observe the entire universe.</note> where he saw the whole universe and everything that happened there. Jamshid is supposed to have built Persepolis <note>The ancient ceremonial capital of the Persian Empire; its ruins are still known in Persian as Takht-e Jamshid, or "The Throne of Jamshid."</note>. There is a legend that his cup was found buried in its foundations, and that it was formed of an enormous turquoise. It is said that he was the first to drink wine, and that he recommended it to his subjects as a health-giving beverage. He, too, was the father of chemistry and the possessor of the philosopher’s stone <term>the philosopher’s stone: a legendary substance believed by alchemists to be capable of turning base metals into gold or granting eternal life.</term>.

### III

*Stanza 1.*—King Solomon sent the lapwing or hoopoe <note>The hoopoe, or "Hudhud" in Arabic, appears in Islamic tradition as a sagacious messenger and a guide to hidden water sources.</note> as his messenger to Bilkis, Queen of Sheba. After which he said: Behave not insolently towards me, but come unto me humbled. And he strewed musk upon it and sealed it with his seal. Then he said to the lapwing: Fly with this letter and deliver it unto them, then turn away, but remain near them and hear what answer they make. And the lapwing took the letter and flew with it to Bilkis. *Stanzas* 5 and 6.—The accepted explanation of these lines is that by the glass Hafiz <note>Shams al-Din Muhammad Hafiz (c. 1325–1390), the premier lyric poet of Persia.</note> means his own heart, which he sends to his mistress that she may see that her own image is reflected in it; but I prefer here (and indeed for the whole poem) a mystical interpretation. The heavenly voice tells him to seek for comfort in Sufiism <term>Sufism: the mystical tradition of Islam focused on the direct, personal experience of God.</term>, and bids him look upon the mirror, for he shall see God himself reflected in it—which is only another way of putting the doctrine that man and God are one. The poet's reputation has gained him admittance into the company of the Sufis, let him hasten to them, for they shall give him that for which he seeks.

A horse and robe is the Eastern gift of honour. Lane <note>Edward William Lane, a 19th-century British scholar known for his translation of the "One Thousand and One Nights."</note> in one of his notes to the "Arabian Nights" quotes a significant story concerning these gifts: 

> "A person chancing to look at a register kept by one of the officers of Harun al Rashid <note>The famous fifth Abbasid Caliph of Baghdad (r. 786–809 AD).</note>, saw in it the following entry: '400,000 pieces of gold, the price of a dress of honour for Jafar ibn Yahya, the Vizir <note>Jafar the Barmakid, the Caliph's closest friend and minister who was later suddenly and brutally executed.</note>.' A few days after he saw beneath this written: 'Ten kerits <term>kerit (qirat): a very small unit of currency or weight, used here to show the Vizir's total fall from grace.</term>, the price of naphtha and reeds for burning the body of Jafar ibn Yahya.' (The kerit of Baghdad was worth a twentieth part of a gold piece.)

# NOTES

## IV

*Verse 3.*—The Persian poets often describe the dimple in the chin of their mistress as a dangerous well filled with her lover’s tears. It is a poetic trap where the lover, drawn in by the beauty of the lady's mouth, may figuratively fall and drown in his own sorrow.

*Verse 6.*—“Oh rose, tearing thy robe in two”: this refers to the flower bursting into bloom. In Persian imagery, the "robe" of the rose is its bud, which splits open under the influence of the warm spring breeze—a wind often personified as coming from the direction of the beloved.

## V

*Stanza 1.*—When the conqueror Timur <note>Better known in the West as Tamerlane (1336–1405), the Turkic-Mongol ruler who founded the Timurid Empire.</note> entered the city of Shiraz, it is said that he summoned Hafiz to appear before him. Timur questioned the poet: “With my sword I have subdued most of the world to embellish my capitals of Bokhara and Samarkand, the fairest jewels of my empire; how is it that in your verse you claim you would trade them both for the black mole on the cheek of your Shirazian mistress?” Hafiz reportedly replied: “It is because of such legendary generosity that I am now as poor as you see me.” The Emperor was so impressed by this witty retort that instead of punishing him, he sent the poet away with hundreds of gold pieces.

“It is Molière in reverse,” <note>original: "C’est du Molière renversé"</note> says Darmsteter <note>James Darmsteter (1849–1894), a prominent French author and researcher of Iranian studies.</note> regarding these lines, quoting a famous old French song:

> “If the king had given me
> Paris, his great city,
> And if I had to leave
> The love of my sweetheart,
> I would say to King Henri:
> Take back your Paris,
> I love my sweetheart better, oh hey!
> I love my sweetheart better!”
<note>This French song, "Si le roi m'avait donné," is famously quoted by Molière in his play *The Misanthrope* to show the superiority of simple, heartfelt emotion over complex courtly poetry.</note>

Hafiz is buried in the garden of Mosalla, a place he often praised; the stream of Ruknabad, another of his favorite landmarks, flows nearby.

*Stanza 2.*—The Luli <term>Luli: A term used in Persian poetry for musicians and dancers, often identified with the Roma or "gypsy" people.</term>, or gypsies as they were dismissively called, were a people of the tribe of Keredj, originally from India, who lived in the region between Shiraz and Isfahan. Their youths were renowned for their striking beauty and musical talents, and they frequently provided the singers and dancing girls for

### I

Sir Henry Layard <note>Austen Henry Layard (1817–1894) was a British archaeologist and traveler famous for uncovering the ruins of Nineveh.</note> met with a similar tribe near Baghdad. “They bear,” he says, “a very bad reputation on the score of morality, and according to general report lead very dissolute lives. The dancing boys and girls who frequent Baghdad, and are notoriously of evil fame, come principally from this district. Whilst we were resting at the caravanserai <note>A roadside inn where travelers and their animals could rest, typical of the Silk Road.</note> a party of them came to perform their indecent dances before us, as they were in the habit of doing on the arrival of travellers.”—*Early Adventures.*

In Turkestan there was formerly an institution called the **Feast of Plunder**. When the pay-day of the soldiers came round, dishes of rice and great quantities of cooked food were prepared and set out on the ground. The soldiers then rode up, armed as if for battle, and carried off the food with mimic violence. Thus they made reparation to their conscience for accepting a pay lawfully earned, and reminded themselves that rapine was their true profession.

*Stanza 3.*—**Joseph** is the Oriental type of perfect beauty. The story of his relations with **Zuleikha**, Potiphar’s wife, is one of the famous love stories of the East; Jami <note>Nur ad-Din Abd ar-Rahman Jami (1414–1492), often considered the last great classical Persian poet.</note> made it the theme of a long metaphysical poem. The part played by Zuleikha in Persian tales is far more creditable than that which is assigned to her either in the Bible or the Koran <note>In Persian literature, Zuleikha’s obsession is often depicted as a spiritual journey from physical lust to divine love.</note>.

Every translator of Hafiz has tried his hand upon this song, which is one of the most famous in the <term>Divan<gloss>The collected works of a Persian poet, usually arranged alphabetically by the final letter of the rhyme.</gloss></term>. It is only right to inform the reader that the original is of great beauty.

The whole poem has received a mystical interpretation which seems to me to add but little to its value or to its intelligibility; but in case any one should wish to gather the higher wisdom from it, I may mention that the mole, powder, and paint, of which a beautiful face does not stand in need, represent the ink, colour, dots, and lines of the Koran; and this is the explanation given to the couplet concerning Joseph and Zuleikha by a thorough-going Western mystic: “By reason of that beauty daily increasing that Joseph (the absolute existence, the real beloved, God) had, I (the first day) knew that love for him would bring Zuleikha (us, things possible) forth from the screen of chastity (the pure existence of God).” The learned translator seems to have felt that his version presented some...

## VII

*Stanza 1.*—Those who have seen a Persian garden will not find it difficult to understand why it should play so large a part in Persian poetry. Often enough you may pass with one step out of a barren desert of dust and stones into one of these green and fertile spots, full of violets in the spring, and of roses and lilies in the early summer; and from the blinding glare of a Persian sun into a cool and shadowy retreat planted with great plane-trees. The water which flows in numberless streams through the garden, and leaps in countless fountains, has worked all the miracle. The change from desert to flowery paradise is one of those strong contrasts so common in the East which take hold of the imagination of all who see them.

*Stanza 3.*—That is, do not attempt to light the torches of a <term>Mahommadan monastery</term> <note>An archaic term for a Muslim Sufi lodge, known as a khanaqah or zawiya.</note> from the lamp of a Jewish synagogue. One of the most famous of the Prophet’s sayings is: there is no monasticism in Islam. <note>original: "la rahbaniyya fi-l-islam"</note> Nevertheless, from the time of Abu Bekr <note>The first Caliph and a close companion of the Prophet Muhammad.</note> and Ali <note>The cousin and son-in-law of the Prophet, and a central figure in Sufi lineages.</note> onwards, such religious associations grew up and flourished. Nearly all the celebrated doctors of whom the <term>Sufis</term> <note>Followers of the mystical dimension of Islam who seek direct personal experience of God.</note> boast in the first six hundred years after the <term>Hejra</term> <note>The migration of Muhammad from Mecca to Medina in 622 CE, marking the start of the Islamic calendar.</note> belonged to them.

> “Verily our messengers write down that which ye deceitfully devise,” says the <term>Koran</term> <note>The Quran, Chapter 10 (Sura Yunus), Verse 21.</note> (chap. x.). Two guardian angels attend every man and write down his actions; they are changed daily and a fresh pair takes their place. The books which they have written shall be produced on the Day of Judgment.

*Stanza 4.*—It was this verse which decided the right of <term>Hafiz</term> <note>The 14th-century poet whose tomb in Shiraz remains a major site of pilgrimage.</note> to receive honourable burial. <note>According to tradition, orthodox clerics initially refused Hafiz a religious burial due to his poems about wine and love. They eventually consulted his own poems for an omen; the verse they drew was so pious it shamed them into allowing the funeral.</note>

<summary>This page provides historical and mystical context for Hafiz's poetry, explaining the significance of the Persian garden as a symbol of paradise and the origins of Sufi monasticism. It also references the Quranic concept of recording angels and the legend of Hafiz's contested burial.</summary>
<keywords>Hafiz, Persian gardens, Sufism, Quran, Abu Bakr, Ali, Hegira, spiritual symbolism, Shiraz</keywords>

# NOTES

->VIII<-

**Stanza 3.**---When God had created man and made him wiser than the angels, he bound him to himself by a solemn treaty. “Am I not thy Lord who has created thee?” he demanded, and man answered “Yes.” <note>original: *bala*. This refers to the "Covenant of Alast" in Islamic theology, based on Quran 7:172, where all human souls acknowledged God's sovereignty before the world was created.</note> But the Arabic word *bala*, which signifies assent, means also sorrow, <note>In Arabic, *bala* (بلى) means "yes," but the phonetically similar *bala'* (بلاء) means "trial" or "affliction." Persian poets often use this wordplay to suggest that accepting the gift of life and the love of God is to accept a burden of suffering.</note> and they say that the first of our fathers knew full well what a terrible gift was that life which he had received from his Lord, and sealed the treaty with a seal of grief. Therefore since the earliest day, life and sorrow have gone hand in hand, bound together by the first great pact between God and man.

**Stanza 4.**---Compare <term>François Villon’s</term> <note>A 15th-century French poet known for his reflections on the transience of life.</note> rough and powerful treatment of the same theme:---

> Where are the <term>Dauphin</term> <note>The title of the heir apparent to the French throne.</note> of Vienne and of Grenoble,
> The brave, and the wise?
> Where are the lords and the eldest sons
> Of Dijon, Salins, and Dole?
> Where are their many private retainers,
> Heralds, trumpeters, and pursuivants?
> Have they well stuffed their noses? . . .
> The wind carries them all away!
> <note>original: "Où sont de Vienne et de Grenobles / Le Dauphin, les preux, les senés? / Où de Dijon, Sallin et Dolles, / Les sires et les fils aînés? / Où autant de leurs gens privés, / Hérauts, trompettes, poursuivants? / Ont-ils bien bouté sous le nez? . . . / Autant en emporte le vent!" This is from Villon's "Ballade des seigneurs du temps jadis" (Ballad of the Lords of Olden Times).</note>

Solomon, the type of human greatness, is the King whose mastery has left nothing behind. He harnessed the wind as a steed to his chariot, he spoke with the birds in their own tongue, and the wise and magnificent <term>Assaf</term> <note>Asaph, traditionally identified in Islamic lore as King Solomon’s vizier or grand wazir.</note> was his minister. Upon his seal was engraved the name of God which is unknown to men and before which the <term>Jinn</term> <note>Spirits or genies in Islamic mythology, created from smokeless fire.</note> and the Angels must bow down. It was with this seal that he fastened up the bottles in which he imprisoned the Jinn---those bottles which the fishermen in the “Arabian Nights” pull up in their nets.

->IX<-

**Stanza 1.**---This poem is addressed to the <term>Vizir</term> <note>A high-ranking political advisor or minister.</note> of Sultan Oweis of Baghdad, <term>Hadji Kawameddin</term>, <note>Qiwam al-Din Abdullah, a patron of the arts and a friend to Hafiz.</note> who founded a college for <term>Hafiz</term> <note>The 14th-century lyric poet of Shiraz whose works are being annotated here.</term> in <term>Shiraz</term>. <note>A city in southwestern Iran, famous as a center of Persian culture and the home of Hafiz.</note> With true Persian exaggeration the poet

must needs write to his patron much in the same terms in which a lover would write to his mistress ; but his words, though they sound strangely to our ears, are nothing more than the Oriental way of saying, “Awake, my St. John!” <note>This is a reference to the opening line of Alexander Pope’s *Essay on Man* (1733), addressed to his patron Henry St. John: "Awake, my St. John! leave all meaner things / To low ambition, and the pride of kings." The author is explaining that Hafiz's seemingly romantic language toward his patron is actually a conventional expression of respect and intellectual partnership.</note>

The mystical interpretation of the first few lines is said to be : 
> As the wine glows in the cup like the reflection of a ruddy cheek, so in the goblet of my heart I have seen the reflection of God, the true Beloved.

*Stanza* 6.—It is related that upon a certain occasion when Hafiz was feasting with the <term>Vizir</term> <note>A high-ranking political advisor or minister.</note> in the latter’s garden, a servant handed to him a goblet of wine, and as he took it he saw in it the reflection of the crescent moon overhead. The incident suggested this verse to him. I should say that the anecdote was of doubtful authenticity.

->X<-

This song is not to be found in the best editions of the <term>Divan</term> <note>The *Divan* (ديوان) is the collected volume of a poet's work, traditionally arranged alphabetically by the final letter of each rhyme.</note>, and is believed to be spurious ; but it is printed in most of the popular editions, and is as widely known as any of the poems which pass with a better right under the name of Hafiz. It is set to a soft and well-nigh tuneless air which sounds like dream music, or the echo of something very beautiful coming from a great distance, the singer ending on an almost whispered repetition of the first exquisite phrase. <note>The author is likely referring to the famous poem "Taza ba Taza, Nau ba Nau" (Fresh and Fresh, New and New), which is immensely popular in India and often attributed to Hafiz despite scholarly skepticism.</note> I have been told that the boatmen on the Ganges sing it as they row, and the monotonous accompaniment of the water under the oars must be even more fitting to the melody than that of the lute strings.

->XI<-

*Stanza* 2.—I have found no explanation of these difficult lines, and, for want of a better, I venture to suggest the following : the Garden of Irem, as has been said in the Note to Poem II., was a mimic Paradise constructed by a certain fabulous King Shedad, who wished to be considered a rival to his Maker by his fellows, for which temerity a swift and sharp judgment fell. <note>The Garden of Irem (Iram) and King Shedad (Shaddad ibn 'Ad) are figures from Islamic and Arabian folklore. Shaddad built a magnificent garden to surpass Heaven, but he and his people were destroyed by a divine scream before he could enter it. It is a common poetic symbol for human pride and the fragility of worldly power.</note>

<meta>This page continues the scholarly commentary on the odes of Hafiz, specifically completing the notes for poem XIII and beginning the analysis for poem XIV. It maintains the previous page's focus on interpreting Persian poetic metaphors for a Western audience.</meta>

<page-num>133</page-num>

# NOTES

upon him; the River of Life <note>original: *Nahr al-Hayat*</note> is one of the many streams which waters the divine Paradise. To my thinking, Hafiz takes the one as a type of the wildest human ambition, the other as a part of the most beautiful vision which the mind of man has conceived. And to what does it all amount? he asks. Only to this: that we are like to one who sits and dreams upon the banks of a mighty and resistless river, fed from many sources, and sings, if he be wise, his song of praise, and so departs.

*Stanza* 4.—The river <term>Kausar</term> <note>From the Arabic *al-Kawthar*, meaning "Abundance"; it is a river in Paradise promised to the Prophet in the Quran.</note> is another of the streams of Paradise; indeed, it is said to be the central spring from whence all the others flow. A part of its waters are led into a great square lake, a month’s journey in compass. On the banks of this lake the souls of good Muslims <note>original: "Mahommadans"</note> rest and find refreshment after they have crossed the terrible bridge <note>Known as *As-Sirat*, this bridge is said to be thinner than a hair and sharper than a sword's edge, spanning the fires of Hell.</note>, sharper than the edge of a sword, which is laid over the midst of Hell. The waters of the lake are whiter than silver and sweeter than musk. Round it are set as many cups as there are stars in the firmament, and he who has drunk of it shall thirst no more.

->XIV<-

*Stanza* 1.—Hafiz wrote this poem upon the death of his son.

*Stanza* 3.—<term>Rosenzweig</term> <note>Vincenz Ritter von Rosenzweig-Schwannau (1791–1865), an Austrian diplomat and scholar who produced a major three-volume translation of Hafiz’s work into German.</note>, in his edition of the <term>Divan</term> <note>A *Divan* (ديوان) is the collected volume of a poet's work, usually organized alphabetically by the rhyme of the poem.</note>, says that the allusion is to the dust and water which God kneaded into the body of Adam, and that, out of derision, Hafiz calls the human body a "house of joy."
The moon, according to Persian superstition, has a baneful influence upon human life.

*Stanza* 4.—Rosenzweig says that “I had not castled” <note>A metaphor from the game of chess, referring to the move that protects the King.</note> means that Hafiz had not taken the precaution of marrying his son, and so securing for himself grandchildren who would have been a consolation to him on their father’s death. For that reason he had nothing more to lose, and was indifferent as to what his next move in the game should be.
