<header>NOTES</header>

->XV<-

*Stanza 3.*—“Night is with child”—this is a Persian proverb that is extraordinarily suggestive of the clear, deep, Eastern sky. To the observer, the sight seems to slip through the gaps between the stars and penetrate a darkness which is heavy with hidden possibilities.

->XVI<-

*Stanza 2.*—These lines are exceedingly mysterious, as, indeed, is the whole poem. I have searched for an explanation of them in other editions of *Hafiz*, but have found little more than a simple translation of the original Persian words. For the deeper meaning of this stanza, see the Introduction, p. 56.

<term>Sidreh</term> <note>The *Lote-tree of the Extremity*, marking the boundary of the seventh heaven beyond which no creature can pass.</note> and <term>Tuba</term> <note>The *Tree of Bliss* in Islamic tradition.</note> are two trees in the Garden of Paradise. The former is the dwelling place of the angel <term>Gabriel</term>. Concerning the latter, <term>Sale</term> <note>George Sale (1697–1736), an English orientalist and the first to produce an influential English translation of the Quran.</note> says: “They tell stories that it stands in the palace of Muhammad, though a branch of it will reach to the house of every true believer; that it will be laden with pomegranates, grapes, dates, and other fruits of surprising size, and of tastes unknown to mortals. So that if a man desires to eat of any particular kind of fruit, it will immediately be presented to him; or if he chooses meat, birds ready dressed will be set before him, according to his wish. They add that the boughs of this tree will spontaneously bend down to the hand of the person who would gather its fruits, and that it will supply the blessed not only with food, but also with silken garments and beasts to ride on, ready saddled and bridled and adorned with rich trappings, which will burst forth from its fruits; and that this tree is so large that a person mounted on the fastest horse would not be able to gallop from one end of its shade to the other in a hundred years.”—*Introduction to the Koran.*

*Stanza 4.*—He means either that the descent to the underworld is easy <note>original: *facilis descensus Averni*; a famous line from Virgil’s *Aeneid* suggesting that the path to destruction or the grave is effortless.</note>, or, more probably, that a great number of those whom the strict religious authorities view with suspicion will be found to have an equal claim to a heavenly reward, since the distinction between a <term>Sufi</term> <note>A practitioner of Islamic mysticism, often emphasizing internal spiritual experience over legalistic ritual.</note> and the orthodox is, in reality, nothing at all.

<header>NOTES</header>

Stanza 5.—"The lovers of wine"—that is to say the <term>Sufis</term> <note>Islamic mystics who used the imagery of wine and intoxication to represent divine love and spiritual ecstasy.</note>, who will be equally indifferent whether he comes to them with or without trailing clouds of human approbation, since they will judge of his worth by a different standard.

->XVII<-

Stanza 3.—The allusion is to the expulsion of Adam from the Garden of Eden.

Stanza 4.—Concerning the Last Judgment, a beautiful tradition relates that there are seven degrees of punishment, but eight of blessedness, because God's mercy exceeds His justice.

->XVIII<-

Stanza 1.—Blue is the Persian colour of mourning. Hafiz compares the weeping lovers, clad in robes of grief, to a bed of violets, and as the violets bow their heads when the wind passes over them, so they bow down when their mistress passes by with flowing curls.

Stanza 3.—"<term>Erghwan</term>," <note>original: "Erghwan"</note> the <note>original Latin: "Syringa Persica"</note> Persian lilac. In the early spring, before it comes into leaf, it is covered with buds of a beautiful reddish-purple colour.

"<term>Khizr</term>," <note>original: "Khizr," also known as Al-Khidr, the "Green One."</note> a prophet whom the Muslims <note>The original text uses "Mahommadans," an archaic term for followers of Islam.</note> associate with the biblical figures of Phineas and Elias, as well as St. George, saying that his soul passed by <term>metempsychosis</term> <note>The process of the soul passing into another body after death; reincarnation.</note> successively through all three. He discovered the fountain of life and drank of it, thereby making himself immortal. It is said that he guided Alexander the Great to the same fountain, which lay in the Land of Darkness. It was he, too, for whom Moses set out to seek when he had been informed by God that Al-Khizr was wiser than he. He found him seated on a rock, at the meeting of the two seas, and followed him for a time, learning wisdom from him, as is related in the eighteenth chapter of the <term>Koran</term> <note>The central religious text of Islam.</note>. His name signifies Green; wherever his feet rested, the earth was covered with green herbs.

Hafiz looked upon the prophet Al-Khizr as one of his special...

guardians. About four <term>Persian miles</term> <note>Original: "farsakh," a traditional unit of distance roughly equal to 3.7 miles or 6 kilometers.</note> from Shiraz there is a spot called <term>Pir-i-Sabz</term>, the Old Green Man; whosoever should pass forty nights in it without sleeping, on the fortieth night <term>Al Khizr</term> <note>The immortal "Green Prophet" who discovered the Water of Life; he is considered a patron of travelers and a source of mystical inspiration.</note> would appear to him and confer upon him the immortal gift of song. Hafiz in his youth fell in love with a beautiful girl of Shiraz called <term>Shakh-i-Nahat</term> <note>Literally "Branch of Sugar-cane," a poetic name for his beloved.</note>, and in order to win her heart he determined to meet Al Khizr and receive from him the art of poetry. For thirty-nine mornings he walked beneath the windows of Shakh-i-Nahat, at noon he ate, then he slept, and at night he kept watch, undismayed by the terrible apparition of a fierce lion which was his nightly companion. At length, on the fortieth morning, Shakh-i-Nahat called him into her house and told him that she was ready to become his wife, for she preferred a man of genius to the son of a king. She would have kept him with her, but Hafiz, though he had gained his original end, was now filled with desire to become a poet, and insisted upon keeping his fortieth vigil. That night an old man dressed in green garments came to him and brought him a cup of the water of immortality.

### XIX

*Stanza 2.*—See Note to Stanza 1 of Poem III.

*Stanza 5.*—“Narrow-eyedness” is the exact translation of the Persian word for greed <note>The Persian term "tang-chashmi" literally means "narrow-eyed," serving as a pun on the physical features of the Mongol invaders and the moral quality of avarice.</note>, and there is consequently, in the original, a play of meaning between the physical and moral attributes of the Tartars.

It is significant that Hafiz should choose the “narrow-eyed” <term>Tartar</term> <note>A historical collective term for the Mongol and Turkic tribes who invaded Persia.</note> robbers as types of cruelty. Just as the Anglo-Saxons prayed to be delivered from the Danes, so a clause in the Persian litany of the thirteenth and fourteenth centuries might have been: “From the power of the Tartars, good Lord, deliver us!” First under <term>Hulagu</term> <note>Hulagu Khan (c. 1218–1265), the Mongol ruler who conquered Baghdad and established the Ilkhanate in Persia.</note>, and then under <term>Timur</term> <note>Also known as Tamerlane (1336–1405), the Turkic-Mongol conqueror whose campaigns caused immense destruction across Central Asia and Persia.</note>, they overran and devastated Persia. The destruction wrought by them was very similar to that wrought by the Arab conquerors in the Roman provinces of North Africa. They rased to the ground great cities; they reduced populous and fertile regions to a barren desert by breaking down the old reservoirs and destroying the

irrigating system, completely changing the physical conditions of parts of the country. In the mountains to the north of Tehran, for instance, there are villages bearing names the etymology of which points to their having stood at the outlet of a reservoir of which no other trace remains, and it is said that the country surrounding the town was far more thoroughly irrigated before the <term>Tartar invasion</term> <note>Refers to the Mongol invasions of the 13th century, led by Genghis Khan and his successors, which caused widespread destruction to Persia's infrastructure.</note>, and supported a larger population. The invaders completely destroyed the ancient city of <term>Rhages</term> <note>Now known as Rey, an ancient and once-magnificent city located on the southern outskirts of modern-day Tehran.</note>, which lay at a distance of about three miles from the modern capital. The same thing happened in North Africa. The ruins of Roman towns are to be found in country which must once have been fertile, but which is now reconquered by the sands of the Sahara.

“One poor robe.” The Persian runs: <note>original: "man dervish-i-yek kaba"</note> “I, a poor man of one robe”—*dervish* signifying in its primary sense, it is hardly necessary to say, *poor*. I should think that the double meaning is significant. In its mystical sense, the poem describes how Hafiz found consolation in the ecstatic drunkenness of the <term>Sufis</term> <note>Followers of the mystical branch of Islam who seek a direct, personal experience of God.</note>, in the minstrel’s song, or divine message, which brought him a word from God; and when finally the last shred of his orthodoxy had been torn from him, when in his desperate struggle with existence he was forced to abandon even his <term>dervish robe</term> <note>The "khirqa," a patched cloak traditionally worn by Sufis as a symbol of their poverty and commitment to the spiritual path.</note>, Heaven mercifully showed him a safe refuge in the Sufi doctrines.

->XXI<-

*Stanza 1.*—Sir Henry Layard <note>A famous 19th-century British archaeologist and diplomat, best known for excavating the ruins of Nineveh.</note> gives the following account of a party of dervishes with whom he travelled, from which it would appear that the contempt of Hafiz for the dervish habit was not wholly uncalled for: “They were a picturesque and motley crew. One or two of them were what the Persians call <term>luti</term> <note>In this context, dissolute or roguish characters who lived on the fringes of society, often feigning religious devotion.</note>, young men with well-dyed curls, long garments, and conical caps embroidered in many colours—debauched and dissolute fellows, who, under the guise of poverty, and affecting abstinence and piety, were given to every manner of vice. Others were half-naked savages, with hair hanging down their backs, and the skins of gazelles on their shoulders—barefooted, dirty, and covered with vermin. They carried heavy

<meta>This page continues the commentary on the poetry of Hafiz, specifically discussing the social and religious identity of the dervish in Persian society as a context for understanding the poet's metaphors.</meta>

<page-num>138</page-num>

# NOTES

iron maces, and seemed more disposed to exact than to ask for charity. As they went along they shouted **“O God! O Muhammad! O Ali!”** <note>original: "Yah Allah ! yah Muhammad ! yah Ali !" — invocations of God and the founding figures of the Shia faith.</note> They all had slung from their shoulders the carved cocoa-nut shell, which is indispensable to the dervish, and serves for carrying food and for drinking purposes. Round their necks they wore charms and amulets, with beads and coloured strings and tassels.” He goes on to say: “Most Persian dervishes, although they have great pretensions to sanctity, by which they impose upon the people, high and low, are without any religion. They are, however, credited with working miracles, and with being able to give efficacious charms. . . . Although these dervishes are rank impostors, and generally arrant scoundrels, they maintain their influence over the ignorant and superstitious Persians of all classes, who greatly fear, and do not dare to offend them. Consequently no one ventures to refuse them admission into their houses, and even into the women’s apartments, where those who go stark-naked, and are looked upon as specially holy and protected by **God** and **Ali**, can enter with impunity. Sometimes they will demand a specific sum of money from a rich man, and if he refuses to pay it, will establish themselves in the gateway or porch of his dwelling, or outside close to it, and enclosing a small plot of ground, sow wheat or plant flowers, and remain until what they ask for is paid them, hooting hideously day and night, calling upon Mohammad, Ali, and the <term>Imams</term> <note>The twelve divinely appointed leaders in Shia Islam, revered as spiritual and political successors to Muhammad.</note>, or blowing on a buffalo’s horn so as to disturb the whole neighbourhood. The owner and inmates of the house are helpless. They do not dare to remove by force the holy men.”—*Early Adventures*.

*Stanza 2*.—That is to say, the prayer-carpet of the orthodox **Muslim** <note>original: "Mussulman"</note> had not enough value to procure for him so much as one glass of **mystic wine** <note>original: "Sufi wine" — a metaphor for divine love or spiritual intoxication.</note>. Nor was he worthy to lay his head even upon the dusty steps of the tavern—the place of instruction, in <term>Sufi</term> <note>The mystical dimension of Islam focused on the inner search for God and the renunciation of worldly vanities.</note> doctrine.

*Stanza 3*.—To be clothed in one colour is the Persian idiom for sincerity. He means that the single purple robe of the grape is worth more than the hypocritical garment of the dervish, all torn and patched with long journeying—in the wrong road.

<meta>This page continues the commentary on the poetry of Hafiz, shifting from the general social status of dervishes to a specific historical anecdote that explains the real-world circumstances behind the composition of one of his poems.</meta>

# NOTES

*Stanza 5.*—So far I have endeavoured to give the mystical interpretation of the poem. There is, however, a story attached to it which turns it into a historical rather than a theological document. It is related that the King of the <term>Deccan</term> <note>A large plateau region in southern India; at this time, it was ruled by the Bahmani Sultanate, a center of Persianate culture.</note>, Mahmud Shah Bahmani, had heard of the fame of Hafiz, and having a pretty taste in literature, was desirous of attracting him to his court. Accordingly he ordered his <term>Vizier</term> <note>A high-ranking political advisor or minister.</note>, Mir Feiz Allah Inju, to send the poet a sufficient sum to pay for his journey from <term>Shiraz</term> <note>A city in south-central Iran, world-renowned as the center of Persian poetry and gardens.</note>. Hafiz resolved to accept the invitation. He wound up his affairs in his native town, using some of the money the Sultan had sent him in paying his debts and in making gifts to his sister's children, and set forth upon his journey. But when he reached the town of Lar he found there an acquaintance in very bad case, having been plundered by robbers and reduced to a state of beggary. Hafiz was moved to compassion and gave him the remainder of the money which Mahmud Shah had sent to him.

He was now himself unable to continue his journey for want of means, and perhaps it was bitter experience that taught him that in very fact his prayer-carpet would not fetch him a glass of wine <note>A reference to a common metaphor in Hafiz's poetry where the poet's religious identity—symbolized by the prayer rug—is of less value than the "wine" of spiritual truth or earthly relief.</note>, and that without the necessary silver pieces he would be thrust from out the tavern doors. From these straits he was rescued by two friendly merchants, who were also on their way to India, and who offered to pay his expenses to <term>Hormuz</term> <note>An essential port city on the Persian Gulf, serving as the gateway for trade between Persia and India.</note>, and there place him on a vessel of Mahmud Shah's which was coming to fetch them. Hafiz accepted the offer, went to Hormuz, and embarked on the ship. But before they had left the port a violent storm arose, and persuaded the poet that no advantages he might reap from the journey would be worth the sorrow of the sea. Under pretext of bidding farewell to some friends, he disembarked, and in all haste made the best of his way back to Shiraz, sending to Feiz Allah this poem as an excuse for failing to keep his engagement. The Vizier read it to Mahmud Shah, who was transported by the beauty of the verses and the philosophic dignity in which Hafiz had cloaked his fears of the dangers of the road and the discomforts of sea-sickness. With singular generosity he sent the defaulting poet a further present, consisting of some at least of the riches of his lands and seas.

<summary>This page recounts a historical anecdote where the poet Hafiz attempts to travel to India to visit the Bahmani Sultan, but ultimately turns back due to a storm, sending a poem instead to explain his change of heart.</summary>
<keywords>Hafiz, Mahmud Shah Bahmani, Shiraz, Hormuz, Vizier, Persian poetry, Indian-Persian relations, travel anecdotes</keywords>

### XXIII.

This poem is said to have been written by Hafiz upon the death of his wife.

### XXIV

*Stanza 5.*—Shah Shudja <note>The Mozaffarid ruler of Shiraz (reigned 1358–1384) who was a patron, and sometimes a critic, of Hafiz.</note>, as has been related in the Introduction, was not always on the best of terms with Hafiz, partly because he was jealous of the latter’s fame as a poet, and partly because Hafiz had been the protégé of Shah Shudja’s former rival, Abu Ishac <note>The Injuid ruler of Shiraz whom Shah Shudja’s father overthrew.</note>. Accordingly the King looked about for some means of doing the poet an injury, nor was it long before he found what he sought. He accused Hafiz of denying the <term>Resurrection</term> <note>The Islamic concept of the Day of Judgment and the bodily rising of the dead, known as *Ma'ad*.</note>, basing the accusation upon the last couplet of this poem—the last three lines of the present translation—and cited him before the <term>Ulema</term> <note>The collective body of Muslim scholars specialized in Islamic law and theology.</note> as an infidel. But Hafiz was too many for him. Before the day on which he was to answer the charge against himself, he inserted another couplet into the ode, in which he stated that the dangerous lines did not express his own opinion, but that of a heretical Christian. He came off with flying colours; for not only was he entirely cleared, but it was also acknowledged that he had dealt a good blow on behalf of the Islamic <note>original: "Mahommadan"</note> religion, since he had shown up one of the errors of the infidel.

### XXV

*Stanza 1.*—There are many ways of taking omens which are still practised by the Persians. Concerning astrology and <term>geomancy</term> <note>A method of divination that interprets markings on the ground or the patterns formed by tossed handfuls of soil, rocks, or sand; in Persian, this is called *raml*.</note> Mr. Browne <note>Likely Edward Granville Browne (1862–1926), the preeminent British orientalist and historian of Persian literature.</note> questioned a learned Persian, and received the reply that there was positive proof of their truth. The Persian added, however, that the study of these sciences was very difficult, and many who professed to be acquainted with them were mere charlatans. Many dreams also, he said, were capable of interpretation, and might furnish indications to events which were yet to come. Mr. Browne relates that he consulted a geomancer, who, by means of dice, gave him much information as to his future—none of which has yet been justified by the event—but on being asked to perform the less

<summary>This page provides historical and cultural context for three of Hafiz's poems, detailing a narrow escape from a heresy charge involving Shah Shudja and explaining Persian traditions of divination and geomancy.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the commentary on Persian customs and literary allusions, following a discussion on taking omens (geomancy) mentioned at the end of the previous page.</meta>

<page-num>141</page-num>

->NOTES<-

difficult task of answering some questions as to his past, turned the conversation into other channels. “I discussed,” says the traveller <note>Edward Granville Browne (1862–1926), a famous British orientalist, in his book *A Year Amongst the Persians*.</note>, “the occult sciences with several of my friends, to discover as far as possible the prevailing opinion among them. One of them made use of the following argument to prove their existence: ‘God,’ he said, ‘has no <term>avarice</term> <note>original: *bukhl*; the concept that God is not stingy with His knowledge or power.</note>; it is impossible for Him to withhold from any one a thing for which he strives with sufficient earnestness. Just as if a man devotes all his energies to the pursuit of spiritual knowledge he attains to it, so if he chooses to make occult sciences and magical powers the object of his aspirations they will assuredly not be withheld from him.’” —*A Year Amongst the Persians*.

An omen <note>The Persian practice of *fal*, or seeking an augury to guide future actions.</note> can be taken by opening the Koran or some other well-accredited book (the *Divan* of Hafiz among the number), pricking a pin into the page, and following whatever directions can be drawn from the verse thus indicated. This method is frequently used before setting out upon a journey. The stars also are consulted in order to select a favourable day for embarking upon any enterprise, certain stars having special influence over men—the influence of the moon, for instance, is dangerous to life, and one of the stars in the constellation of Cassiopea is of evil presage. Besides these omens, divinations are taken from the movements and position of certain animals and birds, and from various passing events. To meet a one-eyed man is of bad omen, especially if he is blind of the left eye, or to hear an unlucky word on setting out from your house of a morning. Lane <note>Edward William Lane, a British orientalist known for his translation of the *One Thousand and One Nights*.</note>, in one of his notes to the “Arabian Nights,” tells of a Sultan who was setting out on a raid, when one of his standards happening to strike against a cluster (or <term>Pleiades</term> <note>original: *al-Thurayya* in Arabic; a prominent star cluster in the constellation Taurus.</note>, as they are called in Arabic) of lamps, he regarded this to be of evil import, and was about to abandon the expedition. “Oh our lord!” said one of his officers, “our standards have reached the Pleiades.” The Sultan, encouraged by this fortunate suggestion, continued on his way, and returned victorious.

### XXVI

Stanza 2.—For <term>Jamshid</term> <note>original: "Djemshid"; a mythical Great King of ancient Persia who ruled during a golden age.</note>, see Note to Stanza 2 of Poem II. He was the fourth king of the First or <term>Pishdadian dynasty</term> <note>The first legendary dynasty of the Persian people as recorded in the *Shahnameh* (Book of Kings).</note>, and is supposed to have flourished eight hundred years before the

<meta>This page continues the commentary on Persian historical and literary figures, transitioning from the discussion of omens and occult sciences to the specific legendary and historical personages mentioned in the poetry being annotated.</meta>

<page-num>142</page-num>

->NOTES<-

Christian era. Firdusi <note>original: *Ferdowsi*; the 10th-century poet who composed the *Shahnameh* (Book of Kings), the national epic of Greater Iran.</note> says he reigned seven hundred years. Kaikobad was the founder of the Second dynasty, the Kayanian. He was set upon the throne by the hero Rustum <note>The most famous legendary hero in Persian mythology, often compared to Hercules.</note>, son of Zal. It was in his reign that Rustum overcame Afrasiab’s army, killing his own son <note>This refers to Sohrab; the two fought without knowing each other's identity until it was too late.</note> in the battle “by the great Oxus stream, the yellow Oxus,” a story which all readers of Matthew Arnold <note>The Victorian poet who popularized the story in the West with his 1853 poem *Sohrab and Rustum*.</note> know. Kaikobad is said to have reigned one hundred and twenty years. Bahman, another member of the Kayanian house, is better known to the Persians as Ardisher Dirazdast, the Artaxerxes the Long-Handed <note>original: *Longimanus*; a Greek epithet for the Persian king Artaxerxes I, who was said to have a right hand longer than his left.</note> of the Greeks. He came to the throne in B.C. 464. He was the grandson of Darius, the Persian Gushtasp. He is supposed to have been the Ahasuerus of Scripture <note>The king mentioned in the biblical Book of Esther.</note> who married Esther. Persian historians ascribe to him also remarkable longevity, and state that he reigned one hundred and twelve years. Kaikaus, mentioned in the next stanza, was the son of Kaikobad, second king of the Kayanian dynasty; Kai <note>A royal title meaning "great" or "king."</note> may be Kaikhusro, the third king of the same dynasty.

*Stanza 3.*—The loves of Ferhad and Shirin are famous in Persian legend. Shirin is called by some Mary, and by others Irene. The Greeks describe her as a Roman by birth and a Christian; the Turks and the Persians say that she was a daughter of the Emperor Maurice, and wife of Khusro Parwiz <note>Khosrow II, the last great Sasanian King of Persia before the Muslim conquest.</note>, who came to the Persian throne in A.D. 591. It was Khusro Parwiz who conquered Jerusalem, and carried off, say the Persians, the true Cross, which had been enclosed in a gold box and buried in the ground. He was devotedly attached to his wife Shirin, but she had given her heart to her humble lover Ferhad. He, despairing of ever reaching one whose rank had placed her so far above him, wandered through the deserts and the mountains of Persia calling upon her name, and in order to beguile his weary hours executed the sculptures upon the rock Behistun <note>A massive rock relief and inscription located in Iran, famous for helping scholars decipher cuneiform script.</note>—so says the legend. At length the King sent to him and told him that if he would cut through the rock and cause a stream upon the other side of the mountains to flow through it, he would relinquish Shirin to him. Ferhad set himself to the task, and had almost accomplished it when Khusro sent him the false news of Shirin’s death. On hearing it, Ferhad threw himself from the top of the rock and so died.

# NOTES

Shirin’s <note>The Christian queen of the Sassanid Empire and heroine of many Persian romances.</note> end was scarcely less tragic. Khusro Parwiz <note>Khosrow II (reigned 590–628 AD), the last great Sassanid king of Persia.</note> was put to a violent death by his son <note>Siroes, also known as Qubad II.</note>, who proceeded to make proposals of marriage to his father’s widow. Shirin promised to marry him if he would allow her to see once more her husband’s corpse. She was led to the place where the murdered King lay, and drawing a dagger, she stabbed herself and fell dead across his body.

It is difficult to conceive anything more exquisite than the little scarlet tulip <note>The *laleh* or tulip is a pervasive symbol in Persian poetry, often representing the blood of a lover or a martyr.</note> growing upon a barren Persian hillside. On the top of a bleak pass over the mountains between Resht <note>Rasht, a city near the Caspian Sea.</note> and Tehran, I have seen companies of tiny tulips shining like jewels among the dust and stones.

There is a tradition that this poem was sent to the King of Golconda <note>A wealthy sultanate in the Deccan region of India; the poet Hafiz was famously invited to the courts of Indian rulers.</note>.

## XXVIII

*Stanza* 1.—According to Oriental belief, Jesus Christ’s gift of healing was due to a miraculous quality in His breath <note>original: *Dam-i-Isa*; in Persian literature, the "Breath of Jesus" is a common metaphor for a life-giving or healing influence.</note>.

## XXIX

*Stanza* 3.—Maghilan <note>The *Mughilan* or desert acacia, a thorny shrub.</note>, a thorny shrub which grows on the deserts of Arabia near to Mecca. When the pilgrims <note>Those performing the Hajj pilgrimage to Mecca.</note> see it they know that they have almost reached their goal, and forget the hardships of the journey and the barrenness of the wastes through which their road lies.

## XXX

*Stanza* 2.—The quarter of Jafrabad has ceased to exist. Its position was to the east of the town, opposite to the fields and to the ruined mosque of Mosalla. Between Jafrabad and Mosalla runs the highroad to Isfahan, traversing, at the distance of a mile from Shiraz, the pass of Allahu Akbar <note>Literally "God is Great"; the pass is so named because travelers catching their first glimpse of the beautiful city of Shiraz from this height would instinctively cry out the *takbir*.</note>.

The angel Gabriel <note>Known in Islam as Jibra'il.</note>, the Holy Spirit, is the highest of all the angels. It is his duty to write down the decrees of God;

through him <note>The Archangel Gabriel (Jibril), who in Islamic tradition served as the medium for the revelation of the Quran and is often depicted as a celestial protector with many wings.</note> the Koran <note>Quran: The central religious text of Islam, believed by Muslims to be a revelation from God.</note> was revealed to Mahommad <note>The Prophet Muhammad (c. 570–632 AD).</note>, and it is he who, hovering above the throne of God, shelters it with his wings. Hafiz <note>Hafez (c. 1315–1390), the preeminent Persian lyric poet whose work is a cornerstone of Iranian culture.</note> therefore claims for Shiraz the protection of him who is guardian of the highest place in heaven.

Ibn Batuta <note>Ibn Battuta (1304–1368), the renowned Moroccan explorer who traveled more than 75,000 miles across the Islamic world, documenting his journeys in the 'Rihla'.</note>, the Arab traveller who visited Shiraz about the year 1340, has left a charming description of the native town of Hafiz and of the manners of his contemporaries. “Shiraz,” he says, “is a well-built town of a great size, a wide celebrity, and a high place among cities. It possesses pleasant gardens, far-reaching streams, excellent markets, fine streets, and a numerous population. The town is constructed with taste and admirably arranged; each trade has its own bazaar. The inhabitants are a fine race and well clad. Shiraz lies in a plain; gardens surround it on every side; and five rivers flow through it, amongst them one called <term>Ruknabad</term> <note>original: "Ruknabad"; a famous stream in Shiraz celebrated by Hafez for its clarity and life-giving properties.</note>, a stream of which the water is excellent to drink, very cold in summer and warm in winter. The principal mosque is called the Old Mosque <note>The Atigh Jame' Mosque, one of the oldest religious structures in Iran, dating back to the 9th century.</note>; it is as spacious and as well built as any one could wish to see. The court of it is vast and paved with marble; in hot weather it is washed with fresh water every night. The wealthy citizens come there every evening to repeat the prayers of sunset and of night. The inhabitants of Shiraz are well-to-do, pious, and chaste; the women in particular are distinguished for their modesty. They go completely veiled, give much in alms, and repair three times a week to the great mosque. Often as many as two thousand assembled there, sitting with fans in their hands on account of the great heat. Each day in one of the <term>mausoleums</term> <note>A stately building housing a tomb or group of tombs.</note> the whole Koran is read aloud, and the readers have very beautiful voices. The people bring with them fruits and sweetmeats, and when the congregation has finished eating, the preacher begins his discourse. This takes place between the mid-day and the evening prayers.” Ibn Batuta struck up acquaintance with a <term>Sheikh</term> <note>original: "Sheikh"; a title for a religious scholar, elder, or leader of a community.</note> whom he found seated in a small hermitage at the corner of a mosque. The Sheikh was engaged in reading the Koran. In answer to Ibn Batuta’s questions, he told him that he had founded the mosque himself, and that the hermitage was to be his tomb. Lifting a carpet, he showed him his grave, covered over with planks. “In that box,” he said, pointing to a chest opposite to him, “are my winding-sheet <note>A burial shroud used to wrap the body in Islamic funerary rites.</note>, some spices with which my corpse will be perfumed, and a few pieces of money which I

May God have mercy on Shiraz!” he concludes piously.

### XXXI

*Stanza 3.*—The month of <term>Sha'aban</term> <note>original: "Sha'aban"; the eighth month of the Islamic lunar calendar, preceding the month of fasting.</note> is the eighth month of the Arabic year. It is followed by <term>Ramazan</term> <note>original: "Ramazan"; known more commonly today as Ramadan, the ninth month of the Islamic calendar, observed by Muslims worldwide as a month of fasting, prayer, and reflection.</note>, during which month the Prophet <note>Muhammad (c. 570–632 AD), the founder of Islam.</note> decreed that from two hours before dawn until sunset nothing should pass the lips of his followers. The fast is so strictly observed, especially by the lower orders, that not only do they refrain from eating and drinking, but they will not even smoke until the sunset gun puts an end to the day’s abstinence. The night, however, is passed in feasting and revelry, and the richer classes will sleep late in Ramazan and shorten the long hours that must pass before they may breakfast.

### XXXII

*Stanza 3.*—According to the popular science of the East, the colouring of precious stones, even of those which are buried deep in the earth, is due to the action of rain and wind and of the rays of the sun.

*Stanza 4.*—It is a favourite Persian image to describe the hair of the beloved as entangling and entrapping the unfortunate lover. Her long locks are often compared to deadly snakes, and her curls to hooks which catch and tear her lover’s heart.

### XXXIV

Stanza 1.—The story of the creation of Adam, and of the part played in it by the angels, is told by <term>Mahommad</term> <note>The Prophet Muhammad (c. 570–632 CE), the founder of Islam.</note> in the following terms: "When thy Lord said unto the angels, I am going to place a substitute on earth; they said, Wilt thou place there one who will do evil therein, and shed blood? but we celebrate thy praise and sanctify thee. God answered, Verily I know that which ye know not; and he taught Adam the names of all things, and then proposed them to the angels, and said, Declare unto me the names of these things if ye say truth. They answered, Praise be unto thee, we have no knowledge but what thou teachest us, for thou art knowing and wise. God said, Oh Adam, tell them their names. And when he had told them their names, God said, Did I not tell you that I know the secrets of heaven and earth, and know that which ye discover and that which ye conceal? And when we said unto the angels, Worship Adam; they all worshipped him, except <term>Eblis</term> <note>original: "Eblis"; Iblis, the primary devil or Satan-figure in Islam.</note>, who refused, and was puffed up with pride, and became of the number of unbelievers."—*<term>Koran</term> <note>original: "Koran"; the Quran, specifically Chapter 2 (Surah Al-Baqarah), verses 30–34.</note>, chap. ii.*

Tradition <note>Referring to Hadith or collective Islamic folklore that expands upon the Quranic text.</note> has amplified and adorned this story. It is said that the three <term>archangels</term> <note>High-ranking angels in the celestial hierarchy.</note>, Gabriel, Michael, and Israfil <note>In Arabic: Jibra'il (Angel of Revelation), Mika'il (Angel of Sustenance/Nature), and Israfil (the Angel who blows the trumpet on Judgment Day).</note>, were each in turn ordered to take from the earth seven handfuls of clay of three different colours, red, white, and yellow, that God might create out of it the races of mankind. But each in turn was moved by the earth's prayer that he would not rob her of her substance, and each returned to heaven empty-handed. The fourth time God sent Azrail <note>Azrael, the Angel of Death.</note>, the angel of death, who tore the seven handfuls from the earth, but hearing her lamentations, promised her that when man ceased to live his substance should return to the earth from whence it had been taken. With the clay that Azrail brought him God moulded the figure of man, and when it was finished he left it forty days to dry. The angels came often to gaze upon it, and Eblis, kicking it

<meta>This page continues the commentary on Poem XXXIV, specifically detailing the Sufi interpretation of the creation of Adam and the symbolism of the "clay" and "wine." It then transitions to Poem XXXV, discussing a famous Persian refrain.</meta>

<page-num>147</page-num>

# NOTES

with his foot, found that it rang hollow. When the figure of clay was dry, God breathed the breath of life into its nostrils, and ordered the angels to submit to the man he had created. But <term>Eblis</term> <note>The primary name for the Devil in Islam, often identified as a jinn who refused to bow to Adam.</note> refused, saying that he had been created of pure fire, and would not serve a hollow mould of clay; for which reason God cast him out of Paradise. The rest of the angels acknowledged the superiority of Adam after God had made him tell them the names of all the creatures of the earth, though they had at first protested that it was not seemly that they should bow down to him, for their love for God was greater than his. It is with this legend in his mind that <term>Hafiz</term> <note>The 14th-century Persian lyric poet Hafez of Shiraz.</note> speaks of the angels as standing at the tavern door, where man may enter and receive instruction in God’s wisdom, but where they must knock in vain, and as moulding a wine-cup with the despised clay out of which the human body was moulded. I think he means that man himself is the vessel into which divine love and wisdom are poured; and when he says that the angels first brought him wine, he means that by their example they showed him what it was to be intoxicated by the contemplation of God.

*Stanza 3.*—“Concerning the forbidden fruit,” says <term>Sale</term> <note>George Sale (1697–1736), whose 1734 English translation of the Quran remained the standard for centuries.</note> in a note to the second chapter of the <term>Koran</term>, “the <term>Mohammadans</term>, as well as the Christians, have different opinions. Some say it was an ear of wheat, some will have it to have been a fig-tree, and others a vine.”

There are supposed to be seventy-two sects in Islam. Many Mahommadan writers compare them to the seventy-two branches of the family of Noah after the Babylonian confusion of tongues and the dispersal of the children of Adam. <note>This traditional number refers to a "hadith" (saying of the Prophet) predicting that the Muslim community would split into seventy-three sects, only one of which would find salvation.</note>

---

->xxxv<-

*Stanza 1.*—The second line of this poem is as often quoted as any, perhaps, in the <term>Divan</term> <note>The "Diwan," or collected works, of Hafez.</note>: “May the memory of those days be remembered; may they be remembered!” <note>original: "Yàd bàd àn ruz-i-gàràn, yàd bàd!"</note> A man will set it upon a letter to an absent friend, even when he is not particularly anxious that days gone by should be preserved from oblivion; and how often must the simple little line have been used by those to whom its very simplicity made it more poignant than pages of sentiment!

<summary>This page concludes the notes on Poem XXXIV by explaining the Sufi symbolism of the angels at the tavern door and Adam's clay, then introduces Poem XXXV with a discussion on its famous opening refrain about nostalgia.</summary>

<keywords>Adam and Eve, Iblis, Sufi symbolism, tavern door, forbidden fruit, George Sale, 72 sects of Islam, Diwan of Hafez, nostalgia</keywords>

--- Page 152 ---
<meta>This page continues the commentary on the poems of Hafiz, specifically concluding the notes on Poem XXXV and providing annotations for Poems XXXVI and XXXVII. It follows the previous page's discussion on the Quranic legends and the symbolism of the wine-cup.</meta>

<page-num>148</page-num>

<header>NOTES</header>

*Stanza 3.*—The <term>Zindeh Rud</term> <note>Literally the "River of Life," now commonly known as the Zayandeh River.</note> was a river that flowed past <term>Isfahan</term> <note>A major city in central Iran, famous for its Islamic architecture and history as a capital under the Safavid dynasty.</note>. There are unfortunately no longer rose-gardens upon its banks, for it disappeared completely in the terrible earthquake which occurred in the spring of the year 1853. I suspect from internal evidence that this poem was sent to some friends of <term>Hafiz</term> living at Isfahan, upon whom the passionate appeal need reflect no discredit, since it may quite possibly be merely the Oriental way of writing a letter of thanks. At the same time, in spite of this rational explanation, it must be acknowledged that the meaning of the name Zindeh Rud is River of Life. I tremble to think into what a slough of mysticism the innocent little stream might be induced to guide us!

## XXXVI

*Stanza 2.*—“Love and Faith,” says <term>Rosenzweig</term> <note>Vinzenz Ritter von Rosenzweig-Schwannau (1791–1865), an Austrian diplomat and orientalist who published a major three-volume edition and translation of Hafiz’s Divan.</note> is the name of a well-known Persian story which has been retold by many writers.

## XXXVII

*Stanza 4.*—See Note to Stanza 4 of Poem XXXIII.

The word <term>bezoar</term> <note>A mass found in the digestive system of animals, historically prized as a universal antidote to poison.</note> comes from two Arabic roots which signify the annihilator of poison. Murray gives several examples of its use by seventeenth and eighteenth century writers in the sense of an antidote, chiefly to snake bites. <term>Topsell</term> <note>Edward Topsell (c. 1572–1625), an English cleric and author known for his bestiary, "The History of Four-Footed Beasts and Serpents."</note>, for instance, in his book on Serpents (1607), remarks that “the juice of apples being drunk, and endive, are the proper Bezoar against the venom of a Phalangie”—whatever that may be. The word was also applied to various substances held as antidotes, especially to a concretion found in the stomach of some animals, formed of concentric layers of animal matter deposited round some foreign substance. This concretion was called the bezoar stone. The original sort was the <note>original: "lapis bezoar orientale"</note> **Eastern bezoar stone** obtained from the wild goat of Persia, which was in later times called the bezoar goat; also from various antelopes, etc. The <note>original: "lapis bezoar occidentale"</note> **Western bezoar stone**, obtained from the llamas of Peru, was less valued.

<summary>This page provides historical and linguistic context for Poems XXXV through XXXVII, specifically discussing the geography of Isfahan and the etymology and medicinal history of the "bezoar" stone.</summary>
<keywords>Zindeh Rud, Isfahan, Hafiz, Rosenzweig, bezoar stone, Persian geography, natural history, 1853 earthquake, medicinal folklore</keywords>

chamois yielded German <term>bezoar</term> <note>A stone-like mass found in the stomachs of certain animals, once highly valued as an antidote to all poisons.</note>. “The stone,” says Frampton, in his “Joyful News,” “is called the Bezaar, being approved good against Venome”; and Hawkins, in his “Voyage to the South Seas,” talks about “the becunia and other beasts which breed the beazer stone.”

### XXXIX

*Stanza* 1.—It is related that <term>Ghiyasuddin Purabi</term> <note>Sultan Ghiyasuddin Azam Shah, who ruled Bengal from 1390 to 1410 (though the text dates his succession to 1367).</note>, who succeeded his father to the throne of <term>Bengal</term> <note>A region in the northeast of the Indian subcontinent, known today as Bangladesh and the Indian state of West Bengal.</note> in the year 1367, fell sick. During his illness he was nursed by three faithful handmaidens whose names were Cypress, Tulip, and Rose, and owing to their care he eventually recovered. The rest of the Sultan’s ladies were jealous of the gratitude that the three maidens had earned from Ghiyasuddin, and nicknamed them contemptuously “the three bath women,” because they had washed the King’s body while he was ill. He therefore determined to do them honour by commemorating their devotion in a poem, and to this end he composed the first line of a couplet, and ordered the poets of his court to complete the ode. The line ran thus: “Cup-bearer, a tale runs of a Cypress, a Rose, and a Tulip” <note>original Persian: "Sàki hadis-i-sarvo gul o làleh miravad." The names of the handmaidens—Sarv (Cypress), Gul (Rose), and Laleh (Tulip)—are common metaphors in Persian poetry for stature and beauty.</note>. But the poets were unable to perform the task to the King’s satisfaction, and at length some one suggested that the line should be sent to <term>Hafiz of Shiraz</term> <note>The most celebrated Persian lyric poet (c. 1315–1390), famous for his 'Divan' or collection of poems.</note>, the fame of whose great skill had reached Bengal. This was accordingly done, and Hafiz composed the ode here translated, with which the Sultan (whose taste seems to have turned towards the discursive in poetry) was much delighted. The three cups of wine are an allusion to the three maidens who washed the King’s body; the parrots of India are the court poets of Ghiyasuddin, and the Persian sweetmeat is the ode that Hafiz sent to Bengal.

*Stanza* 4.—<term>Samir</term> <note>Known in Islamic tradition as Al-Samiri, a figure who, according to the Quran, tempted the Israelites into worshipping the Golden Calf while Moses was on Mount Sinai.</note>. Al Samiri belonged, say the <term>Mahommadans</term> <note>An archaic term for Muslims, commonly used by Western scholars in the 19th century.</note>, to a certain tribe among the Jews called the Samaritans, whence his name. In this the Mahommadans strangely betray their ignorance of history, for the Samaritans were not formed into a people, nor did they bear that name, until many ages later. Some say that he was a proselyte, but a hypocritical

<meta>This page concludes the extensive notes for Poem XXXIX regarding the figure of Al-Samiri and his role in the story of the Golden Calf according to Islamic tradition. It then provides brief annotations for Poem XL, concerning Persian folk protections against the evil eye, and Poem XLII, identifying the mythological and celestial figures of Khizr and Zohra.</meta>

<header>NOTES</header>

one, and originally of <term>Kerman</term> <note>A major city in southeastern Iran, historically known for its distinct culture and geography.</note> or some other country. His real name was <term>Musa ibn Dhafar</term> <note>In English: Moses, son of Dhafar.</note>. He was a magician and an alchemist. Pharaoh employed him as a rival to Moses when the latter worked miracles with his hand and his staff, but <term>Al Samiri</term> <note>The Samaritan; a figure in Islamic tradition (based on the Quran) who is said to have led the Israelites into idolatry during Moses' absence.</note> was unable to show wonders as great as those performed by Moses. It was he and not Aaron, according to <term>Mahommadan tradition</term> <note>Historical term for Islamic tradition.</note>, who cast the golden calf. The calf was made of the ornaments of gold and silver and other materials which the Israelites had borrowed from the Egyptians; for Aaron, who commanded in his brother’s absence, having ordered Al Samiri to collect those ornaments from the people, who carried on a wicked commerce with them, and to keep them together till the return of Moses, Al Samiri, understanding the founder’s art, put them all together into a furnace, to melt them down into one mass, which came out in the form of a calf. The Israelites, accustomed to the Egyptian idolatry, paying a religious worship to this image, Al Samiri went further, and took some dust from the footsteps of the horse of the angel Gabriel, who marched at the head of the people, and threw it into the mouth of the calf, which immediately began to low, and became animated; for such was the virtue of that dust. (George Sale, Notes to second and twenty-second chapters of the <term>Koran</term> <note>The Quran.</note>.) Al Simiri is mentioned by name in the twenty-second chapter of the Koran: “Al Samiri led them astray.”

->XL<-

*Stanza 2.*—According to Persian superstition, the smoke of burning <term>rue</term> <note>Specifically *Esfand* (Peganum harmala) in Persian; the seeds are burned to create a fragrant smoke that is believed to ward off jealousy or the "evil eye" (Cheshm-e Bad).</note> has the power to avert the evil eye.

->XLII<-

*Stanza 1.*—<term>Khizr</term> <note>Al-Khidr, the "Green Man," a legendary figure and prophet in Islamic tradition who found the Fountain of Youth (the Water of Life) and gained immortality. He is often seen as a spiritual guide.</note>. See Note to Stanza 3 of Poem XVIII.

*Stanza 3.*—<term>Zohra</term> <note>The Persian name for the planet Venus.</note> is the planet Venus, the musician of the heavens, and the protector of all musicians and singers upon the earth. Zohra played a part in very ancient mythology.

--- Page 155 ---
<meta>This page continues the extensive commentary from the previous page, shifting focus from the figure of Al-Samiri to the legends of the fallen angels Harut and Marut and their association with the planet Venus (Zohra).</meta>

<page-num>151</page-num>

->### NOTES<-

The <term>Mahommadans</term> <note>A historical term for Muslims, common in 19th-century English scholarship.</note> borrowed and adapted the <term>Magian</term> <note>Pertaining to the Magi, the priestly caste of ancient Zoroastrian Persia.</note> legends concerning her, and their account runs as follows: Once upon a time the angels fell to marvelling over the wickedness of man and the ease with which he was led astray, notwithstanding the warnings sent down to him through the prophets. But God, hearing their words, determined to expose them also to temptation, that they might learn how easy it was to fall. Therefore he appointed two of them, whose names were <term>Harut and Marut</term> <note>Two angels mentioned in the Quran (Surah Al-Baqarah 2:102) who were sent to Babylon to test mankind.</note>, to go down to the earth as judges over man, and he taught them a secret word by the power of which every evening, when their work of judgment was done, they could return to heaven. 

For some time the two angels accomplished their duties faithfully. But at length a woman called <term>Zohra</term> <note>The Arabic and Persian name for the planet Venus, often personified in folklore as a beautiful woman or musician.</note>, more beautiful than any other woman upon earth, came before their judgment-seat demanding redress against her husband, and the two angels conceived a violent passion for her. On the following day, when she returned with the same petition, they drew her aside and declared their love to her. She replied that she would satisfy their desires if they would do three things: destroy her husband, worship the gods she worshipped, and drink wine. Murderers and idolaters the angels could not agree to become, but they consented to drink wine, “not knowing,” says the Persian commentator of the *<term>Mesnavi</term> <note>The 'Masnavi-ye Ma’navi', a world-renowned spiritual poem composed in the 13th century.</note> of <term>Jelaleddin Rumi</term> <note>The famous Persian poet and Sufi mystic, Jalal al-Din Muhammad Rumi.</note>*, “that wine was the source of sin and the mother of shame.” 

Then said <term>Zohra</term>: “Every night, by the power of a divine word, ye return to heaven. Teach me also that word.” The angels confided to her the secret of God, and as soon as she had heard the word she pronounced it in her turn and rose up into heaven, where God changed her form and turned her into a star. The angels attempted to follow her to heaven, but they were refused admittance. On the intercession of a very pious man, however, they were allowed to choose whether they would be punished in this world or the next; they chose the former, and now suffer punishment in the land of <term>Babel</term> <note>Babylon, the ancient city in Mesopotamia often associated in Islamic lore with magic and the occult.</note>—whither, if any man have a mind to learn magic, he may go and learn it of them, for they are masters of all magic arts. Tradition says that <term>Mahommad</term> <note>The Prophet Muhammad.</note>, whenever he looked upon the planet Venus, was wont to exclaim: “God curse Zohra! for it was she who led the two angels Harut and Marut into sin.”

The same story, says <term>Rosenzweig</term> <note>Likely Vinzenz Ritter von Rosenzweig-Schwannau (1791–1865), an Austrian diplomat and orientalist noted for his translations of Persian poetry.</note>, is to be found in the <term>Talmud</term> <note>A central text of Rabbinic Judaism, which contains similar narratives regarding fallen angels such as Shemhazai and Azazel.</note>.

<summary>This page recounts the Islamic and Persian legend of the angels Harut and Marut, who were seduced by the woman Zohra (Venus) and subsequently punished in Babylon, serving as an origin story for magic and the celestial position of the planet Venus.</summary>
<keywords>Harut and Marut, Zohra, Venus, Rumi, Masnavi, Babylon, magic, Islamic folklore, fallen angels, Rosenzweig</keywords>

<page-num>152</page-num>

...where the two angels are called <term>Asa and Asail</term> <note>These are alternative names for the fallen angels, often appearing in Jewish mystical traditions and identified with the Quranic Harut and Marut.</note>. The <term>Talmud</term> <note>A central collection of Jewish law and legend.</note> relates that the angels, after their fall into sin, were carried into a great mountain and suspended by chains over an abyss. It was they who taught <term>Solomon</term> <note>Known in Persian as Sulayman; a king and prophet legendary for his wisdom and his power over the supernatural world.</note> his wisdom.

*Stanza* 4.—For the folk beliefs concerning the origin of precious stones, see the Note to Stanza 3 of Poem XXXIII <note>In medieval Persian thought, it was often believed that sunlight or celestial influence gradually transformed ordinary rocks into gems like rubies and emeralds over thousands of years.</note>.

### XLIII

This ode is inscribed upon the tomb of <term>Hafiz</term> <note>The 14th-century master of the Persian ghazal. His tomb in Shiraz remains a site of pilgrimage and poetic devotion.</note>.
